THE CHURCH UPON THE ROCK PAGE 3 

THE BASIS OF RELIGION 

If man uses his reason, and is honest with himself, he must come to the conclusion that God exists and that we have definite duties towards Him. 

Our idea of God is that He is a pure personal Spirit who exists by necessity, who did not make Himself and who was not made by any other, who has always existed, will always exist, and is everywhere. 

We cannot see Him; but cold logic demands that we accept His existence. He has made Himself known to us not only through His works but also through formal revelation. 

Argument From Motion 

Our first proof of His existence, we adduce from motion. Everything around us has motion. Yet we can find nothing in the universe that has within itself the final cause of its movement. Nothing in the visible world is put into motion without help. We are forced, therefore, to conclude either that there is an infinite series of things moving one another in a long chain or that there is a being that is supreme power, uncaused by any other, the origin of all motion. It would be absurd to accept an infinite chain of finite causes, for that would merely push the question further and further back without ever answering it. We must conclude, therefore, that there is an all-powerful Being, the ultimate source of all other beings. This Being is God.

Argument From Cause 

Nothing in the world comes into existence of itself. Inanimate things obviously have no power to reproduce themselves. Living beings come into existence only through other living beings. The most patent fact in all science is that everything must have a cause. 

Cause and effect follow in an 

We are unbroken chain. There can never be any effect without a cause. compelled, therefore, to accept the theory that a chain of causes extends back into infinity, an obvious absurdity, or to accept the existence of an uncaused First Cause. That is, we must accept God. 

Argument From Contingency 

Furthermore, everything in the universe is subject to change. All living things on earth must eventually die. All matter undergoes changes. If any being had within itself the ultimate power of its own existence, it would be necessarily changeless, for change involves dependence or lack of perfection. The fact that all visible things change proves they are contingent; they do not have the ultimate power of being within themselves. But, if they are contingent, they must depend on other beings; and again we come to the necessity of acknowledging either the absurdity of an infinite chain of contingent beings or a self-existent being to whose nature existence belongs. That self-existent being is God.

Hence we realize that, if God did not exist, nothing would exist. 

Whatever exists that is not God exists through God alone. As Father 

Thomas Pegues, O.P., points out in his Catechism of the Summa Theologica: 

Final analysis shows that whatever does not exist of itself can exist only through some other being that exists of itself: this latter, we call God. But

PAGE 4 THE CHURCH UPON THE ROCK 

whatever exists that is not God does not exist of itself. Therefore, final analysis shows that whatever exists that is not God exists through God only. 

We can prove that whatever exists and is not God does not exist of itself by this argument: Whatever has need of some other does not exist of itself. But whatever exists that is not God has need of some other. Therefore, whatever exists that is not God does not exist of itself. Whatever exists of itself neither depends nor could depend on anything or anybody; but whatever has need of something or somebody depends upon this something or somebody. Everything we know of in the universe is somehow dependent on some other thing. Therefore, we know that the universe itself is not God, for its parts are mutually dependent and therefore do not have the final cause of being in themselves. Whatever exists of itself must have everything in itself or through itself; it must be all perfect; hence it can receive nothing from anything or anybody. It is not even dependent on itself. It exists by necessity. The very fact, therefore, that every thing in the universe has need of some other proves that nothing in the universe is the ultimate power or God, and it also proves that God must exist. For, if God did not exist, nothing would exist." 

If we, then, get clear in our mind what being is we cannot deny God. 

Being, looked upon from one aspect, is absolutely necessary existence depending on nothing else for its existence. On the other hand, it is existence depending ultimately on necessary Being for its existence. There are two kinds of being: Necessary Being or God and contingent being or creatures. 

The man who gets this straight in his mind cannot doubt God's existence without doing violence to his reason. The so-called philosophers who doubt 

God are compelled to go to such absurd lengths as to deny that anything, including themselves, exists. Just how we can doubt our own existence if we have no intellects with which to doubt is a neat little problem that these philosophers do not answer.

Argument From the Degrees of Perfection 

Another proof is found in the fact that different degrees of perfection exist in the world. Plants have one sort of perfection; animals have a higher type; man, with his intellect and will, has a still higher type than the lower animals. But all degrees of perfection demand ultimately the absolute perfection in which they participate and which is their reason for existence. 

The only reason why a contingent being can exist is because it in some sense imitates a perfect being. The existence of degrees of perfection argues, therefore, towards the existence of an absolutely perfect being, who is God. 

Argument From Order 

Yet another argument, we gather from the order and the beauty in the world. So terrifying is this argument to modern atheists that their scholars try to assert that there is no order and little beauty in the universe. Yet, whether we turn to the astronomer's telescope or the laboratory worker's microscope, or whether we merely look around, we see marvelous precision and seemingly unlimited power of arrangement. All that science is, is the proper labeling of the laws we see operating in nature. Man makes nothing; all he does is to take advantage of laws already operating. Sometimes he cannot understand those laws. But, as centuries pass, he learns more and more about them. To deny the existence of these laws or the order and the beauty that result from them is to be treasonous to human

THE CHURCH UPON THE ROCK PAGE 5 

reason.

If a watch cannot make itself; if an epic poem cannot write itself; if, after centuries of study of his mind and body, man has hardly scratched the surface in understanding even himself, who but a fool could deny that a Supreme Intellect made the universe and guides it? 

Argument From the Nature of Our Thinking 

Try as you will, neither your imagination nor your intellect can conceive of absolute void. Hard as it is for us to think of a Being absolutely without limit, it is harder still to think of space or of a period in which there is absolutely nothing. We do violence to our imagination and our intellect by feeding them complete void. It would seem, therefore, that the very nature of our mental make-up demands a perfect Being, present everywhere and always existing, as our final object. 

Religion Is Necessary 

If man is contingent; if he does not have the ultimate power over himself within himself; it stands to reason that he owes duties towards the power on whom he does ultimately depend. Since man knows the difference between right and wrong, and realizes that right should be rewarded and wrong punished, reason demands that he find out and observe the laws that differentiate the good from the wicked. Reason, therefore, demands religion. 

God Is Personal 

We know that God is personal because obvious planning and personality exist in the world. The distinguishing mark of personality is intellectuality. To deny personality to God is to deny that the universe has any intelligent plan behind it—an obvious absurdity. If there is no intelligence behind the universe, we must admit that the universe has created something greater than itself—a thinking man. How could the tremendous powers of the human soul arise from a soulless universe? Nothing ever creates something greater than itself. It is easier to believe in a guiding intelligence behind all than to accept the idea that either the universe or man came about by chance. The existence of human personality demands the existence of a Creator with personality. The existence of order in the universe also demands an intelligence behind it.

PAGE 6 THE CHURCH UPON THE ROCK 

II 

MAN HAS A SPIRITUAL AND IMMORTAL SOUL 

Reason proves to man that he is different from other creatures in this world, even from the higher-type dumb animals. Man is an animal, but a rational animal. His power of reasoning makes him completely different from other animals. 

Man is composed of a material body and a spiritual soul, so united as to form one being. The soul is present in every part of the body and completely present in every part. Being spiritual, it is not divisible into parts, but simple. It is the life principle in man. When death comes to him, it occurs through the separation of the soul from the body. The soul lives right on, maintaining personal consciousness. The body then disintegrates.

It is possible to prove by reason alone, apart from formal revelation, that the soul is spiritual and that it is immortal. The proofs are not easy to follow. We are here dealing with the most abstract questions of human nature. But the conclusions are most convincing, despite the fact that some philosophical schools refuse to accept them. 

We prove the soul a spiritual substance by the fact that it is capable of acts that are spiritual. We know that effects cannot rise above their cause and we see genuinely spiritual effects in our acts; hence the source of these acts must be spiritual. 

One of the chief tests of spirituality in the soul is to be found in man's universal power of classifying, something shared with no other animal. He has the faculty of setting up what the logicians call universals, ideas under which all specimens of a species may be recognized. I have, for instance, the universal idea of a triangle. Any triangle, regardless of its shape or size, fits under that universal; hence I always know a triangle when I see one. I have the universal idea of a horse and any sort of horse fits under it.

The universal idea must be spiritual, for it fits all sorts of things without difficulty. It is simple. If a horse is large, the universal idea fits it just as well as it does a small horse. Only a spiritual and simple idea could fit both types so easily. No material faculty could classify in this way. 

But our

Being both material and spiritual, man knows how far both the material and the spiritual in him can go. The material part of us, even in our mental operations, is wonderful, but it falls far short of the spiritual. We get all our knowledge through the senses, which are material. The sense images thus gathered furnish food for our imagination, which is a sense operation and not a spiritual one, and for our sense memories. spiritual intellects are able to take our sense images and from them get purely spiritual images, with which they do wonderful feats. We have powers of apprehension, judgment, memory, and will that are wholly spiritual. Thus, though all our information comes through our senses, our spiritual intellects pass judgment and draw out new knowledge from the data furnished by our material nature. So close is the union of the spiritual and the material in our make-up that the two always work together. In this *Some of our memories are wholly spiritual; some are sensory and hence material. 

Our imagination is sensory.

THE CHURCH UPON THE ROCK PAGE 7 

life, spiritual operations in us are always accompanied by corporal activity. 

Fools who do not remember this are easily deluded into believing that all our mental actions are purely corporal. Their fundamental problem is that they have not grasped the idea of union of soul and body. 

The soul is the ultimate principle in me by which I think, feel, and will, and by which my body is animated. It cannot be corporal because the spirituality and the simplicity of many of our mental acts or states prove that the agent to which they belong is simple and spiritual in nature. Our universal ideas, intellectual judgments and reasonings, and especially the reflective nature of our self-consciousness are manifestly simple, indivisible, and spiritual in character. Since no effect can rise above its cause, the principle from which these activities comes, the soul, must be spiritual. 

The reflective nature of our self-consciousness is a particularly strong proof of the soul's spirituality. The human intellect has the peculiar power of turning completely back on itself, studying what is in itself. No material organ has such power. Our imaginations can be studied by our intellect but are powerless to turn back on themselves. Our sense perceptions cannot have this reflex action. Only in our spiritual souls is there any such capacity. 

Only in our intellects, also, can we get an idea of a being or a force that is wholly spiritual.

Again, we know that our volitions are free. Yet experience proves that free volition cannot be exerted by a material agent or be intrinsically dependent on matter. If volition were intrinsically dependent on matter, all our acts of choice would be inexorably bound up with and predetermined by changes in our organism. One of the natural reasons why freedom is sometimes difficult to exert is because the material side of us is so easily dominated by other matter. Here again fools are often put astray and led to imagine that real freedom does not exist. But if a philosopher who does not believe in freedom of the will has his house robbed he is just as anxious to have the burglar punished as any other man would be. 

Because the soul is simple and not composite, it is not liable to perish by corruption or internal dissolution. As the soul has no parts, it cannot fall apart. It cannot dissolve into its elements like a body, for, being simple, it is not made up of elements or parts. The characteristic evil of the soul, sin or wickedness, obviously does not kill it or lessen its powers in the same way as disease and injury lay waste the body. Destruction of the material body does not necessarily mean the death of the soul because the soul has powers that manifestly do not depend on the body. Even though we do get all our knowledge through the senses, our souls can do things with that knowledge that matter could not accomplish. It would seem that, if the soul were to perish at all, it must do so simply by annihilation. 

The philosopher Kant advanced the objection that, although we may deny the soul extensive quantity, i. e. division into parts, yet we cannot refuse it intensive quantity, i. e. degrees of reality, and consequently its existence may be terminated by gradual diminution of its powers, although not by decomposition into parts. (He did not deny the immortality of the soul.) His argument seems useless to us. There is no evidence that the soul ever deteriorates. A diminution of its powers never happens. 

When a man becomes mentally weak, we can always find the cause for this in his body, never in his soul. It is only the body, not the soul, that weakens. 

The idea of simplicity forbids us to accept any diminution in the soul or

PAGE 8 THE CHURCH UPON THE ROCK 

If its powers. This could come only by a dissolution of parts, and the soul has no parts. If the agent is indissoluble, as is implied by the very fact of simplicity, there can be no lessening of its powers. All evidence in favor of mental diminution clearly points to the material side of us. the material brain weakens, the intellect is no longer fed with proper images; but, just as soon as the material brain is cured, the intellect is there, working as well as ever. Insanity is a disease of only the material part of man's make-up; the spiritual has nothing to do with it. 

From the spiritual nature of the soul, therefore, we gather a powerful argument in favor of immortality. God could, it is true, do away with our spiritual souls; but formal revelation assures us that we will all live forever. 

In itself, the soul is so constructed that it must live on, except it meet with annihilation by God.

Using the same reasoning, we realize that the souls of the lower animals, as well as plant souls, do not have immortality. These souls or life principles are bound up with matter. We can find no activity, even in the highest dumb animals, that is spiritual. Hence when death comes both the soul and the body perish. God could keep them alive, but there is no proof that He does. Sentimentalists who want to meet their dumb animal friends in the next life can go on hoping to do so if they wish. There is no heresy in their belief. But neither is there any proof for it adducible from reason or revelation.

Materialists try to make out that what we call the soul dies with the body, being merely a part of it. Against their contention, we hold that man is himself aware of the difference between his soul and his body; that he is conscious of the preservation of his personal identity despite all the changes of his body; that in exercising his will he knows that he controls his body and that it does not control him; and that the acts of his spiritual soul are so evident that consciousness rebels at the absolute identification of his soul and his body, despite their close union. 

If it be claimed that man is merely a machine or a combination of chemicals, a vast gulf is left unexplained between man and mechanism. 

What about man's nervous system, his brains, his consciousness? Machines and chemicals have none of these. Man's tremendous genius, his power to explore the fields of science, to search out philosophical and religious truths, his authorship of sublime music and literature, his conceptions of material and spiritual beauty-certainly these feats are not the work of machinery or of chemicals. of A surgeon who said some years ago that he had operated on hundreds persons and never seen a soul can be answered by any child who knows the catechism. The soul, being a spirit, cannot be seen. The doctor was as foolish as the astronomer who declared he had never caught a glimpse of God in the heavens. Neither have we, God being a spirit, but we have seen His works. The vastness of the universe is one of our great proofs that it must have intelligence behind it. 

It is reasonable to expect to find the proofs for the existence of the human soul and of God in the philosophical sphere, not in that of physical science. The soul and God are spiritual. We must turn to the metaphysical departments of science, not to the physical, to prove the existence of spirits. 

We can hardly put spirits into test tubes. It is by introspection that we learn

PAGE 

something of their nature. Yet there are fools who would apply the laws of mathematics and surgery to their search for all that is spiritual. It would be as logical to turn to botany in the hope of finding proof or disproof of 

Einstein's theory of relativity. 

William James, the psychologist, in his lecture on Human Immortality, quoted with approval the following words of Immanuel Kant: "The death of the body may indeed be the end of the sensational use of our mind, but only the beginning of the intellectual use. The body would thus be not the cause of our thinking, but merely a condition restrictive thereof, and, although essential to our sensuous and animal consciousness, it may be regarded as an impeder of our pure spiritual life” (Kritik der reinen Vernunft, ii ed., p. 809).

Materialists have never been able to prove that the soul is so absolutely dependent on the body that the dissolution of the one must necessarily involve the cessation of the other. Mere opinions do not count in so important a problem.

Pessimists advance the argument that life is so worthless that its continuance is not desirable. The restfulness of oblivion appeals to them. 

But it cannot be denied that life is sweet to the vast majority and is hateful to any sane man only in a conditional sense. 

Some pantheists do not want individual immortality, but absorption into the universal life force. Pantheism holds that everything is divine, that the world is an emanation or part of the essence of God; that the world is the real outpouring of the Divine substance; or else that God is absorbed by the world of which He is the force and the life; or that the world is absorbed by God. The absurdity of all these opinions is evident to anybody who has grasped the difference between necessary and contingent being. 

God cannot be perfect and imperfect at the same time, but He would be if everything were God. If God had any imperfection, He would not be God; necessary being, by its very nature, demands absolute perfection, just as imperfection proves contingency. 

There is no essential difference between annihilation and absorption into a universal life force with the loss of individuality. Every argument for immortality is an argument against Pantheism. 

What is called the ethical argument for immortality has strong appeal to many. Man's life here is incomplete, says the Encyclopedia Brittanica, xiii edition, p. 338, vol. 13-14, and the more lofty his aims, the more worthy his labors, the more incomplete will it appear to be. The man who lives for fame, wealth, power, may be satisfied in this life; but he who lives for the ideals of truth, beauty, goodness, lives not for time but for eternity, for his ideals cannot be realized, and so his life fulfilled, on this side of the grave. Unless these ideals are mocking visions, man has a right to expect the continuation of his life.

The juridical argument is just as strong. The present life does not show that harmony of condition and character that our sense of justice leads us to expect. We see the wicked prosper and the good suffer. We look forward logically, therefore, to a future life when the injustices of this life will be corrected.

Tennyson, in his poem In Memoriam, argued that the heart protests against the severance of death, and claims the continuance of love's com-

PAGE 

munion after death; since man feels that love is the most godlike feature of his nature, love's claim has supreme authority. 

Ralph Waldo Emerson asserted that the impulse to seek proof of immortality is itself the strongest proof of all. Addison's famous poem on Plato's argument for life after death contained the undying line: "It must be so, Cato, else why this ceaseless longing for immortality?" Schopenhauer found his great argument in the universal will to live. 

Reason, therefore, proves that the soul is immortal; but the strongest proof we have is that from religion. Jesus Christ taught the immortality of the soul, and He proved His teaching by miracles, by the fulfillment of prophecy, and by such holiness of life that He showed Himself to be the messenger of God."

To sum up some of the chief arguments from reason: 

We maintain the demonstrability of human immortality even without recourse to formal revelation. The objects of the activity of the human intellect are intrinsically independent of matter and of material conditions. 

Even when the intellect deals with material things, it does so in an immaterial or spiritual way. spiritual way. It acts in ways that are independent of time and space and it can turn back and, as it were, grasp itself, examining what is in itself and understanding itself, a form of activity wholely beyond merely material beings. From all this, we demand a subject or agent, i.e. the soul, that is intrinsically independent of matter and of material conditions. That agent must, therefore, be immaterial or spiritual. But, if a being is spiritual, it is not subject to the laws of corruption that we see operating around us on material things, including our own bodies. Since the faculties of the soul, its intellect and will, remain rooted in it, the immortal existence of the soul will be a vital or conscious existence. Nothing we know of can impair the existence of the soul. God alone could put it out of existence, but we know from revelation that He will not. 

Man's innate desire for perfect happiness would be absurd if there were no after-life to set right the inadequacies and injustices of this life. 

There could not be such firmly implanted desire without its being intended for fulfillment. Otherwise the Maker of all would be unwise. Furthermore, the necessity of reward for the good and punishment for the wicked demands a future life; for we know that injustices are not all corrected in this one.

PAGE III WHY RELIGION? WHY CATHOLICITY? 

Having proved the existence of God and the spirituality and the immortality of the human soul, we need also to show that all men are obliged to practice religion, that the religion which God has revealed through His Divine Son is worthy of belief, that Christ established a Church which all are obliged to join, and that the only true Church of Jesus Christ is the Catholic Church. For the most part, these theses will be developed as we proceed with this book.

But it is well to set down a few fundamental points first. 

We prove that men are obliged to practice religion by the fact that they are utterly dependent on God. They, therefore, have the clear duty of honoring Him and of praying to Him. As we have already established that He is necessary being, whereas we are only contingent beings, relying on Him for everything, it stands to reason that we must acknowledge Him in worship and seek His help. Through positive revelation we know the need of praying to Him; but reason itself insists on prayer's necessity. Those who lack strength enough themselves to meet the trials of life and to work out their salvation obviously should use every means to get strength. Both reason and revelation show us that prayer and worship give this strength. 

Even the wildest tribes worship. 

But how can we prove that the religion which God has revealed through His Divine Son is worthy of our belief? We can do it because Jesus 

Christ announced Himself as the ambassador and the true Son of God, His coming was foretold by the prophets, He made startling prophecies which were themselves fulfilled, He led a most holy life, and He preached doctrines that He said all must believe; further, He worked wonderful miracles that showed the God of truth approved His teachings. 

We can prove that He established a Church that all are obliged to join because: 

I. He gathered about Him a group of disciples and actually formed a Church that was adorned with the power of working miracles during its earlier days (a power not common but found in all ages). This Church, although it taught doctrines that made men curb their animal appetites, and was horribly persecuted, nevertheless spread in a way that was clearly miraculous.

II. He promised that His Church would last until the end of time and would be Divinely protected against error. Only the Catholic Church definitely traces herself back to Him and is able to prove that there has been no contradiction in her doctrines at any time. 

III. He declared that all men must believe and be baptized-that is, join His Church-in order to be saved.

We can prove that the Catholic Church is the only true Church of 

Christ because it alone:

I. Possesses the marks of the Church that He and the Apostles declared necessary—unity, holiness, catholicity, and apostolicity. 

II. The history of the Catholic Church gives evidence of miraculous strength, permanence, and unchangeableness, thus proving that it is under the special protection of God.

We assert that it is folly to hold that "one religion is as good as another." If God established a Church Himself, certainly the sects that

PAGE  

men founded cannot be considered its equal. The many religious denominations teach all sorts of conflicting doctrine. But God is truth. He cannot deceive or be deceived. There must be one Church that teaches unspotted truth and that does not change its doctrines from age to age. There must be objective truth in religion, just as there is in science. Only the Catholic 

Church is able to establish her claims to being this Church. 

The theory that different systems of worship and belief, such as Buddhism, Shintoism, Mohammedanism, etc., all have great good in them and are manifestations of the same spirit of struggle towards the one Light is absurd. If men worship idols they are not worshiping God. If they follow a type of worship that was invented by man, they can hardly expect to please the God who revealed how He wished to be worshiped. 

We can, therefore, make no admission that mistakes or lies or halftruths are as good as truth. We are frankly intolerant of falsehood and error. Nevertheless, we are not intolerant of people who, in good faith, worship as best they know how, although they do it wrongly. We hold with the encyclical letter sent to the Bishops of Italy August 10, 1863, by 

Pope Pius IX. He said: "It is known to us and to you that those who are in invincible ignorance (i.e., ignorance which they have no means of dispelling) of our most holy religion, who observe the precepts of the natural law, which God has written in the hearts of all men, and who in their willingness to obey God live an honest and upright life, may, by the aid of the Divine light and grace, attain to eternal life; for God, who beholds, searches, and knows the minds, the hearts, the thoughts, and the habits of all men, in His sovereign goodness and mercy, does not permit any one to suffer eternal punishment who is guiltless of a wilful transgression of His law." 

If a person does not know the Catholic Church to be the true one and lives outside her, according to the best of his ability and in good conscience, dying with perfect contrition for his mortal sins, or with pure love of God because He is so infinitely good and deserving of all love, his soul is saved. 

But the salvation is not effected by the fact that he is a Methodist, a Presbyterian, a Mormon, a Buddhist, or whatever he is, but as a Catholic. He has done what the Church demands must be done in his case for salvation and hence goes to heaven as a Catholic. There will be no "denominations" in heaven. He belongs to the soul of the Church, which consists in sanctifying grace, together with the infused virtues and gifts of the Holy Ghost. We also distinguish the body of the Church, consisting of those who belong to that actual society headed by the Bishop of Rome. 

The writer agrees with those theologians who hold that the majority of men will be saved, for otherwise it would seem that the Redemption failed. But there are some noted authorities who hold otherwise. Texts such as "Many are called, but few are chosen," can be interpreted as referring to particular groups, not to all mankind.

What can we say on behalf of sects like some of the Protestants and Mormons, which hold to a genuine revelation by Christ but say it was corrupted in the course of time and, after a lapse of centuries, re-established by their founders? Our reply to them is that, if this be true, the Holy 

Ghost was not with the Church for centuries, and Christ would therefore be a deceiver, inasmuch as He promised that the Paraclete would remain with her forever and keep her in all truth.

PAGE IV NATURE OF GOD, ANGELS, AND HUMAN BEINGS 

God is the Creator and Sovereign Lord of heaven and earth and of all things.

He is one simple and infinitely perfect spiritual substance, all powerful, eternal, immense, incomprehensible, unchangeable, infinite in intellect and will and in every perfection. Man is made up of parts. There are no parts in God. All the Divine attributes are really identical with one another and with the Divine essence.

When we say that God is eternal, we do not mean that He has existed for an indefinite period of time. We mean that He exists with a total exclusion of the limits that time implies. Everything is always in the present with God. As Christ said: "Before Abraham was, I am" (John viii, 58).

God exists necessarily. He did not make Himself nor did anyone make Him. He is present everywhere and transcends the limits of actual or possible space. "God's center is everywhere, His circumference nowhere."

We call God the Creator of heaven and earth and of all things because He made all things out of nothing, simply by His will. He made them all for His own honor and glory and to have His creatures share His happiness. 

We cannot see God because He is a pure spirit. On this earth, we can get only a negative idea of a spirit. It is a personal force in which there is absolutely nothing of the material. Philosophy proves that the spiritual is a more perfect state of being than the material. God, being a spirit, has no body. He is the Creator of everything that exists outside Himself, and the only reason why anything contingent does exist is because it imitates in some manner His perfection.

There are other spirits besides God, namely the angels and our souls. 

God made us and the angels to show forth His goodness and to share with us His everlasting happiness in heaven. If we do not serve Him on earth, we will be unhappy throughout eternity.

Because He made everything, God knows everything; nothing at all can possibly happen without His knowledge. "All things are naked and open to His eyes" (Hebrews iv, 13). God can do everything and nothing is difficult to Him. We can do nothing without His co-operation. We need His co-operation even when we sin, but this does not make Him a participator in our sin. If there is a sum of money lying on the table and 

I take it and the money is mine, what I do is all right; but, if the money belongs to somebody else and I take it, I commit a sin; yet the act is exactly the same in both cases. In one instance, however, it is not in the right order and in the other it is in the right order. Thus, we see, sin is something negative; and it belongs to us, not to God. 

God is eternal, unchangeable, exists everywhere, is all-knowing, allwise, all-powerful, all-holy and just, good, merciful, long-suffering, and faithful. The whole purpose why we are on earth is to serve Him. 

There are three persons in God, called the Father, the Son, and the Holy Ghost. The Father is God, the Son is God, and the Holy Ghost is 

God, but they are not three Gods, only one God. They have existed from

PAGE  

all eternity and are equal in every way. What one can do the others can do. 

How do we know about the Trinity or three Persons in one God? We know it only through the revelation of God. We could not possibly attain to this truth unless God had told us about it. 

Angels are pure spirits, without any bodies. They are of a higher nature than men, who are composed of both bodies and souls. 

Man is composed of a material body and a spiritual soul, and is made to God's image and likeness (Gen. i, 26). This likeness is chiefly in the soul, because the soul, like God, is a spirit that will never die, and has understanding and free will. The soul gives life to our bodies. When death occurs, the soul leaves the body, which, having lost its life principle, corrupts. The chief powers of the soul are memory, will, and understanding. 

Forms of life lower than man have souls too, but their souls are dependent on matter for life and, so far as we can determine, die with the body. 

Man's soul is not dependent on matter for life. Man's soul cannot die. 

It may die the spiritual death of mortal sin, but this does not mean that it will cease to exist, but that, when in mortal sin, it is impossible for it to enter eternal life.

God made us to know, to love, and to serve Him in this world and to be happy with Him forever in the next. We can never be wholly happy in this world, but the greatest happiness we can obtain here comes from knowing, loving, and serving God.

Because life on earth is so short, and life after death never ends, we must take better care of our souls than of our bodies. "What does it profit a man," asked Christ, "if he gain the whole world, and suffer the loss of his own soul?" (Matt. xvi, 26.)

God made the world so that His intellectual creatures might see their Creator's glory in it, and for man's use and benefit. Man was given a free will, just like the angels, in order that the chief end of creation, God's glory, could be achieved. If some creatures did not have understanding and free will, the world could not give the proper glory to God and the whole end of creation would be frustrated. But angels and men, having intellect, can perceive the works of God, and, having wills, can honor and love Him for them and thus give Him glory. To have this glory given is the purpose of creation. God could not have caused creation for any reason except 

Himself, for He would then be dependent in a manner on creatures, and His infinite perfection demands that He be altogether independent of anything but Himself.

God is infinite Himself, and, as everything that exists is in some manner an imitation of Him, we find endless variety in creation. No two leaves, no two blades of grass, no two seas, no two planets are alike. 

Man's free will is at the same time his greatest danger and his greatest natural glory. Together with his understanding, it makes him godlike. 

Although he needs God's help to be saved, nevertheless his free will, in a sense, makes his reward an earned one. He is not an automaton, but a creature capable of making choice, and his dignity is thereby given ineffable splendor. God could have made creatures with understanding but without free will; but they would have been less than human.

PAGE 

HOW WE LEARN ABOUT GOD 

God has taught us about Himself in two ways-through nature and through formal revelation. St. Paul said of the Gentiles that they were inexcusable if they did not believe in God. "For the invisible things of 

Him, from the creation of the world, are clearly seen, being understood by the things that are made; His eternal power also, and Divinity" (Romans i, 20). The Apostle here taught that God is so revealed in nature that it is impossible to look on these works and not realize that God exists. We know for a fact from ancient history that, even in nations and ages where many false gods were adored, there was always a recognition of an all-ruling supreme Deity.

Another way of knowing God through nature is by the voice of conscience, which chides us when we do wrong and approves us when we do right.

Formal revelation has come to us through the Scriptures and Tradition. 

The Scriptures consist of two parts, the Old and the New Testament. The 

Old Testament contains the Divine revelations made to man before the coming of Christ. In it are twenty-one historical books telling of the creation of the world, the lives of the patriarchs and the history of the Jews; seven moral books, consisting of holy maxims and rules of life, and seventeen prophetical books, consisting chiefly of prophecies. The New Testament contains the revelations put into written form through the inspiration of the Holy Ghost after the coming of Christ. This Testament consists of four 

Gospels giving the history of Christ, the Acts of the Apostles, twenty-one Epistles by St. Paul and other disciples, and the Apocalypse or Revelation of St. John. The Catholic Church holds that the writers of these books were moved by the Holy Ghost to compose the works and were kept from error while they were writing the various books, preserving, however, the individual style of the authors, because inspiration is not the same as dictation.

In addition to the Scriptures, we hold that revelation consists also of Tradition. The Scriptures tell us of truths which they themselves do not record. St. Paul in II Thessalonians ii:14 says: "Therefore, brethren, stand fast; and hold the traditions which you have learned, whether by word or by our Epistle." The Apostle here refers to doctrines that came to the people by word of mouth in addition to those which came by writing, and he puts both on exactly the same basis. Again, St. Paul said to Timothy: "And the things which thou hast heard of me by many witnesses, the same commend to faithful men, who shall be fit to teach others also (II Tim. ii, 2). In St. John's Gospel, xx, 30, we find these words: "Many other signs also did Jesus in the sight of His disciples, which are not written in this book." We see, therefore, that the Apostles did not pretend to be giving the whole of Christianity in the Scriptures. The Bible in fact is not a systematic explanation of Christianity. The Gospels and Epistles were all written to meet certain needs in the Church of their own day, and it remained for later writers to make a systematic explanation of religion, basing it on what they had learned from the Scriptures and Tradition. 

We hold that the interpreter and custodian of both the Scriptures and

PAGE  

Tradition is the Catholic Church. In fact, she was in existence over three hundred years before it was exactly decided just what was and what was not to be included in the New Testament. The Church has never at any time withheld the Bible from the generality of the public. She insists that her children read only versions of which she has approved, but she does this to keep false versions that are wrongfully translated or interpolated out of their hands. Her whole aim is to preserve the purity of the Scriptures.

PAGE VI 

WHAT IS MEANT BY THE TRINITY AND BY THE INCARNATION OF GOD 

"There is no God but one," says St. Paul in his First Letter to the 

Corinthians, viii, 4. There cannot be more than one God, because, being supreme and infinite, He cannot have an equal. A God could not be created, or come into existence, because these things in themselves are imperfections, and God must be perfect. In order to be God, it is essential that He should have existed necessarily through all eternity. 

In God there are three Persons-the Father, the Son, and the Holy Ghost. They are of the same substance, and hence are really the same God; but they are genuinely distinct. The Father is not the same Person as the 

Son or the Holy Ghost, the Son is not the same Person as the Father or the Holy Ghost, and the Holy Ghost is not the same Person as the Father or the Son; yet they are all the same God. "There are three who give testimony in heaven; the Father, the Word, and the Holy Ghost; and these three are one,” says the First Epistle of St. John v, 7.

The Father is the First Person of the Blessed Trinity and is the Prin ciple from whom the Son and Holy Ghost proceed. He is called the Father, because from all eternity He generates the Son. 

The Son, or the Word, never had a beginning any more than the Father, and is fully equal to the Father. "In the beginning was the Word . . and the Word was God," says John i, 1. The Word, in time, assumed a created body and soul. This fact is known as the Incarnation. The union of the Godhead with a human nature is known as the hypostatic union. 

It is a mystery, like the Trinity, too great for the human mind to grasp. 

But, like all the other mysteries of the Catholic Church, there is nothing in it that is repugnant to human reasoning, even though we can not fully grasp it.

As in the Godhead there are three Persons in one God, in the Second Person of the Blessed Trinity there are two natures in one Person, the human and the Divine. Christ, as God, existed from all eternity. "Before Abraham was, I am," He declared of Himself (John viii, 58). But as man He was born of the Blessed Virgin Mary. He had a true human body and a true human soul, just like ours, except for sin and its consequences; His body and soul being joined to the Divinity by union in the same Person. To both 

His body and soul is given the fullest adoration because of this union with the Godhead (adoration is always directed to the Person). When 

Christ died, the human soul left the human body, just as ours will.

But the Divinity, in His case, remained with both, and, when He arose from the dead after being crucified, it was by His own Divine power that this was accomplished.

When He spoke of Himself on earth, He sometimes referred to His human nature and sometimes to His Divine. His references to His Divine nature were so clear that they are unmistakable. And, since His assertions were proved by the most stupendous miracles, God would be a deceiver if He permitted the Savior to work such wonders in confirmation of false claims. Since God is necessarily perfect and cannot lie, the only conclusion that can be reached is that Christ is what He claimed to be-God. "I and the Father are one," He said (John x, 30).

PAGE 

The Son is called the Second Person of the Blessed Trinity, because He proceeds from the First Person.

The Holy Ghost is called the Third Person of the Blessed Trinity, because He proceeds and is sent from the Father and the Son. Jesus Christ said: "But when the Paraclete cometh, whom I will send you from the

Father, the Spirit of Truth, who proceedeth from the Father, He shall give testimony of Me" (John xv, 26).

St. Peter, in Acts v, 4, shows that the Holy Ghost is God by saying to 

Ananias, who, as verse 3 declares, had lied to the Holy Ghost: "Thou hast not lied to men, but to God."

To the Father are usually attributed the works of omnipotence; to the Son, the works of wisdom; to the Holy Ghost, the works of love. While only the Second Person of the Blessed Trinity became man, the various works of God, such as creation, belong to all the three Persons, yet we speak of one or another of the Persons as doing them. This is what is known as appropriating these works to this Person. The works are ap. propriated to the different Persons, such as works of omnipotence, wisdom, or love, according to the relation that the Divine Persons bear to one another.

PAGE 

VII ANGELS HAVE GREAT POWER; MANY MILLIONS IN NUMBER 

Angels are intellectual creatures made by God and are purely spiritual, having no bodies. They have appeared at times to men in material form, but then have assumed bodies which, while seeming to be their own, were really not a part of them, as our bodies are of us. It was necessary for the angels to assume these bodies, otherwise they could not be seen by men, who, with their material eyes, are not able to discern spirits. 

Some of the times when angels have thus appeared to men are: When 

Raphael was visible to the younger Tobias as a beautiful young man (Tob. v, 5); when Gabriel appeared to Zachary and later to the Blessed Virgin (Luke i); when Christ was born; when an angel spoke to the three women who went to Christ's tomb on Resurrection Day to anoint His body; when 

Christ had just risen into heaven (Acts i); and on a number of other occasions.

The angels were created to adore and to enjoy God in heaven. They also assist Him before His throne and administer to Him, act as messengers from Him to men, and as our guardians. 

There are nine choirs of them-Seraphim, Cherubim, Thrones, Dominations, Principalities, Powers, Virtues, Archangels, and Angels. The 

Cherubim are spoken of in Genesis iii, 24, where it is asserted that they guard the Garden of Eden from men. The Seraphim are spoken of in 

Isaias vi, 2. St. Paul in his Letter to the Ephesians speaks of the Principalities, Powers, Virtues, and Dominations, and, in his Letter to the Colossians, of the Thrones, Dominations, Principalities, and Powers. Angels are frequently referred to in the Scriptures. Archangels are told of in St. Jude and 

I Thess. iv, 15.

Angels are not all of the same rank. Heaven is like a flower garden in its various ranks. If our gardens had nothing in them but stately lilies, one of their chief features of beauty, variety, would be lost. It takes the sweet violet and other flowers as well as the stately lily to make a complete garden. So, likewise, in heaven. Men, too, who reach heaven will arrive there in different stages.

The number of angels is frequently spoken of in the Bible as prodigious (Dan. vii, 10; Apoc. vi, 11; Ps. lxvii, 18; Matt. xxvi, 53). That angels were created was defined by the Fourth Lateran council. The decree Formiter against the Albigenses declared both that they were created and that men were created after them. The Epistle to the Hebrews proves that angels are spiritual, by saying: "Are they not all ministering spirits, sent to minister to them who shall receive the inheritance of salvation?” (i, 14). The tenth chapter of Daniel shows us that angels are named as princes over various districts. We know that the angels are of a higher nature than ourselves, both because reason proves to us that the spirit is more perfect than matter, and because the Bible says, referring to man: "Thou hast made him a little less than the angels" (Ps. viii, 6).

Certain angels are appointed by God to guard over us. "He hath given 

His angels charge over thee, to keep thee in all thy ways" (Ps. xc, 11). It is considered a matter of faith that the angels are our guardians, but that each member of the human race has his individual Guardian Angel is not

PAGE 

of compulsory faith, although the view is so strongly supported by the Doctors of the Church that it would be rash to deny it. 

Our Guardian Angels suggest good thoughts and desires, pray for us, offer our prayers and good works to God, protect us during life, and conduct us to heaven. We should have a tender regard for them, remembering that 

God has chosen them for us. We have not made the personal selection, as we do of our favorite saints. The prayers of our Guardian Angels must be powerful indeed. October 2 is the feast of the Guardian Angels.

We know from the way Raphael acted, when helping the three young men in the fiery furnace and when with Tobias, that the angels have great powers over the material world. They can snatch us out of danger. Christian legend records numberless instances when they have saved persons from drowning or other serious perils, when prayed to. They know when spiritual enemies are near and have the power to drive them off. See Tobias xii. This same chapter shows that, if we pray to the angels, they will pray to God for us. Raphael said to the elder Tobias: "When thou didst pray with tears, and didst bury the dead; and didst leave thy dinner, and hide the dead by day in thy house and bury them by night, I offered thy prayer to the Lord."

Raphael, according to his own statement, is "one of the seven who stand before the Lord." When Tobias and his son learned who he was, they fell upon their faces. They were not chided for this obeisance, but were comforted in their fear. God intends us to honor his angels.

That the angels, when they appear to men, assume bodies that are not integral parts of them, is shown by Raphael's words to the two Tobiases: “I seemed indeed to eat and to drink with you; but I use an invisible drink, which cannot be seen by men."

Angels are persons, i.e. intellectual beings with their own individualities, having that spontaneous energy of intellectual activity that evolves itself in acts attributable to them as individuals. 

Raphael, Gabriel, and Michael are the only angels named in the Scriptures. Others are occasionally named in Christian legends, such as 

Victor, who appeared to St. Patrick, the Apostle of Ireland.

PAGE 

VIII FALL OF WICKED ANGELS; WHY DEMONS TEMPT MAN 

The angels, like men, were created with free wills. It was necessary that the highest creatures should have free will, for the end of creation is God's glory and it would not be possible to give this glory to the Creator unless the one giving it were free in the action. The angels were created with a much better understanding of things than we have and intuitively realized why they were created and the necessity for honoring God. 

But Lucifer, who was the chief among them, seeing himself so magnificent, decided that he would not give the worship demanded but would seek eternal happiness otherwise. "I will ascend into heaven," he said in his heart; "I will exalt my throne above the stars of God, I will sit in the mountain of the covenant, in the sides of the north. I will ascend above the height of the clouds, I will be like the Most High" (Isaias xiv, 14).

Many other angels joined Lucifer in his plans. They had never yet seen God, for it was necessary that they should pass through a period of probation before this could happen. But they were all happy and Scripture speaks of their state then as "heaven." Those who did not sin were immediately confirmed in eternal goodness and admitted to the sight of God. 

Precisely in what way the pride of the angels was manifested, we are One old tradition holds that they were shown the Second Person of the Blessed Trinity made man and lying in a manger, with the order to adore Him as God. Seeing Him so humbled and so far beneath their own dignity, the bad angels refused. Lucifer, learning of the coming assumption of a created nature by the Second Person of the Blessed Trinity, proudly hoped that this union would be with himself, according to some writers. not sure.

The Apocalypse, chapter xii, describes what happened to the rebellious angels. "And there was a great battle in heaven, Michael and his angels fought with the dragon, and the dragon fought and his angels; and they prevailed not, neither was their place found any more in heaven. And that great dragon was cast out, that old serpent, who is called the devil and Satan, who seduceth the whole world; and he was cast unto the earth, and his angels were thrown down with him." The dragon, or Satan, was the once beautiful 

Lucifer. The word "Lucifer" indicates this angel's rank, not his real name; angelic names are descriptive, not merely designative. 

The devil and his angels were cast into never-ending torments. Christ, in Matt. xxv, 1, speaks of the "everlasting fire which was prepared for the devil and his angels.'

Once The angels were given only one chance to choose between good and evil, and were not accorded the opportunity to repent after their sin. having chosen good or evil, they were confirmed in it eternally. This happened because they knew precisely what they would do in offending God. 

The sinners saw the full enormity of their crime, and determined to take the risk. Knowledge does not come to the angels through a course of reasoning, as in man, but is intuitive.*

The wicked angels lost their virtue and thwarted the purpose of their creation, but were not robbed of their natural powers. Just as no matter *Intuitive, capable of knowing without deduction or reasoning.

PAGE 

made by God ever passes out of existence, despite its constant substantial changing, so does nothing spiritual ever return to nothingness. We know this from Scripture. Ecclesiastes iii, 14, says: "I have learned that all the works which God hath made continue forever." Even on the Day of Judgment, the universe is not to perish into nothingness, but is to undergo a tremendous change. To exist in itself is a perfection. And, although the bad angels deliberately tried to thwart God's plan for them, He has compelled them to submit to His authority and, out of their evil, has drawn good. Not honored by them in heaven, He compels them to honor Him by submitting to His majesty in hell.

The devil knows that he cannot wage war against God continuously, but for only a short time. When man's probation is over, Satan and his hordes will be unable to do anything more in their fight against the Creator. "Woe to the earth, and to the sea, because the devil is come down unto you," says Apoc. xii, "having great wrath, knowing that he hath but a short time."

God allows the devil to tempt us because, through temptation, we can add to our own glory and His by refusing to yield. We are promised definitely that no greater temptation is ever sent than man can bear. Raphael, the Archangel, said to Tobias: "And because thou wast acceptable to God, it was necessary that temptation should prove thee" (Tob. vii, 13). By resisting the devil, we are made stronger and stronger spiritually, and more easily able to withstand him. "Your adversary, the devil," says St. Peter's

First Epistle, v, 8, 9, 10, "as a roaring lion, goeth about seeking whom he may devour. Whom resist ye, strong in faith: knowing that the same affliction befalls your brethren who are in the world. But the God of all grace, who hath called us unto His eternal glory in Christ Jesus, after you have suffered a little, will Himself perfect you, and confirm you, and establish you."

It is possible for us to have the number of our temptations cut down through prayer, as the Lord's Prayer proves in the words, “And lead us not into temptation."

Satan, before the Redemption, had far more power than now. But, though his power is considerably restrained, it is still great and we must fight against him without ceasing. 

He has three enemies-the

Man is not tempted by the demons alone. world, the flesh, and the devil. Some temptations come from the persons and things around us; some come from within ourselves; some, from the devils. "For all that is in the world," wrote St. John (I John ii, 16), "is the concupiscence of the flesh, and the concupiscence of the eyes, and the pride of life, which is not of the Father, but is of the world."* *A translation of this text directly from the Greek as made by the Protestant scholar, Edgar J. Goodspeed, reads: "For all that there is in the world, the things that our physical nature and our eyes crave, and the proud display of life -these do not come from the Father, but from the world."

PAGE 

IX CREATION OF WORLD BY GOD; PROBLEM OF HUMAN EVOLUTION 

God created heaven and earth out of nothing. At first the earth was void and empty, and darkness was upon the face of the deep. "Be light made," said God, and light was made. Then God divided the light from darkness, calling the light day and the darkness night. This was the work of the first day in creation.

On the second day, God made the firmament and divided the waters above the firmament from those that were below it. He called the firmament heaven.

On the third day, God gathered the waters below the firmament and dry land appeared. This land He called earth, and the waters that He gathered together He called the seas. Then He commanded the earth to bring forth plant life, with the power of propagating itself. 

On the fourth day, He created the sun, the moon, and other heavenly lights, to divide the day and the night, and to distinguish times and seasons. 

On the fifth day, He commanded the waters to bring forth the creeping creatures having life, and flying creatures. The whales and all other water life and the birds were created in their kinds, with the power to increase and to multiply.

On the sixth day, He created the animal life of the earth and man. "Let Us make man to Our image and likeness: and let him have dominion over the fishes of the sea, and the fowls of the air, and the beasts, and the whole earth, and every creeping creature that moveth upon the earth." (Gen. 1,26). 

On the seventh day, God rested, "and He blessed the seventh day, and sanctified it" (Gen. ii).

Just how long these various "days" were, we do not know. They were probably very long periods of time.* They could hardly have been the same length as our days, for the sun was not created until the fourth day and the length of our days is founded upon the revolutions of the earth and the way the sun affects our sphere in these revolutions. 

A Catholic can, if he wishes, accept the theory that part of the world came into its present form through long evolution. That man's soul is a result of evolution is absolutely opposed to the Scriptures. Urraburu (1844-1904), the famous Catholic philosopher, said that all theologians, so far as he knew, think that it must be held that the human body was immediately created by God. Suarez, Valentia, Perrone, Mazzella, Hermann, and other noted theologians hold that this is the Catholic doctrine. 

It can be proved by reason alone that the human soul necessarily was created directly by God, for, if it were not, it would come from a cause less than itself (matter), and this is an absurdity. 

If science could prove—as it has not done that the human body was evolved from a lower form, it does not seem to the present writer that there would be any conflict with Genesis, provided we admit a special intervention of God above and beyond the ordinary laws of evolution, which intervention disposed this body for the reception of the created human soul. St. 

Augustine and other Fathers of the Church were evolutionists in a certain *The term used for day in the original Hebrew easily stands this interpretation. In fact, the English word for day can be used as meaning a long period, as in the expression "man's day."

PAGE 

sense but found no opposition between their theory and Holy Writ. The evolution question is really a problem of science rather than of religion. 

Both sides of the evolution question show intemperate writing. 

A study of how Catholic doctrine may be harmonized with the theory of evolution was presented by the Rev. John A. O'Brien, Ph.D., in his book,

Evolution and Religion (1932; Century Co.). It has the imprimatur of 

Bishop Joseph H. Schlarman of Peoria, Ill. The great Scripture scholar,

Father Charles L. Souvay, C.M., D.D., D.S.S., who died in late 1939 as 

Superior General of the Congregation of the Mission in Paris, wrote the introduction to Dr. O'Brien's work, and said: "No grander idea of God can be conceived this side of the veil which separates us from the beatific vision, than that of the Supreme Cause bringing to existence, by a simple act of His will, matter fraught from the beginning with all the potentialities, the successive unfoldings of which have expanded into this universe and will bring it to the ultimate perfection intended by its Almighty and All-Wise Maker." This quotation must be understood in the sense we have explained above. 

Exactly in what order God brought life lower than man into existence, we do not know. It is certain from reason that the non-living material world itself was not able to supply the principle of life to either plants or animals. This had to come from a higher power, as it was of greater perfection than mere matter. Pesch, the philosopher (in Phil, Nat., vol. v, page 27), says: "Just as God imposed a law upon Himself to conserve things at all times and to aid them in operation by His co-operation, and as He continually creates human souls, so He seems to have imposed a law upon Himself for the period of formation that at the proper time and place He would make organic things and the various species of living things. This often repeated production can be said neither to be a miracle nor creation ; for it is neither outside the order of nature nor is it from nothing.” 

Religion does not answer whether God originally made these living things simply as germs, or whether He created them in a more or less perfect state. We do not know whether only a few inferior species were created whence all others were derived, or whether the species were created as we see them living now and in the fossil state. But we do know for certain from Scripture that the origin of living beings is due to the will of God, that the fact that the earth concurred in making them is also due to His will, and that He gave them the power of propagating according to their species. 

The souls, or life principles, of animals below man and of plants come by generation. The soul of each man is created by God. 

Were it not

As God created the world, so He keeps it in existence. for Him, it would necessarily fall into the nothingness whence it came. 

How old the world is, religion does not tell us. 

Science believes that it has existed millions of years. Likewise religion is silent about the age of the human race. Old-time efforts to set the age of man by working out the mathematics of the genealogical lines in the Bible mean nothing, as it is known these lines were not inclusive of every generation. 

It is of faith that all men of the present human race descended from Adam and Eve. Religion does not offer any solution of the differentiation of mankind into races and colors. Science agrees with our belief that all men are of one species, because, no matter how the races are crossed, offspring can be produced and their offspring can remain indefinitely productive the scientific test of a real species.

PAGE 

CREATION OF ADAM AND EVE; THEIR REBELLION AGAINST GOD 

The Lord God "formed man of the slime of the earth and breathed into his face the breath of life, and man became a living soul” (Gen. ii, 7). 

This first man, Adam, was placed in a paradise of pleasure, where God brought forth all manner of trees, fair to behold and with pleasant fruit. 

Among these was one called the tree of life, and another termed the tree of knowledge. By eating of the tree of life, man would have been preserved in a constant state of health, vigor, and strength, and would never have tasted death. The tree of knowledge secured its name from the fact that 

God foresaw the devil deceitfully attribute to it the power of imparting a superior kind of knowledge, beyond that which God was pleased to give. "Of every tree of paradise thou shalt eat," said God to Adam, "but of the tree of knowledge of good and evil thou shalt not eat. For in what day soever thou shalt eat of it, thou shalt die the death” (Gen. ii). "It is not good for man to be alone," said God: “let Us make him a helpmate like to himself." So God brought all the beasts and fowls He had created before Adam, who named them, but there was not one among them that was fit to become the helpmate of man. "Then the Lord God cast a deep sleep upon Adam: and, when he was fast asleep, He took one of his ribs, and filled up flesh for it. And the 

Lord God built the rib which He took from Adam into a woman, and brought her to Adam. And Adam said: This now is bone of my bones, and flesh of my flesh; she shall be called woman, because she was taken out of man. Wherefore a man shall leave father and mother, and shall cleave to his wife: and they shall be two in one flesh" (Gen. ii).

The state of our first parents at this time was above that natural to man. 

By nature they had reason and free will and could know God as the Author of the world and love Him. In the simple state of nature, however, they would have been subject to suffering and death, to ignorance and the rebellion of the appetites, for man is a genuine animal, even if a rational one. 

But under these conditions man would have been destitute of grace and could never have hoped for the happiness of heaven, though he would have had God's ordinary providence assisting him in avoiding sin and in doing his duty, and, if faithful to the natural law, he would have had his reward in knowing God eternally so far as He can be known by reason and in attaining union with Him by love. If God had given man no more than this, He would not have been unjust, for man would have had everything his nature demanded. -sanc- But God gave Adam and Eve a gift that transcends all naturetifying grace. Man was able to believe in God as He is known by the light of faith, to hope that he would see Him face to face after this life, and to love Him with supernatural charity. As grace subjected man's soul to 

God, so, by a special privilege granted to Adam and Eve, man's body was subjected to the soul, and our first parents could neither suffer nor die, and the lower appetite could not rebel against the reason. As Genesis ii, 25 says: "And they were both naked: to-wit, Adam and his wife: and were not ashamed." Adam had such a knowledge of things, human and Divine, as befitted the head of the race. The two were in a state far above ours now.

PAGE 

When our first parents sinned, therefore, they knew exactly what they were doing. The devil attacked the woman first. Coming in the form of a serpent, he asked her why God had not given them permission to eat of every tree in paradise. Eve told him it was because they would die if they ate of the tree of knowledge. "No, you shall not die the death," said Satan. that, in what day soever you shall eat thereof, your and you shall be as gods, knowing good and evil.” "For God doth know eyes shall be opened

Since the fruit was delightful to behold, this temptation proved too much for the woman. She ate, and gave some of the fruit to Adam, who also ate. As their appetites were subject to reason, it was necessary for this temptation of our first parents to be external. It could not come from within, as do many of our temptations.

By the rebellion, the two lost sanctifying grace. With it also went those precious gifts man's body and soul had received. No longer were human beings above death and suffering, and no longer was reason able completely to hold in check the urgings of the flesh. To keep the appetites down in the future, a fight would be necessary. Immediately our first parents became aware of their nakedness and made themselves clothing of fig leaves.

As to the sin of our first parents, it certainly involved disobedience, as is evident from Gen. iii, 5; Gen. iii, 17-22; Romans v, 19. Theologians are generally agreed that the sin was fundamentally pride and then curiosity (inordinate desire for knowledge), infidelity, disobedience, etc. 

Adam heard God in paradise after the sin, and hid himself. God called him, and Adam said he was afraid of his nakedness. God immediately accused him of eating the forbidden fruit, asking who told him that he was naked. 

Adam said: "The woman whom thou gavest me to be my companion gave me of the tree, and I did eat.” 

When God asked Eve why she had sinned, she said: "The serpent deceived me, and I did eat." Then God pronounced His terrible curse on

Satan and the human race, but gave the first inkling of that tremendous event to occur later in human history-the coming of the Son of God. 

By Adam's sin, original sin passes to all of us. Had Eve alone sinned, we would not have been cursed, for she was not the head of our race, but Adam. Every soul entering this world comes with original sin, but God has given us a definite way to get rid of this stain-by Baptism.* *The two lone exceptions to original sin are Christ, who as God could not be subject to it, and Mary, preserved from it by grace.

PAGE 

XI EFFECTS ON US OF THE FALL OF OUR FIRST PARENTS 

After Adam and Eve had eaten the forbidden fruit in the Garden of 

Eden, God said to Eve: "I will multiply thy sorrows, and thy conceptions, and thou shalt bring forth children, and thou shalt be under thy husband's power, and he shall have dominion over thee." 

To Adam, He said: "Because thou hast hearkened to the voice of thy wife, and hast eaten of the tree, whereof I commanded thee that thou shouldst not eat, cursed is the earth in thy work; with labor and toil shalt thou eat thereof all the days of thy life. Thorns and thistles shall it bring forth to thee; and thou shalt eat herbs of the earth. In the sweat of thy face shalt thou eat bread till thou return to the earth, out of which thou wast taken: for dust thou art, and unto dust thou shalt return” (Gen. iv).

Then God drove our first parents out of Paradise, lest they should eat of the tree of life and live forever. And, to keep them out, He placed 

Cherubim on guard with flaming swords.

On account of the sin of Adam, we all share in his sin and punishment, as we would have shared in his happiness, had he remained faithful. Our first parents would have enjoyed constant happiness in this life and everlasting glory in the next, had they been true to God. But now, having lost innocence and sanctifying grace, they were doomed to sickness, suffering, and death. Their understanding was darkened, their will was weakened, and they had a strong inclination to evil. Every one of us comes into the world in the same condition. We inherit what is called original sin from them. "For by a man came death . . . . in Adam all die” (I Cor. xv, 21-22).

Only two human beings have been conceived without original sin— Jesus Christ and His mother, Mary. St. John the Baptist and probably the prophet Jeremias were conceived in original sin, but were cleansed from it before their birth (Luke i, 41; Jer. i, 5). Some would join St. Joseph to these latter two. Christ is necessarily exempt from all sin as the Son of 

God. Inasmuch as original sin comes through carnal propagation, and He was born of a virgin, it would not have come to Him anyhow. His is the lone example of a virgin birth.

While original sin is forgiven through Baptism, and man is given back sanctifying grace, the corruption of our nature and other punishments remain. God permits this to bring home to us the heinousness of sin. 

St. Paul in Romans v sets forth the Catholic doctrine of original sin. 

These words, from the twelfth verse, are particularly easy to understand: "By one man sin entered into this world, and by sin death; and so death passed upon all men, in whom all have sinned." In I Cor. xv, 21, St. Paul also says: "For by a man came death and by a man the resurrection of the dead;" i.e., death by Adam, resurrection by Christ.

The ancient Jews did not have as clear a knowledge of original sin as we do, but they knew something about it. The fourth Book of Esdras, written by a Jew in the first century after Christ, represents Adam as the author of the fall of the human race, and as having transmitted to all posterity the permanent infirmity, the malignity, the bad seed of sin. Christian 

Tradition, as well as the Bible, upholds the belief. St. Augustine (Contra- 

Jul. 11, x 33) invokes the testimony of eleven Fathers of the Church, Latin

PAGE 

and Greek, as proof of it. Hence Protestants who claim that the doctrine was unheard of among either Jews or Christians before St. Augustine are wrong. Reference to the doctrine is found in this passage from the Old 

Testament: "For behold, I was conceived in iniquities; and in sin my mother conceived me" (Ps. 1, 7).

We share in Adam's sin because he was our common father. A nation is considered as doing what its rulers do, and children, by common sentiment, share in part of the disgrace of a father's crime. So, in like manner, the whole race sins through Adam, its head.

We must remember that sanctifying grace is a free gift, far above what our nature demands. In depriving us of it, therefore, God is not unjust. 

It is due to His infinite mercy, not to any merit of ours, that He has established a way for us to get it back. 

Adam's sin comes to us not by way of imitation, but by propagation. 

It has not destroyed man's free will, or made him incapable of good actions. 

But, unless we acquire sanctifying grace, it is impossible for any of our actions to have supernatural merit. A man without grace might own the whole world and give it away to his fellows, but this would not take him one step higher in heaven. He would be rewarded for it, but only in a natural, not in a supernatural way. 

Original sin is a privation. It consists in this, that man is deprived of supernatural life which he would otherwise have. A simple explanation may give an idea of how it works. If my father were a millionaire, and, ten years before my birth, gambled on the stock market and lost all his wealth, and then died shortly after my birth, I would have no right to inherit the fortune he lost. In the same way, Adam had a great supernatural fortune. He gambled with it and lost it. Hence we come into the world deprived of it. "God is not unjust in punishing us on account of the sin of Adam, because original sin does not take away from us anything to which we have a strict right as human beings, but only the free gifts which God in His goodness would have bestowed on us if Adam had not sinned," says the revised Baltimore Catechism.* 

*Q. No. 61. The revised Catechism was completed but not yet published when this book was written.

PAGE XII 

WHAT IS MEANT BY ACTUAL SIN, MORTAL AND VENIAL 

Besides original sin there is another kind which we commit ourselves, called actual sin. The Baltimore Catechism defines actual sin as "any wilful thought, word, deed, or omission contrary to the law of God." To sin actually, it is necessary to know that a thing is sinful and to give away to it. 

Actual sin is divided into two classes-mortal and venial. Mortal sin is a serious offense against the law of God and is called mortal from the Latin word mors, meaning death. Mortal sin deprives us of spiritual life and sanctifying grace and, if we die in it, brings eternal death to the soul, makes it impossible for the soul to enjoy the life of heaven, and condemns it to everlasting banishment from God. 

Three things are necessary to make a sin mortal: First, grievous matter; second, sufficient reflection; third, full consent of the will. 

By grievous matter in sin we mean anything that does serious damage to God, to ourselves, or to our neighbor: for instance, it would be grievous matter to hate God, to steal a considerable sum of money, to take our own or another's life, or not to carry out the commands made by God through His Church for His proper worship, as in refusing to go to Mass on Sunday when we are able.

By sufficient reflection we mean giving enough consideration to the sin to realize what we are doing. Often some little time is necessary for this consideration before we can commit a mortal sin, but we are sometimes able to give sufficient reflection in a moment or two. 

By full consent of the will, we mean that we must really wish to do the thing that is sinful. It would be impossible to commit a mortal sin regardless of how serious the act might be if we did not give full consent of the will. A man might even murder another and not commit a mortal sin, as in the case when a person of violent temper is suddenly insulted by another, picks up a revolver, and shoots that person on the impulse of the moment, without taking time to reflect on the seriousness of the deed. 

Venial sin is a slight offense against the law of God in matters of less importance, or, in matters of great importance, it is an offense committed without sufficient reflection or without full consent of the will. 

Venial sin lessens the love of God in our heart, makes us less worthy of His assistance, and weakens our power to resist mortal sin. As the Scriptures point out, we fall by little and little, that is, we get into the habit of yielding to sin through repeated deliberate venial sins. 

The Christian Brothers' Manual of Christian Doctrine, page 16, says: 

"Every venial sin, however trifling it may appear, is a wrong done to the Divine Majesty. Hence the saints have not hesitated to say that it were better for the whole universe to be destroyed, than to attempt to save it by committing a single venial sin. Venial sin brings many severe punishments to man during the present life, and, if not expiated before his death, causes him to suffer the pains of purgatory before being admitted to heaven.”
