If a person should die in venial sin, he would not be condemned to eternal punishment, but would have to suffer a cleansing before entering heaven, because the Apocalypse, xxi, 7, speaking of heaven, declares: "There shall not enter into it anything defiled, or that worketh abomination or

PAGE 

maketh a lie, but they that are written in the book of the life of the Lamb." 

The Scriptures in many places show that there are distinctions between sins, although the actual words mortal and venial sin are not used in the Bible. 

Venial sin is regarded as a slight turning away from God, but to commit mortal sin is to turn our backs upon Him. All the venial sins in the world put together would not constitute one mortal sin. The inequality between sins is derived either from the object that determines the seriousness of the sin, or from the circumstances that increase or decrease the malice of a particular sin.

The Manual of Christian Doctrine mentions the following as sins that are generally considered mortal (the reader must always remember that sufficient reflection and full consent of the will are needed to make any sin mortal): "1. All those sins which directly assail God or any one of

His perfections; 2, all sins of lust; 3, all sins against a precept, the object of which was deemed to be of great importance by the legislator who framed it; such as failure to observe the law of fast and abstinence, to hear 

Mass on Sundays or holy days of obligation, to fulfill one's Easter duty, etc.; 4, all sins that grievously wrong our neighbor in his property, his fortune, or his reputation, as extensive theft, murder, etc." If a person committed a sin of which the nature was light but he thought it was a mortal sin, it would be a mortal sin for him because he was willing to offend seriously.

Mortal sin inflicts death on the soul by depriving it of sanctifying grace, which is supernatural life. The soul begins to live naturally in the first moment of our conception and, once it comes into being, it can never perish. It begins to live supernaturally when grace enters it. This supernatural life can be extinguished only by mortal sin. Venial sin tarnishes the soul but does not have the power to kill it. Mortal sin deprives the soul of all supernatural merits which it previously acquired, although when mortal sin is forgiven these merits return, through the mercy of God. The soul in mortal sin is unable to do anything worthy of heavenly reward, although God might give rewards in this world for good deeds done by one in mortal sin. The person in mortal sin also loses all participation in the satisfying merits of other members of the Church. Through the communion of saints, a person in the state of grace benefits not only from his own good deeds and prayers, but from the good deeds, prayers, and sacrifices of other members of the Church and from all Masses offered, etc. 

The chief sources of sin are seven: Pride, covetousness, lust, anger, gluttony, envy, and sloth. These are called capital sins because so many sins result from them. The following sins are said to cry to heaven for vengeance because their malice is so open that it provokes God's judgment more than other sins: Wilful murder, sins against nature (such as sodomy), oppression of the poor, the widows and orphans, and defrauding laborers of their wages.

We can sin by thought as well as by word, deed, or omission. A sin of thought is voluntarily to allow the mind to be occupied with something that is forbidden. Christ said: "You have heard that it was said to them of old: Thou shalt not commit adultery. But I say to you, that whosoever shall look on a woman to lust after her hath already committed adultery with her in his heart" (Matt. v, 7, 8). The Catholic Church is insistent on the doctrine that it is just as easy to offend God by deliberate evil thought

PAGE as it is by evil deeds. But evil thoughts that are not wilfully entertained are not sins.

Pride is an excessive love of our own superiority. An example of it that would constitute mortal sin would be refusal to admit a mistake by one whose doctrines had been formally condemned by the Church. Covetousness is an excessive love of earthly goods. Anger is loss of temper with the desire to do evil to another or to take revenge. Gluttony is inordinate love of eating and drinking, as in drunkenness. Envy is sadness experienced in thinking of the prosperity of others, insofar as we regard this success to be our own loss. Sloth is inordinate love of rest, leading us to omit or neglect our duties.

Although we must constantly strive to do so, none of us can avoid venial sin altogether, except by the special grace of God, as occurred in the case of the Blessed Virgin Mary, who was never stained by the slightest sin. St. James iii, 2, says: "For in many things we all offend." I John i, 8, says: "If we say that we have no sin, we deceive ourselves, and the truth is not in us." These texts give a decisive answer to those several sects that claim a man ceases to be a sinner when he "accepts Christ as his Savior.” 

Quite a number of saints never committed a mortal sin. In our own age, this was true of the Little Flower of Jesus, St. Therese. Under her vow of obedience, she was compelled to write her spiritual experiences, and she told that her father confessor expressed himself as believing that she had never been guilty of a mortal sin.

Early Protestants regarded every sin as deserving of eternal wrath. 

Some of them today do not know what you are talking about when you speak of mortal and venial sins. These terms are not used in the Bible, but the distinctions implied by the terms are shown there. We know positively, of course, that we are not all going to be damned, yet the Apostles taught us that we cannot possibly avoid all sin. Hence we are sure that there are sins that do not damn. Other sins, however, damn, for St. Paul, in Gal. v, 21, after enumerating some of them, says: "They who do such things shall not obtain the kingdom of God."

PAGE 

XIII 

HOW GOD ARRANGED TO REDEEM MANKIND AFTER FALL 

God did not abandon mankind when Adam and Eve had sinned, but promised a Redeemer, who was to satisfy for man's sin and reopen the gates of heaven to him. The first hint of this great event was given in the 

Garden of Eden itself, before our first parents were banished, and many other prophecies of it were made before Christ came. The Old Testament abounds with them.

The Religion has undergone three great stages. From the time of Adam to that of Moses, the Patriarchal religion held sway, when men followed the dictates of their consciences and handed down, by tradition, the revelations they had received. These revelations were not extensive, in comparison with ours, but God took good care of His faithful servants. religion is called Patriarchal because the heads of families were the chief priests. After a period of time that maybe covered thousands of years, God raised up Moses, a mighty prophet, to whom were given revelations upon which the Jewish religion was built. 

The literature of the Jews, as collected in the Old Testament, is the sublimest record of thought that has come down to us from antiquity. 

The Jews, like the Patriarchs, looked forward to a Redeemer and He came in the fullness of time in the Person of Jesus Christ. Judaism was a religion of preparation for Christianity, the perfect revelation. We are not to look for any further public revelation until the end of the world. This is made quite evident in ancient Christian Tradition, which would preclude additional revelations such as Swedenborg's or that of the Mormons. The perfection of Christian revelation as described in such Biblical works as the Epistle to the Hebrews would forbid us to expect any new revelation. 

Judaism was the true faith until Christ came and established the Christian Church. As the Catholic Church is the only denomination that has preserved Christ's religion in its purity, and, in fact, is the only Church among all the Christian organizations that can even prove historically that it extends back to Him, it is the true religion of today. It is absolutely impossible for it ever to pass away. It will be on earth for all time, since 

Christ promised that He would be with it until the consummation of the world, and it will exist eternally in heaven.

Jesus Christ, we know, is the Redeemer promised by God, for He literally fulfilled the prophecies made about Him. He proved the truth of His mission by the most stupendous miracles, and He voiced such sublime teachings, supplementing them by a life of such lofty virtue, that, if He were not what He claimed, we would have to consider God Himself a mocker.

Many prophecies were made about the coming Redeemer and were all literally fulfilled by Jesus Christ. He claimed to be God and, if He is not, God is a deceiver, which is impossible. There is nothing better substantiated in history than the Gospels and other portions of the New

PAGE Testament, and their story of the wonders which He effected is the most startling record in literature. Attempts have been made to discredit these books. We have been told that they were written long after the supposed authors were dead, that they were interpolated, etc. Any one who wishes to make a study of this will find that our scholars have outshone our enemies at every stage. Those interested in the subject should consult any of the modern Catholic Scripture manuals or commentaries. 

We believe of Jesus Christ that He is the Second Person of the Blessed Trinity and as God existed from all eternity. He became man in time, taking a body and soul like ours. He had a human body, a human intellect, a human will, a human memory, and every other human faculty. 

As man

He is a creature, but He is also God and has Divine will and Divine intellect. 

He has two natures, the human and the Divine, existing in one Person, and this Person is God, the Second Person of the Trinity. As God and as man, Christ exists by the one act of existence. 

He was born as man of the Blessed Virgin Mary and, inasmuch as a mother brings forth a person, not simply a body, and the Person in this case is God, Mary is strictly entitled to be called the Mother of God. She has wonderful prerogatives because of this Divine maternity. For instance, she was conceived without the taint of original sin. This is called her 

Immaculate Conception. She also never committed an actual sin, mortal or venial. We do not adore her, because she is a creature and not God, but we give her the highest honor that can be given to an ordinary creature because God has given her this honor. His choosing of her to be His mother is the greatest dignity that could be given to any created person. 

It should be of great consolation to women to know that one of their sex occupies the highest place that has been given by God to any created person. 

Jesus Christ is above her in heaven, but remember that He is a Divine Person who assumed a created nature and is not a created person. 

Christ became man on Annunciation day. The angel Gabriel appeared to Mary and announced to her that she was to be the mother of the Son of the Most High, saying to her: "The Holy Ghost shall come upon thee, and the power of the Most High shall overshadow thee. And therefore the Holy which shall be born of thee shall be called the Son of God." 

The Son of God was therefore conceived and made man by the power of the Holy Ghost in the womb of the Blessed Virgin Mary. It is evident from the account in St. Luke's Gospel that Mary had the freedom to accept or reject. She accepted and thus played a willing part in our redemption. 

Both from prophecy and from the accounts in the New Testament, we know that Christ's birth was a virgin one and that He had no earthly father. To deny this is blasphemy. The Child was carried by the Virgin in her womb for the usual nine months and then came forth in a virginal manner, for example as light comes through a window. God became man in order to free us of sin. God could have forgiven man by a single act of His will had He wished, but, in order to bring home the seriousness of sin, He demanded a satisfaction adequate to the sin. The sin was infinite because it was a serious offense directed against an infinite being, and, therefore, God demanded an infinite satisfaction. Man, being a finite creature, was not

PAGE 

able to give this, and hence God Himself became man and as man offered Himself up to Himself as an infinite propitiation for our sins. 

Christ did not become man immediately after the fall of our first parents, but waited for a considerable time. St. Thomas Aquinas and other great writers of the Church held it was fitting that the Son of God should be preceded on this earth by a long line of prophets, just as a great king is preceded by a procession of courtiers, ladies, and pages, when holding his formal court. Another reason might be advanced. Christ did not come until the world was well civilized and men were accustomed to keeping exact records of their achievements, and, hence, there is far less danger of having legend or falsehood surround the accounts of Him, giving men less excuse when they refuse to believe in Him.

The Son of God lived on earth about thirty-three years and led a holy life of poverty and suffering. For about thirty years of this time, He lived in retirement with His Mother, and then He began His public life, which lasted about three years and was climaxed with His crucifixion. The fact that He gave thirty years of this life to the Blessed Virgin Mary, and only three to us, should be a strong argument as to the intensity of the love that 

God has for her.

Christ could have saved us by a simple act of His will, but He led a life of hardship and suffered the most cruel death that has ever been undergone by anyone to prove the depth of His love for us. Christ, as we know from the Scriptures, claimed equality with God on a number of occasions, but on other occasions said that His Father was greater than He. These texts are easily explained, if we remember that sometimes He referred to Himself as God and sometimes as man. As man, He was one of His own creatures. Jesus Christ is God; Jesus Christ is man.

Those who lived before Christ and who were saved escaped damnation by believing in the Redeemer to come and by avoiding sin, or by repenting of it. Children were saved through circumcision or the faith of their parents, the boys being compelled by the Mosaic law to undergo circumcision eight days after birth.

Christ was crucified as a common criminal, becoming, as was prophesied of Him, the most abject and despised of men. Although the very God of 

Heaven, He humiliated Himself more than any mere human being has ever done, and He suffered more intensely than any other person has ever been called upon to do.

He was absolutely perfect. Not a flaw can be pointed out in His life or teachings. And, as His life was of the greatest benefit in every way, so the career of the Church that He established has ever been of the greatest value to mankind. Ours is the Church that founded modern civilization and we can proudly assert today, without fear of having the contradictory proved, that Catholicity is the greatest bulwark of civilization.

PAGE 

XIV RESUME' OF LIFE OF OUR LORD AND SAVIOR, JESUS 

In our study of the true religion, it is fitting that we give a resume' of the life of Christ. He was born in a stable at Bethlehem of the Blessed 

Virgin Mary, with St. Joseph, the husband of Mary, as His foster father. 

The teaching of the Church is unanimous that Mary had no other children, and, although brethren of the Lord are spoken of in the Scriptures, this term was used by the Jews for close relatives, not only for actual children of the same mother. The Bible gives us other examples of such usage; for instance, Abraham and his nephew, Lot. Christ obeyed all the demands of the Jewish law, and His first sufferings on our behalf, after the poverty of His birth, came when He was circumcised. Wise men of the East saw a strange star that appeared in the heavens at the time of His birth and were guided by it to His crib, where they adored Him, offering Him gifts. They were the first Gentiles or non-Jews to worship Him. He was presented in the Temple by His parents in obedience to the Jewish law, which demanded that this be done with the first-born. King Herod, having heard that a new king had been born, became jealous of Him and attempted to get rid of Him by having all the male children of two years and under in Bethlehem put to death, but St. Joseph, warned by an angel of this unholy design, took Mary and the Child into Egypt, where they remained until the death of Herod, after which they returned to Palestine, making their home in Nazareth. At the age of twelve years, when Christ was taken to Jerusalem for a Jewish festival day by His parents, they lost Him and after a three-day search found Him in the midst of the learned doctors of the Temple, hearing them and asking them questions. This is practically all we know about the childhood of Christ.

In the fulfillment of many prophecies, St. John the Baptist prepared the way for Christ by preaching penance to the Jews just before our Lord's public ministry. It was customary among the Jews at that time to baptize as a sign of penance and Christ Himself, although He was sinless and incapable of sin through being a Divine Person, had Himself baptized by John in order to teach us the value of Baptism and to give us a powerful lesson in humility. While He was being baptized, a voice from heaven declared Him to be the beloved Son of God, and the Holy Ghost descended upon Him in the form of a dove. Immediately after the Baptism, Christ retired into the wilderness, where He kept a strict fast for forty days, which gave us our Catholic idea of the observance of Lent as a time of fast and penance. At the close of this fast, He permitted the devil to tempt Him, in order to show us how we should act under temptation; and, when He had gained the victory, the angels came and ministered to Him.

His first miracle was performed at a marriage feast in Cana, where He turned water into wine. It was at the special request of the Blessed 

Virgin Mary that He did this, although the Scriptures are plain in stating that the time had not yet arrived for miracle working. God designed things in this way to bring home to us with particular force the value of the prayers of the Blessed Virgin Mary on our behalf.

PAGE 

Christ began His public life about the age of thirty years and chose two groups of close followers to assist Him in His work. Seventy-two of these are called disciples and twelve are called Apostles. All His followers are referred to as disciples, but this group of seventy-two was especially close to Him. The Apostles were later constituted as the first Bishops of the Church. Peter was the first Pope and stands out conspicuously in the entire Scriptural record as a leader. 

Christ worked many miracles, casting out devils from persons who were possessed, curing all manner of diseases, even raising the dead to life. He raised to life the dead daughter of Jairus and also the son of a widow of Naim. He raised up the dead Lazarus when the corpse was corrupting. He calmed a stormy sea by His word, He walked upon the water without sinking. He healed a man who was ill for thirty-eight years of palsy. At one time He fed 5,000 persons with five loaves, and at another time 4,000 with seven loaves, and He performed many other wonders. He gave the power of working miracles to His Apostles and Matt. xvi shows us that He promised to make Peter the Head of His Church, to build His Church upon him, and to give him the keys of the kingdom of heaven. 

Christ made many bitter enemies, because He scored the Jewish religious leaders for their sins and for having encumbered the Hebraic law with many observances that had not been revealed by God, such as certain scrupulous prohibitions of activity on the Sabbath. Finally, the hatred against Him became so intense that His enemies determined to put Him to death and He permitted them to murder Him, offering Himself as a sacrifice to God for our sins. He was able to foresee His death and, on the night before He died, suffered what is called the agony in the Garden of Gethsemane, when the burden of the sins of the entire world so pressed upon Him that His body broke out into a bloody sweat, a form of suffering that has happened only two or three times in the history of the world. The 

Jewish leaders aroused the multitude against Him, and the people demanded His death. Because Judea was a conquered nation at the time, it was necessary for them to get the approval of the Roman governor, Pontius Pilate, for this deed. Pilate realized that Christ was innocent and weakly hoped to have the people release the Master if they got a taste of blood. 

He, therefore, had Jesus cruelly beaten, and soldiers crowned the Prisoner with thorns in mockery of His Kingship. Then, yielding to the demands of the populace, Pilate ordered Christ to be put to death and He was led away with two thieves to die on Calvary. He was nailed to the Cross and 

His previous sufferings had been so severe that He died within three hours. 

We call the day on which He died Good Friday because His death proved 

God's infinite love for man and purchased every blessing for us.

As the Baltimore Catechism says: "From the sufferings and death of 

Christ we learn the great evil of sin, the hatred God bears to it, and the necessity of satisfying for it."

After Christ's death His soul descended into hell, not the hell of the damned, but a place of rest called limbo. Here the souls of the just went before the Ascension of Christ into heaven. The gates of heaven were closed to the human race by the sin of Adam, and were not opened again until

PAGE Christ went there Himself at the head of the procession of all those souls who had been confined to limbo before His Ascension into heaven. He won the opening of heaven for us through His death on the Cross. Christ went into limbo to announce to the souls in prison the joyful news of their redemption. While His soul was in limbo, His body was in the holy sepulchre, for His death had occurred in the same manner as ours, through the separation of the soul from the body. He arose from the dead, glorious and immortal, on Easter Sunday, the third day after His death, and stayed on earth forty days after His Resurrection, to show that He was truly risen and to give further instructions to His Apostles. Then He ascended into heaven, where 

He sits at the right hand of God, the Father Almighty. By saying that He sits at the right hand of God we mean that, as God, Christ is equal to His Father in all things and, as man, is in the highest place of heaven next to God. The Blessed Virgin Mary, according to the tradition of the Church, also had her body taken to heaven to rejoin the soul, and she is in the highest place of heaven next to her Divine Son.

PAGE 

 XV CATHOLIC DOCTRINE ABOUT GRACE 

There are two chief effects of the Redemption: first, the satisfaction of God's justice by Christ's suffering and death; second, the gaining of grace for men. By grace we mean a supernatural life or power conferred on us by God through the merits of Jesus Christ for our salvation. It is a free gift. God does not have to give it to us. Without it, we cannot do one single thing worthy of supernatural reward. We are utterly dependent on 

God for our salvation, but, if we do what He has demanded, we can be sure that God will give us sufficient grace to gain heaven. 

Every man receives enough grace to save his soul, but we can, and frequently do, resist the grace of God. Grace is Divine life surging through the soul. It is a mysterious force that is created, but is so intimately connected with God that Christ spoke of it as a residing of Himself and His Father in the souls of those who loved God and whom God loved. "If any one love Me, he will keep My Word, and My Father will love him, and We will come to him, and will make Our abode with him" (John xiv, 23). 

The Scriptures also speak of people in grace as temples of the Holy Ghost.* 

If a person dies in the state of grace, that person is sure to be admitted to heaven because he has supernatural life in his soul. 

The Rev. M. V. Kelly, C.S.B., in his Catechism of Christian Doctrine, says: "Every Christian is born twice. Baptism is his second birth. At Baptism his soul begins its second life. His soul then leads two lives at the same time. Nothing can take away the first life which the soul received when it was created. It never loses that life in this world or in the next. 

Both the souls in heaven and the souls in hell will have this first life forever. .. The first life of the soul is called its natural life; its second life is called supernatural. The body has life when the soul is in it. The soul has supernatural life when God puts sanctifying grace into it. . . . . The soul of an unbaptized person has only natural life. Natural life gives it power to see, hear, think, walk, talk, and do everything that is necessary to get along in this world, but natural life does not give it power to do anything for the next world. While the soul has supernatural life, it can do something every moment for the next world. When the soul has supernatural life, every thought, word, and deed can gain a spiritual reward in heaven." 

If a person is without supernatural grace, nothing that he does is worthy of supernatural reward; therefore, we need grace or the application to us of the merits of Jesus Christ before we can do anything at all or make even the slightest motion towards salvation. As Christ said: "Without Me you can do nothing" (John xv, 5). Unless a person has the assistance of God's grace, it is impossible for him to keep out of grievous sin for any great length of time.

Grace is divided into two main classes-sanctifying and actual grace. 

Sanctifying grace is a supernatural life that comes into the soul and remains there until we drive it out by mortal sin. "Sanctifying grace is an entirely free and gratuitous supernatural gift of God which the Holy Ghost communicates to the soul in consideration of *"Know you not that your members are the temple of the Holy Ghost, who is in you, whom you have from God, and you are not your own?" (I Cor. vi, 19.)

PAGE the infinite merits of Jesus Christ for the purpose of enabling her to work out her eternal salvation," says Father F. J. Remler, C.M., in Supernatural

Merit (page 20). "Its effects are the following: (1) It cleanses the soul from the defilement and disabilities of original sin and mortal sin; (2) it elevates her from the state of fallen nature to that of super nature; (3) it makes her participate in the nature of God; (4) it renders her holy and pleasing in His sight, so that she is loved by Him with an altogether inexpressible tenderness; (5) it confers on her an absolute, incontestable, and inalienable title to the enjoyment of heavenly glory after death, and (6) it makes it possible for her to perform during her sojourn on earth actions that are meritorious, i.e., deserving of a supernatural reward both here and hereafter."

By nature, we have no claim to this gift. God gives it to us because of His own great love for us. It is never refused to any one who fulfills the clear conditions laid down by God for gaining it. 

Actual grace is a help of God that enlightens our mind and moves our will to shun evil and to do good. It is something passing and comes, as it were, in the form of spurts of power from Almighty God, while sanctifying grace is something that lasts, sticking to us like a habit, whence it is called habitual grace. 

If a room is lighted by electricity, the light will stay on until I push a button and shut it off. Sanctifying grace acts in somewhat the same way. 

It stays in the soul until the button of mortal sin is pushed and then it is extinguished. Actual grace is something like a flashlight that I might momentarily press on. It is a transient help. Sanctifying grace is life; actual grace is power.

Father Remler, in Supernatural Merit, page 24, defines actual grace and explains its operation, saying: "Actual grace is an internal, supernatural help which enables us to do good and avoid evil. It operates in the soul in this manner: "I. It enlightens the understanding to see what ought to be done or avoided and inclines the will toward what is good or averts it from evil. 

On this account it is called 'exciting' grace and 'preventing' grace. It is called exciting grace because it rouses and invites us, as it were, to do good and avoid evil; and it is called preventing grace, because it is wholly the work of God in our souls and precedes every deliberate and voluntary act of our own, as experience teaches us; for we feel these holy inspirations arise in us without anything done by us to secure them; or without having in our power to hinder their coming, though, when they do come, we have it in our power to comply with them or to resist them. "II. When we freely comply with this first motion of actual grace it continues to fortify and strengthen us to finish the good work we have begun; and on this account it is 'concomitant' grace, because it accompanies us during the whole action; and 'strengthening' or 'helping' grace, because it assists our weakness and enables us to perform the good work in the face of every obstacle. "For a just appreciation of the great value of actual grace, it must be remembered that without it we cannot, by our own natural strength, do the least thing towards our eternal salvation.' 

It will be seen, therefore, that grace is necessary to salvation because,

PAGE 

without it, we can do nothing to merit heaven. We can and often do resist it, however, to our misfortune. 

The grace of perseverence is a particular gift of God which enables us to continue in the state of grace till death. 

Following are some Scriptural texts that uphold the Catholic doctrine of grace: "By grace you are saved through faith, and that not of yourselves, for it is the gift of God;-not of works, that no man may glory" (Eph. ii, 5). "It is God who worketh in you to will and to accomplish, according to His good will" (Philip. ii, 13). "And we helping do exhort you, that you receive not the grace of 

God in vain" (II Cor. vi, 1).

By being good, we grow in actual grace. "He that is good shall draw grace from the Lord" (Prov. xii, 2).

The chief graces are faith, hope, and charity. Faith is a Divine virtue by which we firmly believe what God has revealed. Like every grace, it is a free gift of God, and we cannot make the slightest motion towards supernatural faith without His assistance. This explains why it is that the world is so slow in accepting revealed truth. It is so steeped in sin as to be unworthy of God's spiritual help. Hope is a Divine virtue by which we firmly trust that God will give us eternal life and the means to obtain it. 

Charity is a Divine virtue by which we love God above all things for His own sake and our neighbor as ourselves for the love of God. Christianity can be summed up in this one word: Love. But to have such a type of love as Christ demands, we must have the help of grace. We can neither believe, hope, nor love in the manner necessary for salvation without the grace of God. 

That faith is a gift is proved by the experience of the Jewish nation. 

Although the Old Testament, which the Jews were constantly studying, told with the utmost precision where Jesus Christ was to be born, how He was to be rejected by the Jews, and how He was to be crucified, these people because of their sins were unable to recognize Him as the Messias. As the 

Old Testament sets forth, they were denied this gift because of their continued rebellion against God.

PAGE 

XVI HOLY GHOST STAYS WITH CHURCH; PREVENTS ERROR 

Christ promised the Apostles that He would send them the Holy Ghost, who would remain with the Church forever and keep her in all truth. “And 

I will ask the Father, and He shall give you another Paraclete, that He may abide with you forever. The Spirit of Truth, whom the world cannot receive, because it seeth Him not, nor knoweth Him; but you shall know Him, because He shall abide with you, and I shall be in you. I will not leave you orphans, I will come to you" (John xiv, 16, 17, 18).

The Holy Ghost is the Third Person of the Blessed Trinity and possesses the same Divine nature and substance as God the Father and God the Son, but is a distinct Person. The Trinity came about eternally somewhat in this fashion. God the Father is infinite intellect. That intellect thought, and its expression of thought is called the Word, just as, when our intellects express a thought in themselves, that expression is called the word of the mind. In the case of God, this Word or Logos is the Second Person of the Blessed Trinity, who is, therefore, the expression of His Father's thought. 

The Father saw the Son and loved Him, and the Son saw the Father and loved Him; this bond of love is the Holy Ghost. The Holy Ghost proceeds from the Father and the Son as the mutual love between them, and has the same nature as they.

That He proceeds from the Son as well as from the Father is denied by the Greek separatists, but the doctrine is demanded by the Scriptures, Tradition, and reason. 

The Holy Ghost is equal to the Father and the Son and is the same Lord and God as they are. When He came down upon the Church, therefore, both Christ and the Father also descended, because they are the same 

God. This is why we have Christ saying: "I will not leave you orphans, I will come to you." We find the Scriptures referring to the Holy Ghost as 

God; for instance: "Why hath Satan tempted thy heart, that thou shouldst lie to the Holy Ghost? Thou hast not lied to men, but to God" (Acts v, 3, 4). The Bible attributes Divine perfections to the Holy Ghost, such as omnipotence, omnipresence, omniscience, eternity, etc. It also attributes

Divine works to Him, such as creation, regeneration, sanctification, communication of all spiritual gifts, etc. (I Cor. xii, 8, 11; Ps. 138, 7, 10; I 

Cor. 2, 10, 11; Ps. 103, 30; John 3, 5; I Cor. 6, 11; Romans 5, 5; Acts 2, 4, 17, etc.). 

Christ, in John xv, 26, says: "But, when the Paraclete cometh, whom 

I will send you from the Father, the Spirit of Truth, who proceedeth from the Father, He shall give testimony of Me." The Greeks call upon this text as proof that the Holy Ghost proceeds from the Father alone, but they overlook the text in the next chapter, verses 14, 15, where Christ says of the Holy Ghost: "He shall glorify Me; because He shall receive of mine, and shall show it to you. All things whatsoever the Father hath, are Mine; therefore I said, that He shall receive of Mine, and show it to you." Christ in these texts proves that what belongs to the Father belongs to Him, and that the Holy Ghost shall receive of Christ as well as of the Father, and, hence, that the Holy Spirit proceeds from both the Father and the Son. 

A statement made by Christ in St. John's Gospel xvi, 13, says: "But when He, the Spirit of Truth, is come, He will teach you all truth." Inas-

PAGE 

much as Christ taught that the Holy Ghost was to remain in the Church forever, and that He Himself was to be with the Church all days, even to the end of the world (Matt. xxviii, 20), we see that the Catholic Church has abundant Scriptural backing for her insistance that it is impossible for true Christianity to lose the actual doctrine of Jesus Christ. 

The Holy Ghost came down upon the Apostles in the form, as it were, of tongues of fire, and sat upon every one of them, ten days after the Ascension of our Lord; and the day of His descent is called Whitsunday or Pentecost. As we know from chapter i of the Acts, there were about 

125 persons in the room when the Holy Ghost came, and Mary, the Mother of Jesus, was among them.

The Holy Ghost came to sanctify the Church, to enlighten and to strengthen the Apostles, and to enable them to preach the Gospel. Before 

His descent, they had been cowards, afraid to show themselves to the Jews for fear that they, like their Master, would be put to death; but, immediately after the coming of the Holy Ghost, they boldly went out and taught, upholding Christ as the long-expected Jewish Messias, the God-man who had been put to death, but who arose again and ascended into heaven, and who is our only hope for eternal salvation.

The Apostles, with the exception of Paul, who was a later convert, were all uneducated to begin with, but with the assistance of the Holy Ghost they were able to give the world a revelation that revolutionized civilization. 

The chief proof we offer of the fact that Christ really rose from the dead is that all or most of the Apostles suffered martyrdom in testimony of this teaching, thus furnishing the only example in history of a group of founders of a religion who willingly went to death in testimony of it. Other prophets, so-called, like Joseph Smith the Mormon, have been killed for their religious beliefs, but did not have the opportunity to accept or refuse death here and now pending. St. John died a natural death, but he was once thrown into a pot of boiling oil and was saved only by a miracle: hence, the Church honors him as a genuine martyr.

While the Blessed Virgin Mary received the Holy Ghost, she never taught in public but always led a retired life of prayer and contemplation. 

Her great work, after she had borne and cared for Christ and had witnessed His Passion, offering up her sufferings with His for our sake, was to be done after death, when she became our chief intercessor in heaven, and the dispenser of the graces won for us by her Son. 

True to the promises of Christ, the Holy Ghost will abide with the Church forever and guide it in the way of holiness and truth. 

As God, the Holy Ghost is everywhere. As the Spirit of Truth and 

Sanctifier of Souls, He is especially in the Catholic Church and her members. 

The Catholic Church is the only organization known to humanity whose doctrines fit every age without need of change. She can furnish a cure for every moral evil that mankind ever faces, and this cure can be used just as effectively for problems in the twentieth century as it was in the first, fifth, thirteenth, or sixteenth. She never changes her doctrines, and has allowed even entire nations to leave her fold rather than compromise in the least in her moral teachings. It is because the Holy Ghost guides her that she is this way. God knows all truth and never changes. Hence the organization that He directly guides cannot possibly accept one thing as truth in one age and something else in another.

PAGE 

XVII SOME FACTS ABOUT OUR ATTITUDE TOWARDS THE BIBLE 

The Bible is a collection of books written by men who were inspired by God. By this we mean that these men were impelled by the Holy Ghost to write and that they were protected by Him from error as they wrote. 

They followed their own literary style and worked like all other writers, consulting other manuscripts and persons. 

Catholics accept the Bible without quibbling, but use scholarship in determining possible interpolations, mistranslations, etc. When the Bible describes great miracles or other things hard to understand, they do not try to explain them away, but accept them, remembering that God is infinitely powerful. "No word shall be impossible with God” (Luke i, 37).

We accept the Bible as inspired because our Church has declared it to be so. The Church was in existence and actively operating some time before the New Testament was written. St. Matthew's Gospel, the first book penned, was prepared about six years after our Lord's Ascension; St.

Mark's was written about ten years after the Ascension; St. Luke's about twenty-four years; St. John's about sixty-three years, etc. (There is controversy among scholars as to the actual dates.) Many truths that had been taught and acted upon until that time were recorded in the Bible, and we can refer to the Good Book today as proof of these truths; but the fact remains that the Church was able to live for some time before the New 

Testament was written.

In addition to the books that appear in the New Testament, a number of others were penned in early Christian days that purported to be inspired. 

The Church, using her Divine authority, separated the wheat from the chaff, the inspired from the uninspired. 

Throughout the centuries, she has jealously guarded the Bible. The claim that she kept it hidden and refused to give her children access to it is false. The claim of Martin Luther that he accidentally discovered it is accepted by no reliable historian today. (See Catholic Encyclopedia, Martin

Luther.) During the middle ages Catholic monks spent years and years transcribing the Bible by hand, before the days of printing. A large number of editions in the vernacular had been issued before the so-called Reformation. That the Bible was not more widely distributed beforehand is easily explained by the fact that printing came in just a short time before the Reformation.

So jealous is the Church of the Bible that she forbids her children to read any edition that she does not herself approve. Thus she guards them from slipshod scholars, who might introduce texts that are not in the Book, change the wording, or take texts from codices that will not stand the tests of scientific scholarship. 

She encourages Bible study: Pope Leo XIII on December 13, 1898, granted to all the faithful who shall devoutly read the Gospels, for at least a quarter of an hour, a three hundred days' indulgence once a day, provided that the Bible they read has been approved by legitimate authority; and a plenary indulgence to all who shall do this for a month and who shall confess their sins, receive Communion, and pray for the Papal intentions. 

Protestants who charge the Catholic Church with being the enemy of

PAGE 

the Bible seem to forget that, were it not for Catholicity, they would have no Bible today.

The Bible gives internal evidence of being unusual. It is the only book extant to which century after century has contributed and which, making prophecies in one century, was able to record, hundreds of years later, the exact details of the fulfillment of these promises. The Old Testament definitely foretells Jesus Christ's birthplace, the fact that He was to be born of a virgin, His miracles, facts about His teachings, when He was to be born, His repudiation by the Jewish people, His crucifixion and death, the spread of His Church among the Gentiles, the dispersion of the Jewish people, and many other things which were fulfilled most literally, as recorded in the New Testament and history. 

Although some of the Bible was written in the very childhood of the human race, there is not a thing in it that is rendered ridiculous by the scientific discoveries made since. On the other hand, it contains injunctions that were made for the protection of the people's health, at a time when the scientific laws back of them were not known to the people. 

Definite laws were laid down for guarding the Jewish people against disease germs, although the people knew nothing about such danger. They were not allowed to handle dead bodies promiscuously, and had to follow many other precautions, as shown in Leviticus, Numbers, etc. 

The Bible shows evidence of inspiration in its descriptions. God is able to get right to the essence of things and describe them in about a fifth or sixth the number of words a man would require. Christ's blessing the children is one of the best known facts of history; Matt. xix describes it in three short verses. St. Paul's conversion is one of the most striking stories we know; it occupies only eighteen verses in Acts ix. Matthew's description of the tremendous convulsions of nature that attended Christ's death is given in four brief verses; it would take a page of a modern newspaper to give as vivid a word picture. The prophet Isaias (v) and the Apostle Paul (Rom. xi) drew magnificent lessons from the vineyard and olive tree; the pseudo Book of Mormon, attempting to unite these stories and to teach the same lessons, takes many weary pages for the task. 

Christ's sermons were marvels of simplicity. Try as we might, it would be impossible to improve upon them. There is not a word too many, and, if we would add any, the impelling force would often be lost. Yet four inspired men recorded these sermons, John writing almost three score and ten years after Christ, Mark and Luke never having heard Jesus personally. 

As a sample of Christ's remarkable clarity, study His address to His disciples on the eve of His crucifixion, as recorded in John xiv, xv, and xvi. 

All we know about Abraham is found in a few pages of the Bible. 

Yet we are as well acquainted with him as we are with Abraham Lincoln or Napoleon, concerning whom libraries have been written. 

Hundreds of the adages and familiar expressions we use are taken from the Bible-like the "fly in the ointment," "sufficient for the day is the evil thereof," "physician, heal thyself," "the abomination of desolation,"

The book thus shows a wonderful knowledge of human nature, as much, in fact, as all the rest of literature put together. etc.

It never records a single meaningless miracle. Yet it was written by

PAGE many men in many places at many times. The value of this argument can be gauged only by a study of spurious Biblical works, where God is recorded as doing the most stupendous things for no apparent reason. 

Book of Mormon.) (See the So we might go on multiplying arguments. But the chief argument for us is the fact that the Church, which is one in faith, holy in doctrine and in results attained, universal in appeal, and apostolic in direct succession, tells us that the Bible is inspired.

 XVIII OLD CONTROVERSIES ABOUT THE BIBLE 

There are two serious controversies between Catholics and Protestants over the Bible. The Catholics accept more books (and parts of books) in their Old Testament than the Protestants do, and Catholics insist that the Church has the final word in interpreting Holy Writ, whereas Protestants claim that this power belongs to individuals. 

Although the Catholic Bible contains a number of portions not found in many Protestant versions, there has been a tendency of various Protestant scholars to pay serious attention to these so-called "Apocrypha." The Smith- 

Goodspeed Complete Bible, "an American translation," gives all the "Apocrypha," and that marvelously erudite work, Cruden's Concordance, a standard reference book for Biblical students for 200 years, pays as much attention to the disputed sections as it does to the rest of the Bible.* 

The "Apocrypha" formed part of the King James Protestant version in 1611 and it is because of Puritan influence that they disappeared from many English editions (except the Catholic). The Anglican Bible today includes these portions, but regards them as doubtful, keeping them separate. 

Yet they were definitely part of the Bible in earlier centuries, coming into the Christian Scriptures from the Septuagint. The first Christians used the Greek version of the Old Testament, known as the Septuagint from the fact that it was translated from Hebrew at Alexandria, Egypt, by seventy Jewish scholars (according to tradition) in the third century before Christ. 

New Testament quotations of the Old Testament are taken mostly from it, not from the Hebrew text. The early Latin editions of the Bible, including the famous text of St. Jerome issued about 400 A. D. (known as the Vulgate), brought the Septuagint into the language of Rome. The text of St. Jerome is the official one of the Catholic Church. The oldest extant Hebrew manuscript of the Old Testament dates from no earlier than the ninth century, A. D. The Greek and Oriental versions are all of later age than the Latin 

Vulgate, so far as manuscripts are concerned. 

Martin Luther separated the "Apocrypha" from the rest of the books and put them into a special section in his German Bible of 1534, and early English Protestant Bibles followed the same system (used in our day by some Protestants). In 1827, both English and American Protestant Bible societies took a stand against their inclusion. 

Following is a list of the "Apocrypha" of the Old Testament: The 

Book of Tobias (Tobit), the Book of Judith, additions to the Book of Esther, the Book of Wisdom (the Wisdom of Solomon), Ecclesiasticus (the 

Wisdom of Sirach), the Book of Baruch, the Story of Susanna, the Song of the Three Children, the Story of Baal and the Dragon (Bel), the 

First Book of the Machabees (Maccabees), the Second Book of the 

Machabees (Maccabees). Catholics do not refer to these works as 

Apocrypha, but as Deuterocanonical. Real Apocrypha are works that were rejected from the canon of Scripture by authority of the Catholic Church. 

The full books involved in the list above are Tobias, Judith, Wisdom, *The concordance, prepared by Alexander Cruden, was first published in 1737. 

Two other editions were issued by him in his lifetime. There are about 220,000 references.

PAGE Ecclesiasticus, Baruch, First and Second Machabees; the portions are verse 

4 of chapter x to the end of Esther, verse 24 of chapter iii to verse 3 of chapter iv of Daniel, and chapters xiii and xiv of Daniel. 

The Deuterocanonical sections of the Old Testament are so called because their place in the canon was not admitted by the Catholic Church until after acceptance of the other books. Although there was some controversy over them until the Church decided the question, they are now regarded by us as on an equal standing with the other parts of the canon. All the Eastern dissident Churches, some of which have been separated from Rome since the fifth century, admit them, although Greek and Russian theologians, influenced probably by Protestantism, have now for some time questioned them.

These books are important for a number of reasons. Ecclesiasticus (chapter xxvii) puts a ban on modern sects that scoff at physicians by advising treatment from doctors when ill; and II Machabees (xii, 43) upholds the value of prayers for the dead and thereby proves the Catholic doctrine of purgatory.

In the time of Christ, two canons of the Scripture were accepted by the Jews. The Palestinian Jews accepted the collection that Protestants admit today. The Alexandrian Jews possessed Greek copies of the Scripture known as LXX (Septuagint)—the Old Testament canon that Catholics accept. 

The claim is made that the prophet Esdras closed the Jewish canon of the Scriptures, and that his collection is that of the Palestinian Jews. But no genuine proof can be offered that he intended to do anything more than make a list of the canonical books written up to his own day. There is nothing to substantiate the claim of some Protestant writers that he had Divine authority to decide that nothing more inspired would be written for the Old Testament. How could he know that another prophet would never arise? The Bible attributes to him no such inkling.

The Jews today accept the same canon as the Protestants. But they did not end their controversy over which was the real canon until 90 A. D. To claim that their action was right would be to assert that the post-Christ Jewish Church is infallible-something to which no Christian can subscribe. 

Out of, say 350 quotations from the Old Testament in the New, 300 are from the Alexandrian version, or LXX. Jesus Christ Himself used it. 

Clement of Rome, Polycarp, Irenaeus, Tertullian, Clement of Alexandria, and other early Christian writers quoted the LXX as inspired. 

There is only one exception in the first three centuries of Christianity among the writers of the Church on the inspiration of the LXX. 

St. Augustine, who lived in the fifth century (August, Ep. 28, apud 

Reusch), spoke of the LXX as approved by the Apostles. 

Just as in the Old Testament, the New Testament has books that are accepted by Catholics and were rejected by some others. These are St. Paul's 

Epistle to the Hebrews, the Second Epistle of St. Peter, and Second and 

Third Epistles of St. James, the Epistle of St. Jude, and the Apocalypse of 

St. John. The division is not so complete, however, as in the Old Testament controversy. The disputed books are important. The Sacrament of Extreme 

Unction rests on the Epistle of St. James, which was thrown out by Martin 

Luther as an epistle of straw because it disproved his theory that good works

PAGE 

are not necessary to salvation, faith sufficing. Modern Protestants accept the same New Testament books as we do. 

We have proved from undisputed books that the Church has the Divine right to teach and is guided by the Holy Ghost. Since she has determined that the questioned books are part of the New Testament, we hold that this settles the matter. In regard to the controversy about who has the power to interpret the 

Bible, we turn to the Good Book itself. II Peter i, 20, says: "Understanding this first, that no prophecy of Scripture is made by private interpretation.' 

The Challoner footnote speaks thus of that text: "This plainly shows that the Scriptures are not to be expounded by any one's private judgment or private spirit, because every part of the Scripture was written by men inspired by the Holy Ghost, and declared as such by the Church; therefore they are not to be interpreted but by the Spirit of God, which He hath left, and promised to remain with His Church to guide her in all truth to the end of the world. Some may tell us that many of our divines interpret the 

Scripture; they may do so, but they always do it with a submission to the judgment of the Church and not otherwise." 

If the Bible were to be left simply to individuals to care for or interpret, we would often be in a sorry way. The following instances of mistakes that have occurred can be cited as proof of this: 

In Cor. ix, 5, St. Paul says: "Have we not power to carry about a woman, a sister, as well as the rest of the Apostles, and the brethren of the Lord, and Cephas?" He is referring to devout women who, according to the custom of the Jewish nation, waited on the preachers of the Gospel and supplied them with necessities. But some Protestant translators have made the text read, "a sister, a wife," whereas I Cor. vii, 8, declares Paul was not married.

In I Cor. xi, 27, St. Paul says: "Therefore whosoever shall eat this bread, or drink the chalice of the Lord unworthily, shall be guilty of the body and blood of the Lord." Some translators corrupted the "or" into "and," thus destroying one of the Scriptural proofs that Communion could be received under one form only. Late Protestant Bibles, such as the Goodspeed, Moffatt, and Twentieth Century translations, all use "or." 

In Heb. xi, 21, St. Paul says: "By faith Jacob, dying, blessed each of the sons of Joseph, and adored the top of his rod." Jacob paid this relative honor to the top of Joseph's rod, as a figure of Christ's scepter and kingdom, just as Catholics venerate relics of the saints. But this practice did not suit certain non-Catholic translators, so they corrupted the text to read, "He, who worshipped leaning upon the top of his staff." 

It is because of private interpretation that Protestantism has split into hundreds of sects in our own day. All of them cannot be true, for they teach everything under the sun. If God offered no better way than this. for man to arrive at the truths necessary for his salvation, He would be strangely indifferent towards us.

Protestantism does not insist much on the lesson taught by St. Peter in II Pet. iii, 16. After telling about the writings of St. Paul, he goes on to say that in them "are certain things hard to be understood, which the unlearned and the unstable wrest, as they do also the other Scriptures, to their own destruction."

PAGE 

WHAT TRADITION MEANS XIX WHY IT IS ON SAME BASIS AS THE BIBLE 

All the teachings of Christianity are not contained in the Bible. The 

Good Book distinctly states this fact. Catholics, in addition to the Bible, also accept Tradition, which consists of the truths of Divine revelation made known by Jesus Christ and the Holy Ghost to the Apostles and handed down to us in the writings of the Fathers and Doctors of the Church, in the decrees of councils, in the definitions of the Holy See of Rome, and in the creeds and prayers of the Church. It is really the voice of the living Church. 

In its broadest sense the term Fathers of the Church denotes all those Christian writers of the first twelve centuries who are considered by general consent to be among the most eminent witnesses and teachers of the orthodox and Catholic teaching of Christianity. This definition would include such men as Tertullian, who, despite the fact that he left us some of the most valuable works we have, himself left the Church and fell into heresy. In its stricter sense, the term Fathers of the Church refers to those great writers of the first twelve centuries whose works were strictly Catholic and whose own lives were in keeping with their writings. Some of these works are very ancient. St. Clement of Rome lived in the first century; SS. Ignatius, Justin, and Irenaeus wrote in the second, etc. The books of these men are distinctly Catholic.

To be a Doctor of the Church, a man must show in his writings such learning that through them "the darkness of error was scattered, dark things were made clear, doubts resolved, the difficulties of Scripture opened" (Benedict XIV); the man must be heroic in sanctity and (although this last condition has not always been insisted upon) the title must be conferred by the Pope or a General Council. This title must not be confused with 

Doctor of Sacred Theology or the other degrees given to students. St. Thomas 

Aquinas, St. Alphonsus Liguori, St. Francis de Sales, and men of this type are Doctors of the Church.

Not a single Protestant Church in existence today teaches the pure doctrines of Martin Luther or John Calvin, the chief founders of Protestantism. But Catholicity of today is found to coincide with the teachings of our great writers in all the centuries since Christ. 

A General Council of the Church is the assembly of Bishops and other chief rulers called together by the Pope and presided over by him or his legate to decide questions of faith, morals, or discipline for the Church. 

The decrees of General Councils, approved by the Pope, are infallible in matters pertaining to faith and morals. 

There have been twenty General Councils in the Church since Christ. 

A council of the Apostles, described in the New Testament itself, was held at Jerusalem in A. D. 51. The first General or Ecumenical Council was that of Nice, in 325, which condemned the Arian heresy, and the last was the Vatican Council, which met in 1869-1870. This latter council has not been called together since 1870, but it has not yet been dismissed. 

The Church is careful about her prayers, and all our prayer books must be issued with ecclesiastical authority, to keep out false doctrine. Christ declared that "he who believeth not shall be condemned." Prayer books

PAGE 

are like law books; there is teaching on every page. If a state allowed lawyers to follow whatever law books they wished, without any interference by the court, chaos would result.

Having studied the sources of Tradition, let us learn why we must accept it.

We know from Holy Writ that the Church has Tradition in addition to the Written Word. "Hold the form of sound words, which thou hast heard of me in faith, and in the love which is in Jesus Christ," said St. Paul (II Tim. i, 13), thus showing that the Church was to remember what it had heard, not only what it might read. Still stronger is the following from II Tim. ii, 2, "And the things which thou hast heard of me by many witnesses, the same commend to faithful men, who shall be fit to teach others also." That both the written and the heard word are to be followed is set forth by St. Paul in II Thess. ii, 14: "Therefore, brethren, stand fast; and hold the traditions which you have learned, whether by word, or by our epistle." Tradition is again specifically mentioned by him in II Thess. iii, 6: "And we charge you, brethren, in the name of Our Lord Jesus Christ, that you withdraw yourselves from every brother walking disorderly, and not according to the tradition which they have received from us." St. John's 

Gospel closes with this significant verse: "But there are also many other things which Jesus did; which, if they were written every one, the world itself, I think, would not be able to contain the books that should be written." 

The Church, it must be remembered, existed for many years before all the New Testament was written, and for six years before any of it was penned. It was the Church that had to separate the genuine Bible from the apocryphal books. If the Church were living and able to do such things in the early centuries, why should she not be considered the living voice of Christ now? Tradition is older than the Bible and, as it is accepted by the 

Church on exactly the same grounds as the Scriptures, and, as the Church herself declared what constituted the Bible, it is hard to see why some modern Christians accept the Bible, but reject Tradition. To be safe, mankind must accept both.

PAGE XX 

THE CHURCH OF CHRIST-THE PAPACY 

If Christ came on earth and suffered and died for men, it stands to reason that He established a definite means for bringing the effects of the Redemption to us. He is Divine, and it is foolish to think that He would be less wise than the average wise man. If a wise man wanted to start a great movement, he would naturally found a society to carry it along after his death.

Christ instituted the Catholic Church and the sacraments to enable men at all times to share in the fruits of the redemption. The Church is the congregation of all who profess the faith of Christ, partake of the same sacraments, and are governed by their lawful pastors under one visible head, the Pope.

Jesus Christ is the invisible head of the Church, and the Pope, the visible head, is Christ's Vicar on earth. The Pope is the visible head of the 

Church because he is the Bishop of Rome and the successor to St. Peter, whom Christ made the visible head of the Church. 

Christ made Peter chief of the Apostles, but all the Apostles were made Bishops. Our Bishops are the successors of the Apostles. The Church was founded to teach, to govern spiritually, to sanctify, and to save all men. 

All are bound to belong to the Church. Any one who knows it to be the true Church, and remains outside, cannot be saved. The only hope of salvation without maintaining visible connection with Catholicity lies in sincerely believing one's false position to be right, and in having virtue or perfect contrition for sins or perfect love of God. 

Christ established a Church that He promised would have Divine assistance and it spread through a pagan world that was at utter variance with it. Lust was open and unashamed in the civilization of paganism. 

Christianity spread despite its demand that men practice self-denial. Our religion is so opposed to the spirit of worldly pleasure or expediency that it could not have done this except miraculously. Hundreds of thousands of early martyrs showed that they preferred to suffer and die rather than to give up following the Cross of Christ. The Church also, in early times, was gifted with miracle working in large numbers of her clergy and laity, and thus had the undeniable stamp of God on her. This gift lasted as long as the young tree of Christianity needed special cultivation by the Divine Gardener. It has never completely disappeared. 

Shortly after the establishment of the Church, writers began to compile the New Testament, under Divine inspiration. We go chiefly to it for our historical arguments for the proof of the Divine character of Christianity, but we must remember that it is not our sole historical record, and we must also remember that the Church was spread all over the civilized world and functioning as a powerful force in public life before it was officially decided what was and what was not really part of the New Testament. Even if there were no Bible, the Church would be in existence today and have powerful proofs of her Divine mission. 

Before and after the writing of the New Testament, it was consistently claimed that the Church was Divinely guided. If God is with the Church,

PAGE 

reason, therefore, demands that it be consistent in its doctrine; that its teachings about faith and morals be not matters of mere expediency, altered to fit certain ages and nations, but something that will lift men up, not descend to the level of their corrupt nature. Put to this test, one Church and only one is God's. That is the Papal or Catholic Church. Every student of history will have to agree that no other denomination on the earth stands adamant, refusing to budge an inch to the special sinful spirit of each age or nation. The doctrine of the Roman Church today is just exactly what it was in the sixteenth century, again in the tenth, again in the first. Every other denomination changes its teaching from time to time. There is only one religion that is ever ancient, ever new, like the God who made it. 

Rome has proved time and again in her history that, rather than sacrifice one iota of her revealed truth, she will permit entire nations and some of her most powerful cleric and lay leaders to withdraw from her communion. If you study history, you must admit these facts. 

We can place the date, without difficulty, for the foundation of every single Christian denomination. There is only one that can trace itself in an unbroken line back to Christ. This is the Papal Church. Every other denomination, whether schismatic, Protestant, or what-not, is an off-shoot of her or of her off-shoots. The historical argument for Catholicity is impregnable.

Many texts can be cited to prove that Christ established a definite Church organization. Time and again, the Epistles set forth that the Church is His mystical body. It is a body with Him as the head, ourselves as the members. Time and again, the Epistles, the Apocalypse, and other portions of the New Testament insist that there is only one system of religious truth and that to teach otherwise will bring damnation. The Apostles were utterly intolerant in their condemnation of false doctrine. There is no room for the heresy that "one Church is as good as another." 

Just before Christ ascended into heaven, He commanded His Apostles to go and to evangelize all nations, "teaching them to observe ALL things whatsoever I have commanded you; and behold, I am with you all days, even to the consummation of the world" (Matt. xxviii, 20). Previously, He had told them: "As the Father sent Me, I also send you" (John xx, 21).

Again He told them that the ecclesiastical power they would exercise would be as binding in heaven as it would be on earth: "Amen I say to you, whatsoever you shall bind upon earth, it shall be bound also in heaven; and whatsoever you shall loose upon earth, it shall be loosed also in heaven" (Matt. xvii, 18). Again He made it emphatic that the voice of the Church would be God's voice in the world: "He that heareth you, heareth Me; and he that despiseth you, despiseth Me" (Luke x, 16).

He named Peter as the head of His Church: "Thou art Peter"-i.e., a rock, for Peter means Rock-"and upon this rock I will build My Church, and the gates of hell shall not prevail against it." Here we have the definite promise of Christ that Peter was to be the cornerstone of the Church and that it would be protected against the wiles of hell in its teaching of faith and morals. Christ continued His address to Peter, saying: "And I will give to thee the keys of the kingdom of heaven. And whatsoever thou

PAGE shalt bind upon earth, it shall be bound also in heaven; and whatsoever thou shalt loose on earth, it shall be loosed also in heaven" (Matt. xvi, 19).*

The figure of speech employed by the Master in giving Peter the keys of heaven was common and is still used by us today when we are giving a prominent visitor the "keys to the city." Peter was given actual power that would be recognized in the city of heaven. Christ made this ultra plain when He told Peter that what he bound on earth would be bound in heaven and what he loosed on earth would be loosed in heaven. Again Christ told Peter that Satan had desired to sift him as wheat, but He had prayed for him that his faith should not fail and that he, being converted, should confirm his brethren. (See Luke xxii.) Again Christ said to Peter: "Feed

My lambs, feed My sheep" (Matt. xxi, 15, 17), and thereby gave him jurisdiction over both the clergy and the laity. 

These texts are irrefutable proof from the Scriptures that Jesus Christ selected St. Peter as the earthly head of the Church, with the promise that what he taught would be guaranteed in heaven, and that he would be guarded from teaching what was not according to the mind of Christ. The 

Pope is, therefore, personally infallible. But we are to understand this infallibility as the Divinely guided Church itself has always interpreted the doctrine. The infallibility of the Pope, according to the Church, extends only to matters of faith and morals when he speaks ex cathedra, i.e., as the universal shepherd of the Christian Church. He can invent no new doctrine. His power extends only to determining what was in the Apostolic deposit of faith.

In conversation, writing, or even in preaching, if he were not teaching as the Universal Shepherd of the Church, the Pope could make a mistake in either faith or morals, the same as any other priest. 

Dr. Deharbe's Catechism says (page 134): "Christ, it is true, made His 

Apostles collectively the foundation of His Church and gave them all collectively the power of binding and loosing, and of governing the Church; *George M. Lamsa, a Protestant Episcopalian, who was Assyrian born and reared, translated the Gospels from the Aramaic, the language that Christ actually spoke. He used the Peschitto or Syriac version, which, according to Catholic scholars, appeared in the second, third, and fourth centuries. It is still used in all the Syrian churches, its latest Catholic edition being that issued by the Dominicans from the Chaldean seminary at Mosul in 1891. Particularly interesting is Lamsa's translation of the text about the Church's being built on St. Peter. It reads: "I tell you also that you are a stone, and upon this stone I will build My Church; and the doors of Sheol shall not shut in on it. I will give you the keys of the kingdom of heaven; and whatever you bind on earth shall be bound in heaven, and whatever you release on earth shall be released in heaven" (The Gospels According to the Eastern Version, A. J. Holman Co., published 1933. Pages 33-34).

Another interesting Protestant translation is from the Greek, The New Testament, an American Translation, by Edgar J. Goodspeed, University of Chicago 

Press, pub. 1923 (see page 33). It follows: "But I tell you, your name is Peter, a rock, and on this rock I will build My Church, and the powers of death shall not subdue it. I will give you the keys of the kingdom of heaven, and whatever you forbid on earth will be held in heaven to be forbidden, and whatever you permit on earth will be held in heaven to be permitted." 

James Moffat's Protestant translation from the Greek, published by George H. 

Doran Co. in 1922, calls attention (p. 42) to the play "on the Greek word for rock," not usually brought out in English. The French version, which he quotes, reproduces it.

These translations, all late, effectively demolish the old-time Protestant contention that Christ did not refer to Peter in person.

PAGE 

but what He promised and gave to the Apostles in common, this He first promised and gave to Peter in particular. Thus Peter received the full and independent, the Apostles, on the contrary, only a subordinate power." 

Nobody can logically hold that Christ intended the office of head of the Church to die with Peter. The Master said that His Church would last until the end of time. Peter died as Bishop of Rome and his successors in that office have always been the recognized center of Christendom. Even when the Apostle John was living, the people of Corinth sent a knotty problem to Rome for settlement, instead of submitting it to the closer see city of the Beloved Disciple. The historic argument for the Papacy is overwhelming. Readers will find it succinctly presented in the late Cardinal Gibbons' 

Faith of Our Fathers. 

We know from the early writings of the Church that the Papacy has been accepted in every age as the center of Christianity. 

Those who pretend that the Primacy of Rome was a gradual growth have merely their unproved assertions to substantiate them. All history proves that they are wrong.

The external power of the Papacy has had a gradual growth, but there has been no gradual usurpation of purely teaching authority, such as is sometimes charged. No nation, for instance, in Peter's time ever thought of sending a diplomatic envoy to the Vatican, but in our own age we have seen even pagan Japan doing this (in First World war days). 

We have already proved by Scriptural references that the Papacy is of Divine institution. The texts we have given not only prove the authority of the Pope, but also substantiate our belief that he is infallible when defining doctrines of faith and morals for the universal Church. The Pope cannot err under such circumstances. Understand, we do not claim that he can introduce new truths. He merely defines that such and such a doctrine is or is not in the deposit of faith we have received from the Apostles. And his definition is not Divinely guarded against error unless he formally speaks as the chief pastor of the Christian world. In ordinary conversation, he could be as mistaken as anybody else.

We also do not claim that he is unable to sin. Every Pope has his 

Father Confessor and goes to Confession regularly. 

We do not regard the Pope as the sole head of the Church. Jesus 

Christ is its invisible head, and, following the teaching of St. Paul and the other Apostles set forth in the Bible, we regard the Church as the mystical body of Christ, with Him as its head and ourselves as its members. We are flesh of His flesh, bone of His bone, so close is the spiritual union.* 

The Pope is the head of the Church merely as the Vicar of Christ and His personal representative on earth. The Pope has this position through being the Bishop of Rome and the successor of St. Peter, who was chosen as the chief of the Apostles of Christ. The Pope has been elected in different ways at different times, but in the main the system used today, i.e., his selection by the chief clergy of Rome or by what are now called Cardinals, has been always employed. It is not how he is chosen, but the fact that he is genuinely put into the position of Bishop of Rome, that makes him Pope. *"Because we are members of His body, of His flesh, and of His bones" (Ephesians v, 30).

PAGE Christ did not definitely set forth how the successors of St. Peter were to be picked out.

What are we to say, however, about those periods when there was more than one Pope? Were there several successors of St. Peter reigning concurrently? Our answer is that there has always been only one true Pope. 

Some people have been mistaken in deciding who this was, but that had nothing to do with whether the man was or was not Pope. The fact that a man claimed to be Pope could not make him one, even though he might have a distinguished following.

Also, what are we to say about those cases in history when weak men or even sinful men have reigned as Pope? Could a sinner head Christ's 

Church? Our answer here is that you will search the Scriptures or Tradition in vain for any promise from God that He would protect the Church to this extent. It is possible for a bad man to become Pope. But it is difficult, because of the care exercised, and in few cases has it happened. God gave men reason and prudence, and expects them to use their brains. If they will not, they must suffer the consequences, in the Church or out of it. It can be said, however, without fear of contradiction, that the saintliest line of men traceable in all human history is that of the Popes; and it can also be shown that not a single proof can be alleged of a case when a Pope was able to use his office for false teaching to the universal Church in faith or morals.

There is not a single text in either the New or the Old Testament to sustain the idea that sin in itself robs hierarchs of the authority conferred by ordination. But this question need worry us little, for any unprejudiced student of history will find that the Catholic Church has always had a majority of good men in its clergy.

PAGE 

 XXI GOVERNMENTAL SYSTEM OF THE CATHOLIC CHURCH EXPLAINED 

The constitution of the Catholic Church makes it the most perfect organization on earth, but its government is really simple. At the top stands the Pope as the earthly representative of Jesus Christ, who is the head of the Church. The Pope is the supreme executive, legislator, and judge. As the Manual of Christian Doctrine of the Christian Brothers points out, it belongs to the Pope: "1. To define all that Christ has prescribed to be believed, to be done, and to be avoided, in order to attain heaven; 2. To point out and condemn all errors contrary to revelation."

He is "the principal doctor, guardian, and defender of revealed truth." 

Next to the Pope in dignity stand the Cardinals. They are advisers of the Pope, preside over the various Sacred Congregations that look after the work of the Church (such as the Congregation of Rites or that of the Propagation of the Faith), dispatch pressing business when the Papacy is vacant, and have the exclusive right of electing the Pope, who is usually taken from their ranks. Often Cardinals are the incumbents of important 

Bishoprics, although it is not necessary to be a Bishop in order to be a Cardinal. The Cardinalate is, in some sense, an honorary rank that has developed in the Church. But since the Apostles, before separating and taking over sees, worked close to Peter, it can be held that the rank has been in the Church from the beginning. The men immediately around the 

Pope were not always called Cardinals; but Peter and his successors always had such aides. Paul was a Cardinal, in the strict sense of the word. 

In exactly the same rank with the Pope, so far as the sacrament of Holy Orders is concerned, stand the Bishops. He is their superior in jurisdiction, but not sacramentally. The Episcopacy has the plentitude of the priesthood; its possessor has by right of ordination the full power with which Christ endowed His shepherds. An ordinary priest's power is limited in comparison with that of a Bishop. For instance, the Bishop can ordain other priests, while an ordinary priest connot. 

Most Bishops have dioceses, i.e., Cathedral cities with surrounding territory over which they exercise spiritual jurisdiction.
