But it is possible to be a titular Bishop, i.e., one without actual jurisdiction, such a Bishop's see being located in a place that was once important but is no longer so, only the name of the old see being preserved. A Bishop takes his title from his see, i.e., the city of his Cathedral; as Bishop of Pittsburgh. 

Some Bishops have greater jurisdiction than others, and hence are called Archbishops or Metropolitans. They head what are called provinces, usually consisting of several dioceses, and those Bishops who are under them are called their suffragans. The Archbishop's power over his suffragans is now quite limited. Some Archbishops are called Primates or Patriarchs. 

A Primate has some jurisdiction over all the Archbishops and Bishops of a country. Often the rank is purely an honorary one (as in the case of the late Cardinal Gibbons, Archbishop of Baltimore). Patriarchs have jurisdiction over Archbishops and Primates, usually in a vast territory, or head

PAGE rites other than the Latin. This position was far more important in earlier ages, when communication with Rome was difficult. Sometimes the title is honorary.

Under the Bishops, we find the priests. Some of the priests are Monsignors, a purely honorary rank, given by the Pope because of distinguished service, and going back to the notaries named by St. Clement (Pope, martyred 

100 A. D.) to collect the acts of the martyrs. There are several grades of these prelates, some known as Right Reverend and some as Very Reverend.* 

In ecclesiastical functions, a Monsignor is always shown greater honor than an ordinary priest. In a diocese, the ranking official next to the Bishop is his Vicar General, whom he himself selects. This official has many powers, such as the granting of dispensations or of the right to proceed with certain marriages ordinarily prohibited by the Church (when there is good reason to set aside the law). High in power in the diocese ranks the Chancellor, who keeps the diocesan records and also usually has the right to dispense in marriage cases. The Bishop likewise has consultors and other priest officials to help him in the work of the diocese. 

Although Canons are rare in the United States, these officials exist in many countries. They are equivalent to American diocesan consultors but usually have distinctive ecclesiastical dress, are bound to sing the Divine Office in common in the Cathedral, to assist in the government of the diocese, and to reside in the see city. Like our diocesan consultors, they name the administrator to govern a see during its vacancy. In some countries they may suggest three names for the vacant Bishopric. In dioceses where there is no income established for their support as Canons, their choir and residential duties are limited. In some ancient dioceses, they have unusual privileges, such as the right to wear prelatial rings and pectoral crosses and to pontificate at Mass. Honorary Canons are sometimes appointed. 

The chief work of the priests is in the parishes, which are certain territorial divisions, each with a pastor in charge, assisted, if needs be, by other priests. People are encouraged to go to their own parish churches and the Canon Law (as the law of the Church is called) makes it necessary to get the proper pastor's consent before another priest can officiate at various functions for the people. A number of priests are also employed as teachers, as editors, as chaplains in institutions, etc. 

The priests of the Church are divided into seculars and religious. The seculars are the ordinary clergy owing obedience only to the Bishop and his superiors. The regulars are so called because they live by approved rules in communities (Latin, regula, rule). Some of these communities are bound by vows, some by promises. These priests turn their income into a common fund and must give obedience in all that is not sin to their superior. The superiors of some communities are called Abbots, who have the right to use pontificalia (mitre and crozier, etc.) like a Bishop and who have other quasi-Episcopal prerogatives. Bishops are consecrated, and *Monsignorial_ranks are given not only by the Pope, but also in the Oriental rites. Some Oriental appointments call for an ordination service, which is regarded as a sacramental rather than as part of the sacrament of Holy Orders. Chorbishops, Archimandrites, and Vartapeds, for instance, are thus ordained.

PAGE 

the act of consecrating them is the conferring of a sacrament; Abbots are blessed, and their benediction is a sacramental. 

Abbots are occasionally given territory around their abbeys, which they govern like Bishops. One such district exists in the United States (the 

Abbatia Nullius, Mary Help of Christians, Belmont, North Carolina). The 

Abbot-Ordinary may confirm, consecrate churches and altars, confer tonsure and Minor Orders even on seculars, etc.; but he cannot ordain to Major 

Orders. If such an Abbot-Ordinary were also a Bishop, of course he could ordain to all Orders.

Whether they are members of orders or not, all priests have exactly the same power, so far as their sacerdotal office is concerned. 

New orders are founded from time to time in the Church to look after certain works or to encourage new holiness of life. Women live in communities with the vows of chastity, obedience, and poverty (sometimes adding another vow or two according to the rule of the order). They are known as nuns or sisters. There are many different orders, particularly among the women, and they, receiving little earthly reward, giving themselves without remuneration except for a bare living, do valiant service for 

God and man.

Finally we come to the laity. The position of our laity is high. In a manner, they participate in the priesthood of Jesus Christ, and they are the backbone of the Church.* There are many societies, sodalities, confraternities, orders, etc., by aid of which a person can live a more holy life while remaining in the world.

The Pope has the full right of appointment of Cardinals, Patriarchs, Primates, Archbishops, and Bishops, and, while others may be given certain veto or advisory privileges, it must always be understood that the Pope is not absolutely bound by them. Bishops have the right of appointment of pastors and other priests in their dioceses. But in case of injustice, there is always the right of appeal open to him who feels himself misused. The law of the Church is such as to give plenty of protection to priests or others. 

While to outsiders the Church may look like an autocracy, the individual has an amount of liberty never realized in man-made governments. 

Occasionally, the Pope calls General Councils of the Church, consisting of Cardinals, Bishops, Abbots, etc., to discuss disciplinary affairs or matters of faith. When the Pope calls such a council and confirms its decreesand only then-it is infallible, i.e., it cannot err in teaching matters of faith and morals.

Where the Church is still in a missionary stage, sometimes priests are given jurisdiction over others, without being consecrated Bishops, and sometimes they are consecrated only as titular Bishops, not having as complete power as they would in a regularly constituted diocese. Sometimes priests who are not consecrated Bishops are given a Monsignorial dignity, with powers of jurisdiction over mission territories. Occasionally one hears of *This participation is more than merely passive. The Baptismal and Confirmation characters sanctify man for the active performance of those most general acts of worship that belong to the vocation of a child of God, fit him to take an active part in the building up of the temple of God, and equip him for the apostolate (Pesch, Praelectiones Dogmaticae, vol. vi, par. 196.)

PAGE an "independent mission," with only an ordinary priest over it who is not even a Monsignor, but this is usually just a temporary arrangement. 

The Pope is kept right by remembering that he has a Superior in heaven who will demand a strict accounting. And the Church is kept in line by the Pope. The system works out with far greater justice to the individual than any temporal government. 

The Pope frequently sends personal representatives to act in his name in stipulated matters in various nations; for instance, the United States has its Apostolic Delegate, who is a titular Archbishop. 

Bishops of large sees or even of small ones where the head of the diocese is in frail health often have auxiliary and coadjutor Bishops, who are titular Bishops, able by their consecration to assist with Confirmations, Ordinations, etc.

PAGE 

 XXII PROMISES OF GOD FULFILLED IN ATTRIBUTES OF CHURCH 

The Catholic Church has authority, infallibility, and indefectibility. 

These are called her attributes.

By her authority, we mean the right and power she has to teach and to govern the faithful. The members of her episcopate are the successors of the Apostles, to whom Christ said: "All power is given to Me in heaven and in earth. Going, therefore, teach ye all nations; baptizing them in the name of the Father, and of the Son, and of the Holy Ghost. Teaching them to observe all things whatsoever I have commanded you; and behold 

I am with you all days, even to the consummation of the world" (Matt. xxviii, 19, 20). This command of Christ, as it states, lasts until the end of time; but the Apostles died a few years after Him; therefore, we must conclude that there are men alive today who are just as much commissioned to represent Him as they were. These men are the Catholic Bishops, whose 

Holy Orders extend in a straight line to the Apostles. 

By the infallibility of the Church we mean that she cannot err in formally teaching a doctrine of faith or morals. This formal infallible teaching occurs when the Church speaks through the Pope and Bishops united in General Council, or through the Pope alone when he proclaims to all the faithful a doctrine of faith or morals. St. Paul in I Tim. iii, 15, speaks of "the Church of the living God, the pillar and ground of the truth." If she is this, it is therefore impossible for her ever to uphold error, or to bring in corruption, superstition, or idolatry. Her children, even those in high positions, might sin, but she can never go on record as a body for an error. She never changes her teaching, for, since she is the pillar and ground of the truth, she cannot change.

That the Church is ever the faithful spouse of Christ, is shown by St. 

Paul in Eph. v: "Christ is the head of the Church. He is the savior of His body. Therefore, as the Church is subject to Christ, so also let wives be to their husbands in all things. Husbands, love your wives, as Christ also loved His Church, and delivered Himself up for it: that He might sanctify it, cleansing it by the laver of water in the word of life; that He might present to Himself a glorious Church, not having spot or wrinkle, or any such thing; but that it should be holy and without blemish." If the Church could teach false doctrine, or depart as a whole from Christ's teachings, how could she be said to be without blemish?

The Holy Ghost is with the Church "forever." Therefore, she cannot err. In John xiv, 16, Christ says: "And I will ask the Father and He shall give you another Paraclete, that He may abide with you forever."

The Old Testament definitely promised that Christ's Church would be fool-proof: "And a path and a way shall be there, and it shall be called the holy way: the unclean shall not pass over it, and this shall be unto you a straight way, so that fools shall not err therein" (Isaias xxxv, 8). 

Isaias lix, 20, 21, also foretells that never is God's truth to depart from the Church of Christ. "And there shall come a Redeemer to Sion, and to them that return from iniquity in Jacob, saith the Lord: My spirit that is with thee, and My words that I have put in thy mouth shall not

PAGE depart out of thy mouth, nor out of the mouth of thy seed, nor out of the mouth of thy seed's seed, saith the Lord, from henceforth and forever."

So we might go on multiplying texts in proof of our argument. It is impossible to accept Holy Writ and contend that a "general apostasy" from truth has ever occurred in the Christian Church. 

When we speak of the indefectibility of the Church, we mean that she will last till the end of time. The text of Isaias quoted above proves this. The promise of Christ that the Paraclete He would send would abide with us forever (John xiv, 16) proves it. Christ's promise that He is with us "all days, even to the consummation of the world" (Matt. xxviii, 20) proves it. Other texts prove it.

The gifts of infallibility and indefectibility are found all through the Church. If Christ were not with the members as a body, they could not remain so faithful. Individuals may err, but the Church as a whole cannot, and, like Christ, Catholicity as a body defies the world to convict her of a single sin!

Although our Church never changes her doctrine, nevertheless it is true that age after age she better understands some of the truths left in the deposit of faith given to the Apostles. This better understanding, we term the development of doctrine, magnificently explained by John Henry Cardinal 

Newman in his book on The Development of Christian Doctrine. The mustard seed, as foretold by Our Lord in a parable, becomes in time a great tree, and the birds of the air rest in its branches.

PAGE 

XXIII 

CHURCH HAS FOUR MARKS THAT PROVE HER DIVINITY 

The true fold of Jesus Christ has certain marks that distinguish her from all other organizations claiming to be His Church. She is one, holy, catholic, and apostolic.

The unity of the true Church is one of the most conspicuous doctrines in the New Testament. Various times, the Church is compared in the 

Epistles to a body, with Christ as the head, ourselves as the members. Time. and again, the Apostles inveigh against any innovation in doctrine. They want what they taught to be believed and not something that is the invention of unauthorized agents. Even if an angel from heaven, declared St. Paul, should teach another doctrine than the apostolic one, let him be anathema.* 

This Apostle spoke of "one faith, one Baptism, one God and Father of all" (Ephes. iv, 5, 6). Christ said: "Upon this rock I will build My Church," designating Peter, and the word Church is in the singular. "There shall be one fold and one shepherd," said Christ (John x, 16), and He taught that any one who tries to enter His sheepfold except through the one door is a thief and a robber.

If we cut off the Head of the Church, we cannot expect the life of His mystical body to come down to us, and if He has designated the Pope as His representative on earth and we refuse to acknowledge the Pope, we cannot be in communication with Christ. It "He that will not hear the Church, let him be to thee as the heathen and the publican," warned Christ (Matt. xviii, 17). Christ Himself taught that no kingdom can stand that is divided against itself (Luke xi, 17). is absurd to believe that He, God Almighty, should be pleased to call everything His Church that pretends to be so, regardless of what it teaches. His and the Apostles' warning that faith is essential to salvation would then be a hoax.

The Church is holy because her Founder is holy and her purpose is to lead all men to holiness of life. The lives of her canonized saints are proof that she has this mark in an eminent degree. Many Protestants think that non-Catholics lead as good and holy lives-if not better than the majority of Catholics. In this, we disagree. 

In this, we disagree. The writer's position has been such that he knows both, thoroughly. There is simply no question about the fact that Catholic lives are on the higher plane, generally speaking. We court investigation of this fact. Orestes Brownson, great convert who had been a 

Protestant minister, never tired of emphasizing that Protestants have no conception of Catholic spirituality.

The denominations that split from Rome at the time of the Reformation have made an historical confession that they are not as holy. Before the 

Reformation, many Christians were declared saints, and many of the Protestants today accept every one of those declared saints by our Church before the Lutheran revolt. But while, since the Reformation, our Church has raised scores of others to sainthood, Protestantism has never dared to do so with one of its heroes. This is its own confession of spiritual poverty. 

We do not wish here to be understood as implying that many Protestants *"But, though we, or an angel from heaven, preach a gospel to you besides that which we have preached to you, let him be anathema” (Gal. i, 8).

PAGE do not lead good lives or that they have not had many sincere leaders. Our only purpose is to prove that, when you come down to the brass tacks, the Catholic Church stands so far ahead that there is no comparison. By their fruits ye shall know them.

When Catholics fall into serious sin, it is always as a result of going adversely to the tenets of their Church. But can this be said of others? 

The polygamy, for instance, of the early Mormons was in keeping with the doctrines of that denomination. Today there is hardly a Protestant denomination that does not officially wink at the most radical denials of faith, despite the Apostolic doctrine that faith is essential. Always remember this, that a God who is Holiness itself cannot possibly alter truth to suit expediency or the popular whim.

The Church is not only one and holy but she is also catholic and apostolic. By her apostolicity, we mean that she can trace herself back in an unbroken line to the Apostles, teaching the same doctrine as they did, and having her rulers, the Pope and Bishops, as their lawful successors, with the same sacrament of Holy Orders. She is catholic, i.e., universal, because she can perfectly fit every nation and every age without substantial change and is truly the one universal religion. She is genuine Christianity. 

The divisions that have split from her have some part of Christianity, but they have as much of man's invention mixed in them as they have of Christ. 

And the boat of Christ does not need more than one pilot. We cannot travel in all directions and still land in the same port. "The Church," says the Baltimore Catechism, "is made and kept one, holy, and catholic by the Holy Ghost, the Spirit of love and holiness, who unites and sanctifies its members throughout the world."

PAGE 

XXIV 

DIVISIONS IN CHRISTIANITY DEPLORED BY APOSTLES IN HOLY SCRIPTURES 

Christianity had hardly been established until divisions began to appear among its followers. But any one who carefully studies the Scriptures must admit that these heresies and schisms were deprecated by the Apostles. 

St. Paul shows that there were some among the Corinthians who held that there is no resurrection of the dead (I Cor. xv, 12) and that there were others at Ephesus who declared that the resurrection was already past (II Tim. ii, 18). St. John reveals that in his day there were "many anti 

Christs" (I John ii, 18 and iv, 3) while St. Paul warns the Thessalonians (II Epistle ii) to let no man deceive them but to "stand fast." We know from the Apostolic writings that incipient Gnosticism, one of the dreadful heresies of the early days, had obtained a foothold in the infant Church, and that clergymen and laity were not lacking who, jealous of the Apostles' authority, rebelled against it. St. Paul was forced to remind the Corinthians that it was a small thing for him to be judged by them "or by man's day" 

Some of the divisions attacked by the Apostles were not heresies or schisms in the strict sense; but they would have developed into them if they had not been checked. And we know that there were some genuine heretics. St. John's Gospel, upholding the Divinity of Christ, was written to offset teachers who denied this fundamental dogma. 

Various strong passages can be quoted from the Scriptures to show that division is deplored by Almighty God. In St. John's Gospel, xvii, 20 and 21, we find Christ praying for those who would believe in Him and asking: "That they all may be one, as Thou, Father, in Me, and I in Thee; that they also may be one in Us; that the world may believe that Thou hast sent Me." In Ephesians iv, we find St. Paul urging the people to be "careful to keep the unity of the Spirit in the bond of peace. One body and one Spirit; as you are called in one hope of your calling. One Lord, one faith, one Baptism. One God and Father of all, who is above all, and through all, and in us all' (verses 3-6). Again in Romans xvi, 17, St. Paul urges the people to mark those who cause divisions and offences contrary to the doctrine that the Christians have learned, and to avoid them. 

In First Corinthians i, 10-13, he beseeches the brethren, by the name of the Lord Jesus Christ, that they all speak the same thing and that there be no divisions among them; but that they be perfectly joined together in the same mind and in the same judgment. The Apostle has been informed that there are contentions among them. Some say that they belong to the party of Paul; some, to that of Apollo; some, to that of Cephas; some, to that of Christ. But Paul asks: "Is Christ divided: Was Paul then crucified for you? or were you baptized in the name of Paul?" The Apostle's language leaves no doubt that he abhorred divisions. 

From these Apostolic writings, we can easily gather that it is fatuous to hold, as many outside the Catholic Church do today, that, regardless of the denomination to which one gives allegiance, salvation is just as certain. 

But nevertheless there is an incident in the life of Christ that should give pause to those who declare that there can be no virtue or great work pleasing to God outside the visible true fold.

PAGE 

In St. Mark's Gospel, ix, 37-39, we find: "John answered Him, saying: 

Master, we saw one casting out devils in Thy name, who followed not us, and we forbade him. But Jesus said: Do not forbid him. For there is no man that doth a miracle in My name, and can soon speak ill of Me. For he that is not against you is for you."

Although the man spoken of in the Gospel certainly knew something of Christ's doctrine, he was not called into the Apostolic college and, it would seem, was irregular enough that John felt altogether justified in forbidding him to preach. Nevertheless, as there is nothing in the context to indicate that he was preaching false doctrine, heresy can hardly claim this Scriptural incident as a pretext to excuse the setting up of an improperly ordained clergy. Christ clearly indicates that the man was teaching the truth. The caster out of devils was irregular but sincere.

Although by no means can we ever agree that the denominations outside the Church are in any way equal to Christ's mystical body as channels. of grace—in fact, in themselves they are detriments to salvation—nevertheless many sincere preachers and laymen in them may be used by God to glorify His name, just as in ancient days the sibyls were used by God to instruct the pagans and prophets were sometimes sent to schismatic Israel. 

Good, sincere people outside the Church unwittingly share in her graces.

PAGE 

XXV 

IS THE CHURCH THE FOE OF FREEDOM? 

HERESIARCHS WANTED LICENSE, NOT LIBERTY 

The charge is frequently made that the Catholic Church is a foe to freedom. Men may mean all sorts of things by "freedom." Let us get down to basic facts by agreeing on what it is. 

It can mean independence from some restraint. It can be the ability to select one's own government. It can also mean license, which connotes independence of all restraint.

We will admit, to begin with, that the Catholic Church is opposed to license. We will also admit that she does not allow man the moral right to select his own spiritual government. She insists that she alone has the right to speak in the name of Jesus Christ, and that Jesus Christ is the only 

Savior of mankind.

But the Church is not tyrannical. She insists on restraint, but has certain definite limits beyond which she does not go. She demands that men obey the natural law and revelation, and, as a result of revelation, that they give spiritual obedience to her. She demands the right to regulate questions of faith and morals. When great controversies arise over what is or what is not the revelation of God on a given point, she claims the power to step into the dispute and to determine it. She does not claim to be receiving new revelations. But she does insist that the power has been left to her to define doctrine handed down from Apostolic times. 

The Church, therefore, says that there are limits beyond which the individual or great groups of individuals have no right to go. She insists that man was put on earth for the purpose of serving God; that he is here on probation; that his prime business is to save his soul; that she is the custodian of the means God has given us for salvation. Therefore, she cannot admit the right of men to desert her or to select their own means of salvation. 

If He has made a 

All except fools admit that God cannot lie. revelation, reason demands that we acknowledge the existence of some method whereby it may be safe-guarded, for it is as necessary to one individual as to another, and we all know that God is not giving revelations to each one of us. Unless we are willing to admit that He is a monster of injustice, we have to concede that He has given the race some method of finding what His revelation is. The Catholic Church claims to be that agency, and provides abundance of Scriptural and historical proofs in evidence of the fact. A mere denial, based on nothing but purely human evidence or sentimentality, is not enough to throw out these proofs. 

Reason proves that man has no right to license. The voice of conscience within us is internal proof that there is a difference between right and wrong. We know that we have the ability to effect either good or evil, for the inward accusing voice tells us. We also have a proof of the generality of this feeling in human nature by the attempts at justice that mark every people everywhere, civilized, barbarous, or savage. If there were not a generally understood difference between right and wrong, the guilty would not be universally punished and the deserving rewarded.

THE CHURCH Upon the Rock 

PAGE 67 Man, therefore, knows by the law of nature that he has not the moral right to do certain things. The natural law throws out the legality of license. Every person with common sense knows that there must be restraints on civilization or civilization will perish. How far these restraints are to extend, is the most mooted question in the world. If the Catholic 

Church is, as she claims, the representative of Almighty God on earth, she certainly has the right to determine these limitations. 

She answers, em- But are there any exempt classes of individuals? phatically, no. She gives greater power into the hands of her Pope than she accords to anyone else, but she reminds even him that there are laws over which he has no power of dispensation. She tells him that he cannot change the natural law or the Divine positive law. By the natural law, she means those regulations based on nature itself. By Divine positive law, she means the revelations of the Almighty. A Pope can dispense from a law of the Church, and other ecclesiastics have certain rights in this regard. 

But no man can change God's law.

Further, the Pope can sin as easily as other men. He has the task of saving his soul just as much as any scrub-woman or grocer. He goes to Confession regularly, because he is a sinner. So there is nobody on the face of the earth who is above law.

As for kings, they have frequently claimed moral exemption. They have always been able to find some ecclesiastical flatterers, who have either positively encouraged them or have not too vehemently discouraged them in this assertion. But the Church, officially, has never done so, and has deliberately allowed entire nations to cut themselves off from her rather than surrender her principles. Prime ministers, legislators, and others, though not openly claiming exemption from the moral law, often act as if they thought they had it. To them, as to all others, the Church says that a time is coming, when God will render to every man according to his works. She teaches that, immediately after the death of every individual, there is a particular judgment, in which his entire life is put into the balance of justice. The time for mercy has then passed. He is judged according to strict justice, but his sins can be overbalanced by the good or penance or special graces from God and the Church effected or obtained during life.

It is to be expected that the insistence of the Catholic Church on the limitations of license will bring constant reproach upon her. There never yet was a bad boy whipped by his father who was altogether convinced that the father was right. There never was a criminal corrected or arrested by a policeman who did not want to argue the case or who was altogether willing to admit the justice of his apprehension. We all are more or less rebellious at heart, because we are a fallen race and are here on probation. 

We are never altogether satisfied with one another and there is hardly a man who does not feel in his heart that he is a superior being, just a little bit of an improvement, in some way at least, over the other fellow. It is hard, then, to bow down and to admit our defectiveness. When Satan and his hordes would not do it, they were driven out of heaven. When Adam and Eve would not do it, they lost the Garden of Pleasure. 

Both angels and men lost happiness by pride, and the human race was saved by humility. God assumed human nature into the Person of His 

Son. The Maker became man and dwelt amongst us. Being in the form

PAGE 

of God, He was equal to God, but He took the form of a servant to save us. 

He humiliated Himself. The human race won heaven back by the path of humility, just as it had lost it through pride. God, therefore, insists on humility for us as individuals, and one of the chief ways by which we are to practice it is by submission to the Church. 

The Church is, therefore, a foe to that freedom which means independence from all restraint. But she is not in favor of tyranny. She reminds even Popes that there are limits beyond which they dare not go without having to answer to God; she reminds her General Councils that they are powerless without the sanction of the Papacy; she puts great power into the hands of her Bishops, but reminds them that they have a superior in Rome; she gives no little freedom to her priests, but forces them to obey her Bishops, and puts them through a course of seminary training when they are compelled to submit to hard discipline; she gives plenty of opportunity for lay activity, but reminds the laics that there are bounds over which they dare not trespass. Everybody in her organization has a superior. 

The Pope has none on earth, but he has One in heaven, and he knows that, if he forgets this, the fact of being Pope will not save him from hell fire. 

The Church is built upon authority. She demands obedience and, though she always tries kindness first, she hurls her anathemas at those who refuse to obey. It does not matter if I should become the most brilliant of writers, if I taught other than the Church had held from the beginning, I would be called to time. If I refused to retract, I would be dropped from the role of the faithful as if I were another Henry VIII. The world raises its eyebrows and shrugs its shoulders at such treatment. It calls it intolerance of the worst sort. But this intolerance looks like that which 

God practiced against the rebel angels. It is also like the intolerance practiced against our first parents. Truth is necessarily opposed to error. The 

Catholic Church, it must be admitted, is as utterly intolerant of sin as Almighty God. But she is also as merciful to the penitent.

With men constituted as they are, it was to be expected that heresiarch after heresiarch would arise in the history of the Church. It was to be expected that great scholars would refuse to agree with her traditions on some points, that great philosophers would split hairs with her, that proud priests and laymen would laugh her to scorn when she hurled her defiance at them. It was to be expected that they would try to justify themselves in the eyes of their fellowmen, and would bring all sorts of charges against her. This is exactly what has happened. 

Men rebel at the idea of strict authority. Tell them that they cannot do a thing, and they want to do it ten times as much as if they had not been forbidden. Tell them that certain individuals have the right to determine what is or what is not the law of Almighty God, and they will question that right. The human race has, for instance, had the Ten Commandments for century upon century, and these Commandments are merely statements of the natural law written in the human heart. Yet there is still to be found a single generation of men the majority of whom have strictly observed them. Even the best people are constantly offending against them. We have become so used to sinners that we take sin as a matter of course. 

Every one of us will have to admit that he uses all kinds of excuses to ease his habitual sins. It was to be expected, therefore, that every time a really powerful heresiarch arose, he would get a following. If men could be promised salvation on easier terms than the Catholic Church was willing

PAGE to offer, they could readily convince themselves that the chance was worth taking. And having left the Church, they would naturally invent all sorts of ugly stories about her, to quiet their conscience. As their children would be taught these stories and believe them, a heritage of hate would be handed down. Because the Church would not admit the right of anybody to leave her or to doubt her Divine authority, she would be branded as the foe of liberty. (All this has happened.) 

Men have gone to the Bible, to reason, everywhere, trying to bolster up their claims against the Church. They have referred to Christ's denunciation of the Jewish religious leaders as proof that it is all right to attack the Pope and the Bishops. But they forget that Christ's overthrow of 

Judaism was contemplated from the beginning and that it had been long foretold. They forget that Judaism was an imperfect religion, a mere ritual of preparation, and that it was supplanted by what St. Paul shows was a perfect religion. They also forget that, while Christ denounced the sins of the Jewish leaders, He recognized their authority up until the end of His earthly career, for His own Church did not officially begin its course until Pentecost day. They forget that He even acknowledged the authority of that contemptible, miserable coward, Pontius Pilate. Christ was a rebel, but the kind of a rebel that the Catholic Church is. He rebelled against sin, never against rightful authority.

As for the Apostles, they were intolerant of heresy. Their epistles ring with protests against it. They are vehement in its denunciation. They not only question the right of any man to dispute their Divine leadership, but St. Paul goes so far as to say that, if an angel from heaven should preach another doctrine than the one he has given, acceptance of it will be anathema. 

He denies even the right of an angel to correct him. The Roman Church is this liberal; not a bit more. She denies the right of any man, however learned, to substitute her doctrine. She tells you, with all the force that she can command, that, if you know her teachings and refuse to accept them, you are going to hell. And the hell she means is a place of indescribably severe torments that will never end. Christ taught that he who believeth not shall be condemned. If anybody does not like this, let him argue it out with God.

Christ made the situation clear. It is a question either of bowing down or of going to hell. There is no freedom here such as heresiarchs demand. There is no dictating to God. It is a question either of accepting 

Him or of suffering the consequences. And, since He is so unyielding, the only thing to do, if we are sensible, is to obey. It is hard. Nobody yet ever admitted that it was easy to be a Catholic. It is absolutely the most rigorous religion on earth. But, strange anomaly, it brings a sense of freedom and a liberation of intellect that is above anything the world can offer. "And you shall know the truth," said Jesus Christ, "and the truth shall make you free" (John viii, 32).

Man is so constituted that he is always subject to something outside himself. If he is subject to error, he is in slavery to that error. If he is subject to truth, it is Christ's promise that he will enjoy the paradox of freedom. Well-instructed and pious Catholics all have this sense of freedom. As we know from the testimony of converts, non-Catholics never really have it.

PAGE 

XXVI 

ST. PAUL TAUGHT THAT WE ARE CHRIST, IN HIS MYSTICAL BODY 

"Just as the natural body is one," says St. Thomas Aquinas, "though made up of diverse members, so the whole Church, Christ's Mystical Body, is reckoned as one person with its Head, which is Christ" (Summa 

Theologica, ques. xlix, first article). "The members of the Church," says the Attwater Catholic Encyclopedic Dictionary, "are bound together and bound to Christ, their head, into a spiritual though real Body, by the supernatural life of grace received in Baptism. Each member has his proper function to fulfill and the graces necessary to fulfill it under the direction of the Head. Membership is necessary for salvation, since only through His Mystical Body are Christ's merits applied. Thus it is not the individual Bishop who baptizes, preaches, governs, but Christ through Him; Christ and the Church sacrifice with the priest; the individual Christian prays not alone, but Christ and the Church pray in his person (and, in liturgical prayer, hardly ever in the singular number), and, if prayer is not answered for the individual, it is for the Body. 

Nor is the Mystical Body confined to the Church visible on earth; it includes the redeemed in heaven and the suffering in purgatory and all those who are said to belong to the 'soul of the Church'." 

The doctrine of the Mystical Body is parallel with that of the Communion of Saints. By this communion, we mean the unity under and in 

Christ of the Church Militant of the faithful on earth, the Church Suffering of the souls in purgatory, and the Church Triumphant of the blessed in heaven. The good angels share membership in the Church Triumphant.

Likewise, people who live in good conscience outside the Catholic Church, sincerely following such truth as they possess, belong to the "soul" of the Church and participate in the Mystical Body. 

St. Paul, in particular, set forth the doctrine of the Mystical Body of 

Jesus Christ. "For as the [human] body is one, and hath many members; and all the members of the one body, whereas they are many, yet are one body, so also is Christ," he says in I Cor. xii. Then, after a discussion of the human body with its numerous members all making up a unity, the members being mutually careful one for another (if one member should suffer all suffer and if one should rejoice all rejoice) he says: "You are the body of Christ, and members of member."

This same doctrine of unity in spiritual life was taught by Christ under another figure, when He represented Himself as a vine and us as the branches that get their life from that plant. "Abide in Me, and I in you," He said (John xv). "As the branch cannot bear fruit of itself, unless it abide in the vine, so neither can you, unless you abide in Me. I am the vine; you the branches: he that abideth in Me, and I in him, the same beareth much fruit: for without Me you can do nothing. If any one abide not in Me, he shall be cast forth as a branch, and shall wither, and they shall gather him up, and cast him into the fire, and he burneth. If you abide in Me, and My words abide in you, you shall ask whatever you will, and it shall be done unto you."

Success in spiritual life necessitates, therefore, union with the Mystical Body. It is only through grace, or the spiritual life that flows to us from

PAGE Christ, the Head of this Body, that we can expect salvation. Without Him, as He says, we can "do nothing." 

St. Paul, in his Letter to the Ephesians, chapter i, says that God “hath subjected all things under" Christ's feet, "and hath made Him head over all the Church, which is His Body, and the fullness of Him who is filled all in all."

It is no wonder that St. Paul was especially impressed with the doctrine of the Mystical Body. When he, as Saul, the persecutor, was on the way to Damascus to continue his onslaughts against the infant Church, Christ appeared, converting him to the new religion, and the Master said: "I am 

Jesus whom thou persecutest" (Acts ix, 5). Christ made this persecution a personal thing directed against Himself. In attacking the Church, Saul was attacking Him. The reference to the Mystical Body is obvious. 

Even some of our sufferings may come because of the Mystical Body. 

St. Paul wrote of himself to the Colossians (i, 24) that he rejoiced in his sufferings for them, and filled up those things that are wanting of the sufferings of Christ, in his flesh, for Christ's body, "which is the Church.” 

This text does not mean that there was anything wanting in the sufferings of Christ insofar as their effectiveness in saving us is concerned, but it implies that we are able to suffer one for another, as Christ did, and as St. 

Paul was gladly imitating Him in doing.

The same Apostle could hardly be clearer in his description of the Mystical Body than when He declared: "No man ever hateth his own flesh, but nourisheth it and cherisheth it, as also Christ does the Church: because we are members of His body, of His flesh, and of His bones" (Ephesians v, 29, 30).

It is as absurd, therefore, for divisions to exist among Christians as it would be if the arm were to rebel against the leg or the feet against the heart. The genuine Christian religion cannot be divided. An immense charity ought to exist among all the members of the Church, and a new appreciation of the value of our souls ought to arise because of our con sciousness of being mystically part of Christ.

PAGE 

XXVII 

SEVEN SACRAMENTS VEHICLES OF JESUS CHRIST'S GRACE TO SOULS 

One of the most striking things about the Catholic faith is its fidelity to the sacraments. By a sacrament we mean an outward sign instituted by 

Christ to signify and to give grace. There are seven sacraments. 

Christ took some of the commonest things of life and made them vehicles of His grace to us. Consider, for instance, the first sacrament, Baptism. The word "baptism" means "washing," and the administration of Baptism consists simply in washing a person, to the accompaniment of certain words, to indicate that we are doing what Christ did. The outward sign or washing signifies cleansing; and the sacrament effects what it signifies it cleanses the soul. Every sacrament effects what it signifies. 

Three things are needed to make a sacrament. It must have a sensible sign. It must have been instituted by Christ. It must have the power to give grace.

Judged by these tests, the Church teaches that the following are sacraments: Baptism, Confirmation, Holy Eucharist, Penance, Extreme Unction,

Holy Orders, and Matrimony. When the sign disappears, there is no longer a sacrament. We refer to the Holy Eucharist as the Blessed Sacrament. 

If the sign of the appearances of Bread and Wine disappears, the Blessed Sacrament is no longer present. Consequently, if a Host were to corrupt, the Real Presence of the Body and Blood of Christ would no longer be there. Dried stains left in a purificator (a linen used for cleansing the chalice at Mass) do not contain the Real Presence. 

There were sacraments in the Old Law, i.e., before Christ, but they were imperfect as compared with those of the New. 

Theologians refer to the matter and form of the sacraments. By the matter, generally speaking, they mean the tangible element that enters into the sacraments, as water poured in Baptism, and, by the form, the words uttered by him who administers the rite. 

The sacraments have their power of giving grace from the merits of Jesus Christ. They usually give far more grace than a prayer would and they work differently than a prayer. A man who is not conscious cannot pray, but he can receive a sacrament, and, if his dispositions have been good, the sacrament works.

Some of the sacraments impart sanctifying grace to us; some increase it in the soul. Those that give it are Baptism and Penance, which are called sacraments of the dead because their purpose is to take away sin, which is death to the soul, and to give grace, which is its life. The following sacraments increase sanctifying grace in the soul: Confirmation, Holy

Eucharist, Extreme Unction, Holy Orders, and Matrimony. They are called sacraments of the living, because we are supposed to be in the state of grace or already supernaturally living when we receive them. They increase sanctifying grace in us and bring special and actual graces. It is probable that one in the state of mortal sin who, thinking himself to be in the state of grace, receives a sacrament of the living with only imperfect contrition, is, by the reception of the sacrament, justified or put into the state of grace. He who knows himself to be in the state of mortal sin

PAGE is obliged to regain the state of grace by going to Confession or by an act of perfect contrition before receiving a sacrament of the living; and, in the case of the Eucharist, he is, generally speaking, obliged first to go to 

Confession.* 

One who receives a sacrament of the living unworthily commits sacrilege, which is the abuse of a sacred thing and a great sin. 

In addition to sanctifying grace, the sacraments give another grace, called sacramental. It is a special help given by God to attain the end. for which He made each sacrament. In Holy Communion, for instance, the special sacramental grace is supernatural love.

If we receive the sacraments with the right dispositions, they always give grace. By the right dispositions, we mean an intention to receive them and the will to serve God. A man who had no intention of staying out of mortal sin would lack the right dispositions. 

Three of the sacraments cannot be received more than once. They are 

Baptism, Confirmation, and Holy Orders. They imprint a mark on the soul, which is a spiritual character or seal that will remain forever, to add to the glory of those who are saved, to the shame of those who are lost. "Now he that confirmeth us with you in Christ, and that hath anointed us, is God: who also hath sealed us, and given the pledge of the Spirit in our hearts," says St. Paul (II Cor. i, 21, 22). All the other sacraments can be received more than once. The greatest of the sacraments is the Holy Eucharist, in which we receive God Himself.

Much of our knowledge of the sacraments comes from the Scriptures. 

But much of it we learn through Tradition, which is as reliable a source of 

Divine information as the Bible.

It is probable that Peter Lombard, who died in 1164, first definitely enumerated and scientifically defined the seven sacraments. But this does not mean that he invented them. They were always accepted in the Church before. Oriental schismatic sects that cut off in the fifth century still teach them.** The Catholic Encyclopedia, volume xiii, page 300, says it is worthy of note that, although the great scholastics rejected many of Lombard's theological opinions, his definition and enumeration of the sacraments "were at once universally accepted, proof positive that he did not introduce a new doctrine, but merely expressed in a convenient and precise formula what had always been held in the Church. Just as many doctrines were believed, but not accurately expressed, until the condemnation of heresies or the development of religious knowledge called forth a neat and precise formula, so also the sacraments were accepted and used by the Church for centuries before Aristotelean philosophy, applied to the systematic explanation of Catholic doctrine, furnished the accurate definition and enumeration of Peter Lombard. The earlier Christians were more concerned with the *This Confession is demanded as a result of ecclesiastical legislation. It was permissible for a long period of Church history to receive Communion after making only an act of perfect contrition, although, of course, Confession has always been in the Church and in common use, demanded with certain frequency by ecclesiastical law. 

**For instance, the Armenian Apostolic Church. The dissident Orthodox 

Churches, which left Papal obedience in the Greek separation in the eleventh century, refer to sacraments as "mysteries" and accept our enumeration.

PAGE 

use of sacred rites than with scientific formulae, being like the pious author of the 'Imitation of Christ,' who wrote: 'I had rather feel compunction than know its definition' (I, i)."

To have a sacrament, we must have a visible sign. Grace is often conferred by God without a sign. For instance, He immediately justifies a sinner who turns to Him with sorrow and love. But, where there is no sign, there is no sacrament. The sign really effects the grace that it signifies.

The Protestant contention that the sacraments are simply pledges or seals of God's promise to us, but do not confer grace, is founded on nothing Scriptural and finds no support in apostolic Christianity. It is because of this doctrine of Martin Luther that many of his followers stopped believing in the Real Presence in the Blessed Sacrament. Luther refused to believe that bread and wine could be substantially changed into Christ's body and blood, soul and divinity, but he declared that Christ was really present spiritually within the bread and wine. But, argued some of his followers, if the Communion is simply a sign of God's promises to us, and brings no grace in itself, how can Christ be actually present? Their position is logical, but it starts on false premises, and hence is wrong. For the sacrament of the Holy Eucharist does confer grace. Christ definitely promised in John vi: "He that eateth My flesh and drinketh My blood hath everlasting life: and

I will raise him up in the last day. For My flesh is meat indeed; and My blood is drink indeed. He that eateth My flesh and drinketh My blood abideth in Me and I in him."

This text alone is enough to prove that the Catholic doctrine of the sacraments is the only correct one. The outward sign of eating Christ's flesh and drinking His blood is here definitely set forth as bringing the abiding of Christ in us, and of us in Him, or inward grace.

PAGE XXVIII 

BAPTISM IS SACRAMENT NECESSARY FOR SALVATION 

"Baptism," says the Baltimore Catechism, "is a sacrament which cleanses us from original sin, makes us Christians, children of God, and heirs of heaven."

It is necessary to salvation. If we are not baptized, we cannot enter heaven. "Unless," said Christ, "a man be born again of water and the Holy

Ghost, he cannot enter into the kingdom of God" (John iii, 5). The word man here means human being. Not even infants are exempted by Our Lord. 

Hence the Catholic Church demands that we have our babies baptized. 

St. Paul calls Baptism a cleansing "by the laver of water in the word of life" (Eph. v, 26). "By nature," says the Catechism of the Council of Trent, "we are born from Adam children of wrath, but by Baptism we are regenerated in 

Christ, children of mercy."

Christ gave the power to men "to be made the sons of God, to them that believe in His name, who are born, not of blood, nor of the will of the flesh, nor of the will of man, but of God" (John i, 12, 13).

Baptism consists of a washing, joined to solemn words which indicate its character as a sacrament. "The word is joined to the element, and it becomes a sacrament," said St. Augustine (in Joan. Tr. Ixxx No. 3). Both the washing and the words are necessary to make Baptism. If the words meant nothing, I might baptize a child every time I washed him. 

The Scriptures teach, then, that Baptism is a spiritual rebirth, and that it is essential to salvation.

The priest is the ordinary minister of Baptism, but in case of necessity any one with the use of reason can baptize. Even a pagan or an unbeliever of any sort could baptize, in case of necessity, by carrying out the ceremony and having the intention of doing what is ordinarily done. A belief in the efficacy of the sacrament is not necessary to make it valid. Unless, however, there is necessity for lay Baptism, we are expected to be baptized in the church edifice by the priest. The priest surrounds Baptism with ancient anointings and other rites, and, when the sacrament is thus administered, it is called Solemn Baptism. The purpose of these ceremonies is to pray for greater holiness for the recipient and to put a check on the spiritual powers of evil, which constantly surround us." 

Everybody should know how to baptize. The ceremony is simple. 

Water is poured on the forehead of the person to be baptized and he who does the baptizing says, while pouring the water: "I baptize thee in the name of the Father, and of the Son, and of the Holy Ghost." These words and no others should be used. The name to be given can be pronounced just before uttering these words. Any kind of genuine water can be used. 

In Solemn Baptism (given by the priest in the church) Baptismal water, specially blessed, is used; in private Baptism, any common water or holy water may be used. The water should be poured on the head of the child in sufficient quantity to flow and to wash the skin. 

After private Baptism, we are commanded to bring the child to the church to have the ceremonies of Solemn Baptism supplied, and the priest should be told that the private Baptism was given.

PAGE 

The Church recognizes as valid Baptism by immersion and also that by sprinkling. In Baptism of immersion, the words are said as the recipient is completely dipped. In Baptism of sprinkling, the words are said as the sprinkling is done and the water flows on the person. The water must flow in administering the Baptism of sprinkling. 

While, however, she recognizes Baptism by immersion and sprinkling as valid, the Church does not permit us to use these methods in the Latin portion of the fold today. Her reason for this, undoubtedly, is because certain sectarians insist that only immersion is valid Baptism and that one who has not been dipped is unbaptized. When one of her practices is thus called into contempt, the Church at times insists on its use, as a test of true faith.

There are three kinds of Baptism that of water, that of desire, and that of blood. Baptism of water is the type we have already described. 

Baptism of desire is the ardent wish to receive Baptism and to do all that God has ordained for our salvation. If a man were being instructed with the intention of becoming a Catholic, and were killed before getting the Baptism of water, he would be considered as having the Baptism of desire and would be buried as a Catholic. If a man were never able to learn of Christ in such a way that he would be held accountable for rejecting the faith that is necessary to salvation, and if he did not know the necessity of Baptism, yet wished to do what was necessary to be saved, he would likewise have the Baptism of desire.

Baptism of blood is the shedding of one's blood for Christ. The 

Master taught many times that Baptism of blood would bring us to heaven. 

For instance, in His Sermon on the Mount (Matt. v, 10), He said: "Blessed are they that suffer persecution for justice's sake: for theirs is the kingdom of heaven." He taught that a man who lost his life for His sake would find it.* Therefore, if we deliberately choose death rather than deny Christ or rather than sin against one of His virtues, we are immediately admitted to heaven, according to the unanimous teaching of the Church in all ages. This does not mean that we are ever permitted to commit suicide to escape an attack on our virtue.

Baptism of desire or of blood produces the effects of the Baptism of water, if we cannot receive the latter. But only the Baptism of water imprints the indelible and eternal mark of Baptism on the soul. It is possible to have the Baptism of blood whether or not we have received the Baptism of water.

In Solemn Baptism, we promise to renounce the devil and all his works and pomps. When we were baptized as infants, our sponsors promised this in our name. The name of a saint is given in Baptism to give us a patron who will pray for us and protect us and whom we might imitate. 

A name that does not belong to a saint can be taken, if the name of a saint is also added.

Sponsors, often called godfathers and godmothers, are given in Baptism, in order to promise, for the child, what it would promise if it had the use of reason. One sponsor is sufficient, although it is customary to have two.

If the parents of the child die or neglect their duty, the sponsor has the obligation to instruct the child in its religious duties. Adults, as well *"He that shall lose his life for Me shall find it" (Matt. x, 39).

PAGE as children, have sponsors in Baptism, and, if the godchild afterwards wanders from the path of faith or virtue, the sponsor has a right to protest. 

If a baby is unbaptized, it cannot enter heaven, but most theologians believe that it goes to a place of rest, where it suffers no pain and where it is considerably happier than it could be in this life. By not admitting it to heaven, God works no injustice, for heaven is so far above anything our nature demands for happiness that we cannot even imagine what it will be like. If I have a great fortune and tell one of my neighbors that I will give his little child a share of it if the parent names the child John, and then the parent does not, the child or parent can hardly accuse me of injustice. The fortune is mine; I can do as I please with it. Heaven is 

God's fortune. And it seems that He takes good care of the unbaptized babies. Nevertheless, it is an awful crime to let a child die without Baptism when we can give it the sacrament. Even the prematurely born child should be baptized, either in the mother's womb by the use of instruments (a priest never officiates at such a Baptism), or, in case of miscarriage, by immediately immersing the sack in water and pronouncing the words while breaking the sack.

In order to act validly as a sponsor in Baptism, the person must be baptized, be old enough to understand what is being done, have the intention of undertaking the office, not belong to any heretical or schismatical sect, not be excommunicated by a condemnatory or declaratory sentence, not suffer from infamy of law, or be excluded from legal actions, or be a deposed or degraded cleric; must not be the parent of or married to the candidate for Baptism; must be designated by the one to be baptized or the candidate's parents or guardians or, in their default, by the minister of Baptism, and must, either in person or through proxy, physically hold or touch the one baptized or receive the candidate immediately after Baptism from the sacred font or from the hands of the minister. In order to act licitly as a sponsor, one must be 14 years old, unless for a just reason the minister admits a younger person; must not be under excommunication for a notorious crime, or excluded from legal action, or suffer from infamy of law, even though no sentence was pronounced against him in the ecclesiastical court; nor must he be under interdict, or otherwise a public criminal, or disgraced by infamy of fact. He must know the rudiments of the faith and must not be a novice or professed member in any religious organization, unless there is no other to act as sponsor and permission is given by at least the local superior. He must not be in sacred Orders, unless he has express permission of his Ordinary to act. the Spiritual relationship is contracted by the one baptized with the minister and the sponsor. In the repetition of Baptism under condition, neither sponsor of the first nor the one at the second Baptism contracts spiritual relationship, unless the same sponsor is present at both Baptisms. This spiritual relationship would forbid marriage between one who baptizes and the person baptized.

Some Protestants refuse to have infants baptized because they say the infants cannot believe. They forget that faith is a Divinely infused virtue and that the habit of it can be infused by God into an infant as well as into an adult. When the opportunity later presents itself, the child will elicit acts of faith through the aid of that habit. We baptize infants

PAGE 

because Christ made no exception in insisting on the universality of Baptism and also because Tradition tells us we must give them the sacrament. 

In early centuries, Christians often waited many years before having their children baptized and adults sometimes put off the sacrament until death. The emperor Constantine was not baptized until he was dying and then received the sacrament from Eusebius, an Arian, not a Catholic, Bishop. 

Spiritual ignorance probably excused him. The fact that Baptism completely cleanses the soul and makes it ready for immediate entry into heaven motivated these long waits on the part of many. But they forgot that we need to be in the state of grace to grow in grace and that he who lives as a baptized Christian has many opportunities to pile up new grace, and thus to win a higher place in heaven; also that grace helps us to avoid sin. 

Postponement of the sacrament is looked upon as an abuse. The Church always regarded it this way. 

Parents are obliged to see that a new-born child receives Baptism as soon as possible. The sacrament ought to be administered by the Sunday following birth and, except in extraordinary circumstances, can not be postponed more than a month without mortal sin. If a child is born where it is difficult to reach a priest, and it is feared that a long time will elapse, a layman ought to do the baptizing, even if the baby is in good health. 

When an adult is baptized, faith and at least imperfect penance for sin are necessary if Baptism is to have its effect.

PAGE 

XXIX 

CONFIRMATION MAKES SOLDIERS OF CHRIST OUT OF GOD'S CHILDREN 

Confirmation is a sacrament in which, by anointing and imposition of hands, with prayer, we receive the Holy Ghost, to make us strong and perfect Christians and soldiers of Jesus Christ.

The ordinary minister of Confirmation is a Bishop. But, by special permission of the Pope, it can be given by a priest. The first member of the American hierarchy, Archbishop Carroll, had the delegation to give Confirmation before he was consecrated a Bishop. The permission is often given in mission lands. The writer has known two Abbots and one Monsignor (a Protonotary Apostolic) who had it in the United States. 

The fact that the apostles confirmed is set forth in Acts viii, 17: "Then they laid their hands upon them, and they received the Holy Ghost." 

Acts xix, 6, also tells of a Confirmation ceremony by Paul. 

Baptism makes us children of God and members of His Church; Confirmation makes us His soldiers. By Baptism we become citizens; by 

Confirmation, the defenders of Christianity. St. Clement, the fourth Pope (reigned 90 to 100 A. D.), declared: "All should hasten without delay to be born again unto God, and afterwards to be signed by the Bishop, that is, to receive the seven-fold gift of the Holy Ghost; for, as has been handed down from St. Peter, and as the other Apostles taught, in obedience to the command of Our Lord, he who culpably and voluntarily, and not from necessity, neglects to receive this sacrament, cannot possibly be a perfect 

Christian" (quoted in Isidore, Ep. iv).

We have shown before that the Holy Ghost is in the Church. It is chiefly by this sacrament that He comes into the individual soul. We receive Him in all the sacraments. But Confirmation is the Holy Ghost's own particular sacrament.

In the administration of Confirmation, the Bishop extends his hands over those who are to be confirmed, praying that they may receive the Holy Ghost, and anoints the forehead of each with chrism, in the form of a cross.

Chrism is composed of oil and balsam, consecrated by a Bishop. "Oil, by its nature rich, unctuous and fluid," says the Catechism of the Council of Trent, "expresses the fulness of grace, which, through the Holy Ghost, overflows and is poured into others from Christ the head, 'like the ointment that ran down upon the beard of Aaron, to the skirt of his garment' (Ps. cxxxii, 2); 'for God anointed him with the oil of gladness, above his fellows' (Ps. xliv, 8), and 'of His fulness we all have received' (John i, 16).” 

Balsam, which has a pleasant odor, typifies the sweet odor of virtue that confirmed Christians should diffuse about them, in accordance with St. 

Paul's, "We are unto God the good odor of Christ" (II Cor. ii, 15).

When anointing the person to be confirmed, the Bishop says: "I sign thee with the sign of the cross, and I confirm thee with the chrism of salvation, in the name of the Father, and of the Son, and of the Holy Ghost." 

The anointing with chrism in the form of a cross indicates that the 

Christian should openly profess and practice his faith, never be ashamed of it, and die rather than deny it. The Bishop gives him a slight blow on

PAGE 

the cheek to remind him that he must be willing to suffer, even to go to death, rather than deny Christ.

When we consider how cowardly the Apostles were before they received the Holy Ghost on Pentecost, and how they went boldly forth, preaching and dying in the name of Christ, after the Paraclete had come, we realize His strengthening power. One day they were hiding for fear of the Jews; the next, they were facing the multitudes, preaching the Man- 

God whom these very people, a short time before, had crucified. 

In proportionate measure, all confirmed Catholics share this strengthening power. That is why they are bothered so little by the campaign of vilification often carried on against their Church, which, the holiest of institutions, is held up as the vilest. That is why millions of Christians eagerly went to death in the persecutions of the ancient Roman empire, rather than deny Christ, even though denial meant nothing more than to burn a few grains of incense in front of an idol. 

To receive Confirmation worthily, we should be in the state of grace. 

It is customary to prepare for the sacrament by Confession and Communion. 

When one is converted to the Catholic Church, one should arrange to receive Confirmation as soon as possible after being admitted into the fold. The sacrament is given in many churches annually, in all at least once every several years. A person to receive it should be instructed in the chief mysteries of religion and the duties of a Christian, and know of the nature and effects of this sacrament. It is at least a venial sin to neglect 

Confirmation in our day, when faith and morals are exposed to many violent temptations.

The effects of Confirmation are an increase of sanctifying grace, the strengthening of our faith, and the gifts of the Holy Ghost. The sacrament imprints an imperishable character on the soul, which will be found through all eternity, in heaven or hell.

The gifts of the Holy Ghost are wisdom, understanding, counsel, fortitude, knowledge, piety, and fear of the Lord. These gifts are enumerated in Isaias xi, 2, 3, as coming from the Spirit of the Lord.

The twelve fruits of the Holy Ghost are charity, joy, peace, patience, benignity, goodness, long-suffering, mildness, faith, modesty, continency, and chastity. These are called fruits of the Holy Ghost because they are the ripe product of His graces. All of them come from a worthy reception of Confirmation. 

Wisdom, understanding, counsel, and knowledge are given in Confirmation to remove ignorance from the soul. Fear of the Lord inculcates a dread of sin. Piety makes us love God and obey Him because we love

Him. Knowledge is given to enable us to discover the will of God in all things; fortitude, to strengthen us to do God's will; counsel, to warn us of the deceits of Satan and of the dangers to salvation; understanding, to enable us to know more clearly the mysteries of faith; wisdom, to give us a relish for the things of God and to direct our whole life and all our actions to His honor and glory.

PAGE XXX 

REAL PRESENCE OF JESUS CHRIST UNDER SPECIES OF BREAD AND WINE 

"The Holy Eucharist," says the Baltimore Catechism, "is the sacrament which contains the body and blood, soul and divinity of Our Lord Jesus 

Christ, under the appearance of bread and wine."

We have accounts of the institution of the Blessed Eucharist in the Gospels of St. Matthew, St. Mark, and St. Luke, and in St. Paul's First 

Letter to the Corinthians. In the sixth chapter of St. John's Gospel, Our 

Lord promises the people His flesh to eat and His blood to drink. 

Following is the account of St. Paul in First Corinthians, xi: "For I have received of the Lord that which also I delivered unto you, that the Lord Jesus, the same night in which He was betrayed, took bread, and giving thanks, broke and said: Take ye and eat: this is My body, which shall be delivered for you; this do for the commemoration of Me. In like manner also the chalice, after He had supped, saying: This is the New Testament in My blood: this do ye, as often as you shall drink, for the commemoration of Me. For as often as you shall eat this bread and drink the chalice, you shall show the death of the Lord, until He come. Therefore, whosoever shall eat this bread, or drink the chalice of the Lord unworthily, shall be guilty of the body and of the blood of the Lord. But let a man prove himself; and so let him eat of that bread, and drink of the chalice. For he that eateth and drinketh unworthily eateth and drinketh judgment to himself, not discerning the body of the Lord." 

The accounts all agree. Christ took bread and consecrated it, declaring that it was His body, and He took wine and consecrated it, declaring that it was His blood. When He said, "This is My body," the substance of the bread was changed into the substance of His body; when He said, “This is 

My blood," the substance of the wine was changed into the substance of His blood. There was no play on words. The words mean just what they indicate. And they indicate that what was bread now became His body, what was wine now became His blood. This was the unanimous interpretation of the Christians for centuries. The figurative interpretation became widespread at the time of the Reformation, but the Catholic Church, which cannot err in such an important doctrine because of the abiding protection of the Holy Ghost, has always adhered to the doctrine taught by Paul and the four Evangelists.

In John vi we are told that Christ said to the Jews: "He that believeth in Me hath everlasting life. I am the bread of life. Your fathers did eat manna in the desert, and are dead. This is the bread which cometh down from heaven; that, if any man eat of it, he may not die. I am the living bread which came down from heaven. If any man eat of this bread, he shall live forever; and the bread that I will give is My flesh, for the life of the world."

The Jews strove among themselves, protesting that He could not give them His flesh to eat, but, far from retracting or explaining away His language as something merely figurative, He went on: "Except you eat the lesh of the Son of man, and drink His blood, you shall not have life in you. For My flesh is meat indeed; and My blood is drink indeed. He that

PAGE 

eateth My flesh, and drinketh My blood, abideth in Me, and I in him. . . . 

He that eateth this bread shall live forever."

So difficult was this to believe that John tells us: "After this many of His disciples went back; and walked no more with Him.” 

The same thing is true today. Most Protestants are unwilling to accept the doctrine of the Real Presence, but, if we are really willing to accept the Bible, how can we doubt the doctrine in the face of Christ's words? 

Jesus Christ is whole and entire both under the form of bread and under the form of wine. This is true because He is present after the manner of a substance. The substance of humanity is present in your whole body and wholly present in every single part of your body. The substance of horse is present in every single part of a horse. Owing to His substantial presence in the Blessed Sacrament, Christ is in the sacred species and is wholly present in every part of them. His presence ceases only when they cease to appear as bread and wine. A sacrament is an outward sign of inward grace, and, if the appearances of bread and wine disappear, so does the Real Presence, as the sign is then gone. If a priest, at Communion, breaks the Host in order to communicate two persons instead of one, each receives the entire Christ. Because of Christ's substantial presence, likewise, we receive the entire Christ in receiving the Consecrated Bread alone. 

After the bread and wine have been changed into the substance of the body and blood of Christ, there remain only the appearances of bread and wine, often referred to as the "species." By these appearances we mean the figure, the color, the taste, or whatever appears to the senses. 

We can see these appearances when we look at the Blessed Sacrament, but we do not see the substance. When the priest breaks the Host, he breaks these species, but not Our Lord's body. The presence is, really, very mysterious. 

The change of the bread and wine into the body and blood of Christ is called Transubstantiation. This word is not in the Scriptures, but is a theological term to express what happens. The idea, of course, is in the Scriptures.

The change of the bread and wine into Christ's body and blood continues to be made in the Catholic Church, through the celebration of Mass by the priests. At the Mass, exactly the same Transubstantiation takes place as occurred at the Last Supper. Christ gave this power to the Apostles, and through them to the priesthood of the Church, when He said, at the Last 

Supper: "Do this in commemoration of Me."

When celebrating Mass, the priest uses the very words of Christ and effects exactly what Christ did. "This is My body," he says, and the bread becomes Christ's body. "This is My blood," he declares, and the wine becomes His blood. The priest has the power because he participates in the priesthood of Jesus Christ, and hence Christ acts through him. 

Because Christ is wholly present in the Blessed Sacrament, and because He is God as well as man, we say that His body and blood, soul and divinity are there.

PAGE 

XXXI HOW MASS OFFERS GOD HIGHEST HONOR IT IS POSSIBLE TO GIVE HIM 

Christ, at the Last Supper, after having changed bread and wine into His own body and blood, the appearances of bread and wine remaining but the substance of them being changed, commanded that His Apostles do this in commemoration of Him. The Catholic Church obeys that command by the celebration of Mass, which "is the unbloody sacrifice of the body and blood of Christ," and which, as St. Paul taught the Corinthians (chapter xi), shows the death of the Lord until He shall come to judge all mankind.

A sacrifice in the strict sense is something that can be offered to the Divinity alone. It is more than a prayer. We can pray to fellow creatures and do so when we ask their help, whether they are living men or saints or angels. If I ask you for a favor, I pray to you. We can pray to God as well as to creatures, but, when it comes to offering sacrifice, we can do this to God alone. Even though a priest offers a Mass of the Blessed Virgin or some saint, he does not offer the sacrifice to the creature, regardless of how great that creature is. The Mass is offered in honor of the creature, but to God, because sacrifice is offered to the Divinity alone. theologians are able to prove that the Mass is a real sacrifice. Catholic

In Protestantism, the idea of sacrifice in the strict sense has been abandoned. Such a thing as having no sacrifice was unheard of for century after century of Christianity, and Protestantism did not imitate primitive Christianity, but Mohammedanism, in this abandonment. The Mohammedans have no sacrifice in their worship. It is a worship of prayer and austerity alone.

But if we go back to the legitimate forebears of Christianity—Judaism and the patriarchal religion that preceded it-we find sacrifice the center of their worship. There was prayer, but something higher than prayer was demanded. God must have His own distinct worship above what we can pay a creature. The Jews today have no sacrifice, but that is because they no longer have a temple or a priesthood. Their priesthood was obliterated with the destruction of their temple by the Romans in the first century. 

Their rabbis are teachers, not priests.

All religions have sacrifice in the broad sense of the word in that many of their workers give their labor voluntarily or for small remuneration. 

Many have fasting and other practices of this kind. But sacrifices of this nature are called sacrificial only by an adaptation of the word. 

A genuine sacrifice is the oblation and destruction of any sensible thing, made by an authorized minister to God alone, in order to acknowledge His supreme dominion and to implore His mercy, resulting in a certain communion with God.

Even the pagans had this idea. They had a false notion of Divinity, but knew that one had to be a god before one could be the object of sacrifice. Only a priest could offer this sacrifice. Even when incense was burned before household gods, it was because the average person believed himself enough of a priest to do this. Many gods had their own priests and nobody but a priest would dare to offer sacrifice. 

The destruction of the victim could be either physical or moral. Sometimes, among both the Jews and the pagans, there was a holocaust, with

PAGE 

the burning of the victim, an animal. Sometimes there was only moral destruction, as when wine was offered and then poured out. The eating of the Paschal lamb was a true sacrifice, the lamb being offered and then eaten. 

A real or equivalent destruction of the offering is, therefore, made in a genuine sacrifice.

The Mass fulfills all the demands for a true sacrifice. It is defined as: "The sacrifice of the New Law, in which Christ is mystically immolated under the species of bread and wine, through a man acting as the minister of the Church for the purpose of acknowledging the supreme dominion of God and of applying to us the satisfactions and merits of Christ's passion." 

The Mass is the same sacrifice as that of the Cross, because the offering and the priest are the same and the ends for which the sacrifice is offered are the same.

The priest is the same Jesus Christ. While it is true that an ordinary priest is not Christ, it is Christ who officiates at every Mass, because a priest is a priest only because he participates in the priesthood of Christ. It is
