Christ's priesthood that officiates. That is why the priest does not say: "This is Christ's body," and "This is Christ's blood," but "This is My body," and "This is My blood."

The purpose of offering up Mass is exactly the same as Christ had on Calvary, namely, to honor and to glorify God, to thank Him for all the graces bestowed on the whole world, to satisfy God's justice for the sins of men, and to obtain all graces and blessings for us. 

It is the same sacrifice. As often as we drink this Chalice and eat this 

Bread, we show the death of the Lord, as St. Paul taught. But the manner of offering it is different. On the Cross Christ really shed His blood and really died, while in the Mass He does not really shed His blood, because Christ can die no more. But there is an equivalent, moral, or mystical shedding of blood, and hence the drama of the Mass fulfills all the essentials of a true sacrifice. The Mass shows the death of the Lord through the separate consecration of the bread and wine. If I separate your flesh from your blood, you will die. By the drama of the separate consecration, in the Mass, of the bread into Christ's body and of the wine into His blood, the priest typifies His death. Christ is not really slain, but He is mystically slain, in every Mass.

St. Paul's teaching about showing the death of the Lord through the 

Eucharist becomes absurd unless we accept the Catholic doctrine. 

We should assist at the Mass with great interior piety and show every outward mark of respect. The most acceptable method of hearing Mass is to follow, in our prayerbooks, the Ordinary of the Mass; thereby we participate to our fullest capacity with the priest in his liturgical worship. It is all right to say other prayers, but those of the liturgy are the best at Mass. 

And of course, the Church would like to have us receive Communion, although not compelling us.

The principal parts of the Mass are the Offertory, the Consecration, and the Communion of the priest. It is debatable what are the absolutely essential parts. The majority of theologians hold that the Consecration is the essence of the Mass. Some, among them the great St. Alphonsus 

Liguori, say the essence includes the Consecration and the Communion of the priest. St. Alphonsus finds the sacrificial destruction of the Victim in the consumption of Christ as the Blessed Sacrament, in the Communion.

PAGE XXXII WHY JESUS CHRIST INSTITUTED THE BLESSED SACRAMENT 

Jesus Christ instituted the Blessed Sacrament in order that it might be the spiritual food of our souls. Holy Communion is the receiving of 

Him in the sacrament of the Holy Eucharist. 

The chief effects of this reception are a closer union with Our Lord and a more fervent love of God and of our neighbor; an increase of sanctifying grace; preservation from mortal sin; the remission of venial sin for which we are repentant; the lessening of our inclination towards sin; and assistance in the practice of good works. 

The old Baltimore Catechism gave the following beautiful list of reasons for the institution of the Eucharist: (1) To unite us to Christ and to nourish our souls with His Divine life; (2) to increase sanctifying grace in our souls; (3) to lessen our evil inclinations; (4) to be a pledge of everlasting life; (5) to fit our bodies for a glorious resurrection; (6) to continue the sacrifice of the Cross in the Church.

The union of Jesus Christ with ourselves in the Holy Eucharist brings about a communication of Divine life to us and a communion of love between ourselves and others who are similarly united to Him. "The chalice of benediction, which we bless, is it not the communion of the blood of Christ? 

And the bread, which we break, is it not the partaking of the body of the 

Lord?" asks St. Paul (I Cor. x, 16-17). "For we, being many, are one bread, one body, all that partake of one bread.”

The increase of sanctifying grace and all virtues in our souls and the lessening of evil inclinations will not be doubted by anyone who believes in the Real Presence of the body and blood of Jesus Christ in the Blessed 

Sacrament. How could God reside in us without these effects? "I am the bread of life," said Christ (John vi). “. . . This is the bread which cometh down from heaven; that, if any man eat of it, he may not die. Except you eat the flesh of the Son of man, and drink His blood, you shall not have life in you. 

He that eateth My flesh, and drinketh My blood, abideth in Me, and I in him. . . . He that eateth Me, the same shall live by Me. . . He that eateth this bread shall live forever." Sanctifying grace is spiritual life. Therefore these promises of Christ assure us that 

Communion brings sanctifying grace and all virtues necessary to salvation into our souls.

These promises are full of assurance that the worthy reception of Holy Communion is a pledge of everlasting life. They also certify to us that the sacrament fits our bodies for a glorious resurrection, especially when we consider this text in John vi, 55: "He that eateth My flesh, and drinketh 

My blood hath everlasting life: and I will raise him up in the last day."

One of the strong proofs of our doctrine that the celebration of the Holy Eucharist continues the sacrifice of the Cross in the Church is this text from St. Paul: "For as often as you shall eat this bread, and drink the chalice, you shall show the death of the Lord until He come" (I Cor. xi, 26). 

Words could not be plainer.

In order to make a good Communion, we must be in the state of sanctifying grace. "Whosoever," says St. Paul (I Cor. xi, 27-28-29),

PAGE 

"shall eat this bread, or drink the chalice of the Lord unworthily, shall be guilty of the body and the blood of the Lord. But let a man prove himself: and so let him eat of that bread, and drink of the chalice. For he that eateth and drinketh unworthily eateth and drinketh judgment to himself, not discerning the body of the Lord." By proving one's self, the 

Apostle means making sure that we are in the state of grace. He who goes to Communion in mortal sin receives the body and blood of Christ but does not receive His grace, and he commits a great sacrilege. 

If we are in the state of only venial sin, we are permitted to receive Communion, even though we should still be unrepentant for that sin. But to receive in this state will keep us from gaining grace as plentifully as we would if we were free from all affection to venial sin. If we are sorry for venial sin, even though it should be only imperfect contrition, the reception of Communion will wipe out the sin. We can, however, make ourselves free of this sin before approaching the Communion rail if we join imperfect contrition to the use of some sacramental, for instance, to making the sign of the cross.

The Church encourages us to prepare for Communion by making acts of lively faith, firm hope, and ardent charity. Practically all prayer books have special departments devoted to Communion prayers. But there is no better preparation than to follow the priest in the Ordinary of the Mass, which is the liturgical preparation for Communion. 

We are expected to fast from the previous midnight when we receive Communion. This fast is broken by taking anything after the mode of food or drink. It would, therefore, break the fast if you took some medicine after midnight. But it would not break it, if, while you were washing your teeth or rinsing out your mouth, some liquid accidentally went down. your throat, as this liquid would not be taken in the manner of drink. Nor would it break the fast if, by accident, a rain drop were to go into your mouth and be swallowed.

Anyone in danger of death can receive Communion without fasting. 

Likewise the ill who have been confined to bed for a month and have no sure hope of a quick recovery can, with the prudent advice of a confessor, receive Communion once or twice a week after they have taken medicine or some liquid food. If I knew that somebody was to shoot me within the next hour or if I were to be put in real danger of death in any other way, I could receive without fasting. When in danger of death, I must receive if possible, under pain of sin. It is not supposed, of course, that this command refers to the danger involved in taking an ordinary airplane or automobile journey or to any of those occasions when the peril is fairly remote.

Another time when you could receive Communion without fasting would be if the Blessed Sacrament were in danger and you consumed It to prevent Its violation, as, for instance, when a church was going to be robbed.

We mortally sin if we do not receive Holy Communion within the Easter time, which in the United States extends from the first Sunday of Lent to Trinity Sunday.

The oftener we receive, the better it is for us. Except when a serious cause like danger of death comes up, you are allowed to receive only once a day, but the Church recommends daily Communion. If you cannot receive

PAGE 

daily, then why not several times a week? If that is inconvenient, then why not monthly? At least do not put it off more than a month. Yearly is the lowest limit consistent with a Catholic life. 

That should be a

This is the Sacrament of God Himself comes into you in Communion. sufficient argument for frequent reception. 

Sacraments. 

A priest is ordinarily allowed to offer only one Mass a day. If necessity or the great convenience of the people requires this, he can binate, that is, offer two Masses, on Sundays or other holy days of obligation. Sometimes priests get permission to trinate on such days, particularly under missionary conditions. On All Souls' day and Christmas, each priest may celebrate three Masses.

By the mere fact of his priesthood, a clergyman is not compelled to offer Mass more than several times a year. Most priests celebrate daily. 

Many are compelled by the routine of their duty, in parish or institutional life, to offer Mass every day. Few would care to miss it, especially in our difficult age.

PAGE 

 XXXIII WHY LATIN AND RITUALISM ARE USED IN OUR CATHOLIC SERVICES 

The Church, for the most part, uses a dead language in celebrating Mass. One reason is because a dead tongue is not subject to change in vocabulary or meaning. Doctors, for the same reason, write their prescriptions in Latin, in all civilized countries. Various languages are used at the present time in celebrating Mass-not Latin alone as many non-Catholics think. They include: Latin, Greek, Syriac, Chaldaic, Aramaic, Ethiopian, Hungarian, Ruthenian, Bulgarian, Armenian, Coptic, and Roumanian. The 

Roman Mass was celebrated in Czech at a special service in Czechoslovakia in 1919. It has also been permitted in our day (not regularly) in the vernacular of Jugoslavia. The Roumanian language is the chief living tongue used by a large group (Byzantine rite). The Ruthenian, or Old Slavonic (Staroslav), is, however, so close to the common language of the people that they have no difficulty following it. A small group uses the Georgian tongue in the 

Byzantine rite and Arabic, the language of the people, is interspersed in some of the Eastern rites. Limited concessions for the use of the vernacular in parts of the Latin Mass (for instance, the Gospel) have been made in our time in Jugoslavia.

We have heard of the Eucharistic liturgy's being offered in English according to the Byzantine rite, but never in public. A group of Hungarians, represented in the United States, uses the vernacular of the mother country in the Mass according to the Byzantine rite, their ancestors having introduced this innovation at a time when not to do so would mean loss of members.

In The tendency in the Latin rite is strongly against the use of the vernacular. Various attempts to introduce it have been dismissed. the Byzantine rite, the tendency towards the vernacular has been more marked, but the use of the vulgar tongue seems to be only tolerated, not encouraged. The United States has two large dioceses of the Byzantine rite the Ukrainian Greeks, with 290,688 members in 1940, and the Greek 

Diocese of Pittsburgh, with 258,895 in 1940. In time, an American Greek rite, using the English language, may develop among these people. 

In his book, The Catholic Eastern Churches, Donald Attwater says (p. 34): "There is no principle against vernacular languages in the Catholic 

Church and many Easterns assist at the Liturgy in their daily tongue; others understand the language used rather better, I imagine, than we understand the English of Chaucer, and a minority make use of a quite dead language."

As we pointed out, however, there is a strong tendency in the Latin Church against the use of the vernacular. Perhaps the best argument in favor of 

English in the Mass is that, with the use of a microphone at the altar, and with good enunciation on the part of the priest, it would be possible for the people to follow the service with ease. It cannot be denied, however, that there is a deep dignity and air of mysticism about the Mass in 

Latin. We do not favor a change.

An attractive feature about the Mass in several of the Oriental rites is that the language is the one Christ Himself used the Aramaic. 

Known also as Syriac, this language is used by the Syrian rite, the Maronite rite, the Malankarese rite, the Chaldean rite, and the Malabarese rite. It

PAGE differs a little from rite to rite, but principally in pronunciation. The 

Chaldaic language differs little from the Syriac. 

The chief divisions of the Oriental rites are as follows: Alexandrian, including the Copts and the Ethiopians; the Antiochene, including the 

Syrians, the Maronites, and the Malankarese; the Armenian, confined to the Armenian nation and emigrants; the Byzantine, including the Bulgars, the Greeks, the Hungarians, the Italo-Greek-Albanians, the Jugoslavs, the Melkites, the Roumanians, the Russians, the Ukrainians, and the Ruthenians; the Chaldean, including the Chaldeans (descendants of converted Nestorians), and the Malabarese of India. There are more than 8,000,000 of these people altogether. The United States has perhaps 700,000 to 800,000 of them, some attending services not in their own churches but in Latin churches.

The use of Latin in the Mass first became common in Northern Africa, whence it spread to Rome in the third century. Greek was the origi nal liturgical language in Rome. Greek chapels can still be seen the catacombs. It is probably no more than an accident of history that Latin became by far the dominant language and rite in the Church. Were it not for the Greek schism, it could as readily have been Greek. There is an ancient opinion that Mass was celebrated in Latin from the beginning (men tioned by Benedict XIV De Missa ii, cap. 2). When Hebrew is spoken of as one of the original languages of the Mass, Aramaic is meant, the tongue. of the Jewish people in Christ's day (closely related to the Hebrew). "The Church cannot be said to use, or even to prefer, any one language," asserts the Addis & Arnold Catholic Dictionary. "She requires some of her clergy to use Greek, Syriac, Coptic, Armenian, Slavonic in the Mass just as strictly as she requires others to employ Latin." 

But so

The use of a non-living language is a development in the Church, which could be done away with at any time if the Pope saw fit. many good results have followed the custom that it is unlikely it will ever be abolished. The laity have no difficulty following the Mass, even though it be said in Latin or another dead language, because their prayerbooks furnish a translation side by side with the dead language. Since most of the service is in an undertone or in singing (singing on the part of the priest occurs only in High or Solemn Masses), the people could not follow it readily without a prayerbook anyhow, even if it were in the popular tongue. 

Originally the Mass was said in at least two languages-Aramaic or Syriac and Greek (maybe more)--according to where the Apostles and their immediate successors worked. The sacrifice was essentially the same, but the language and ceremonies differed somewhat. Different "rites," perpetuated to this day, thereby grew up in the Church. In Eastern Europe and Western Asia, the ceremonies differ considerably from those of our Latin priests, and the priests are under a different discipline. For instance, married men can be ordained priests in the Eastern rites. But there is absolutely no difference in doctrine. 

SS. Cyril and Methodius, the Apostles to the Slavs, in the ninth century, finding that it was difficult to convert these people while using Greek or Latin services, translated the Greek services into Old Slavonic, then the living tongue of the people, and the Slav rites of today are the result. 

Rome upheld the two saints in their work, which caused somewhat of a stir, and thus let it be known to the centuries that the Church stands ready

PAGE 

at any time to make such disciplinary changes when the salvation of souls requires them. If it would be better to abolish Latin as the language of the Mass, Rome could always do it. But under present conditions it is more useful to use a dead language, and it probably will be more useful until the end of time. Our services are so similar that Latin priests from 

China, Germany, South America, and the United States could all say Mass one after the other in some Canadian city, and the Canadians would not be able to see any difference from the ceremony to which they were accustomed. No other Church can prove her universality so well as this. 

Sometimes objection is made by non-Catholics to the ritualism of the Mass. These same persons, while refusing to use rites of any kind in their churches, go to lodge meetings and use all sorts of ritual exercises there. 

Take for instance the Masons, the Odd Fellows, and other societies made up exclusively of non-Catholics, including many ministers who would not even consent to wear a vestment while preaching. Yet in their society meetings, they robe themselves and make their degree work as impressive as possible. Ask them why they do it, and they will immediately reply: "It helps us to teach our lessons, and makes the candidate or member realize that he is in a different atmosphere than that of his daily life.” 

This is exactly why the Catholic Church uses vestments and ritualism in her services. She wants the people to forget the world when they come into her temples, and she tries to raise their thoughts to spiritual matters. 

Since every bit of man's knowledge must come through the senses, she appeals to him through his sight, smelling, hearing, and in every legitimate way. If it be argued that Christ did not go around with mitre and crozier, we answer that He most emphatically did indulge in ritualism when performing miracles, and that He constantly went to the Jewish Temple, where vestments, elaborate rites, candles, incense, etc., were used at the express command of God. Remember that the Jewish Church was not officially done away with during His lifetime. The Catholic Church was formally established on Pentecost Sunday. Christ laid the foundation for Catholicity, but it was a matter of conscience for His followers to live up to the Mosaic rites during His lifetime. These facts are patent to every Bible reader. 

Also let us remember that the Christ of the Apocalypse is the same as the Christ of the Gospels, and that this last book of the Scriptures revels in a liturgical spirit.

It was natural that the ceremonies in Apostolic days should be more simple than those today. If we were driven to Mass in underground channels, where there was constant danger that armed soldiers, bent on massacre, might rush in at any moment, we could not have the detailed ceremonies we now use. But anybody who examines the Roman catacombs will have to admit that early Christianity was in essence a ritual religion. 

Each ceremony of the Mass has a meaning that carries its lesson to the worshiper. Popular books can be obtained fully explaining the Mass rites.

In at least one rite, the Armenian, no Missals or prayer books are required, as the deacon either chants the secret parts said by the priest or explains these parts to the people while the priest is engaged in saying the secret prayers at the altar. He also directs both the priest and the people in what to do. Just before the Gospel, for instance, he asks the people to stand up. He chants the Credo, the people following him. Before the

PAGE 

Consecration, he admonishes the people to be attentive, and then turns to the priest, censes him, and asks him to proceed. The classical or ancient 

Armenian is understood by all educated Armenians its advantage over the vernacular, like the Latin, is that, being a "dead" language, it does not change and is the same the world over. An Armenian from Russia and one from Turkey, both claiming to speak "pure" Armenian, would have difficulty understanding each other; but in the church, at Mass, they are both at home-the celebrant may be from either Russia or Turkey. Latin does this service for the Western Church.

PAGE 

 XXXIV CONFESSION OF SINS PROVED TO BE OF DIVINE COMMAND 

Penance is a sacrament that remits sin committed after Baptism and restores the sinner to friendship with God by means of the absolution of a duly authorized priest. To receive the sacrament worthily, five things are ordinarily necessary: We must examine our conscience; have sorrow for our sins; make a firm resolution never to offend God again; confess our sins to the priest; and accept the penance the priest gives to us. 

Sometimes, as in the case of an unconscious person who is dying, or when a great number of men are about to enter battle, absolution is given without Confession. If the person lives, however, he is bound to make the Confession later.

That the priest has the power to forgive sins is set forth in the Bible. 

Following His resurrection, Jesus said to His Apostles, after breathing upon them: "Receive ye the Holy Ghost. Whose sins you shall forgive, they are forgiven; and whose sins you shall retain, they are retained" (John xx, 21, 23).

Before that time, He had given them the power of binding and loosing in a way that would be effective in the sight of God. To Peter, He said: "And I will give to thee the keys of the kingdom of heaven. And whatsoever thou shalt bind on earth, it shall be bound also in heaven: and whatsoever thou shalt loose on earth, it shall be loosed also in heaven" (Matt. xvi, 19). Hence the clergyman acting in God's name in the confessional is said to exercise the "power of the keys." This same power of binding and loosing was given to the other Apostles as well (Matt. xviii, 18). 

Unless a priest knows the sins that the penitent has committed, how can he know what is to be bound or loosed? The texts given above show that he has power as a judge. A judge must know the facts before he can pass upon them. The Church does not leave it to the whim of the priest whether he is to give absolution or not, but regulates him by strict rules; but he must know the facts. 

Confession is difficult. Nobody likes it. The priests find that hearing the sins of penitents is the hardest work they must do. To sit for hours in a little cooped-up place, hearing the whispered Confessions of the evil done by hundreds, is about as disagreeable a task as might be imagined. 

Some people outside the Church declare that the confessional is a place of corruption. How in the name of common sense could you make 

350,000,000 people of every grade of intellectuality and of every nationality go to Confession regularly, and send their beloved ones, even their small children, if they knew from personal experience that it was corrupt? 

Every one of us is able to bear witness that we are kept purer, more honest, and in every way helped in virtue by the confessional. Its social value is incalculable.

Confession of Sins Demanded of Jews Living Under Ancient Mosaic Law 

Confession of sins was not a new thing in the Christian Church. It was specifically commanded by the Mosaic law. In Numbers v, 5, 6, and 

7, we find: “And the Lord spoke to Moses saying: Say to the children of

Israel: When a man or woman shall have committed any of all the sins

PAGE that men are wont to commit, and by negligence shall have transgressed the commandment of the Lord, and offended, they shall confess their sin, and restore the principal itself, and the fifth part over and above, to him against whom they have sinned." Could anything be plainer? Christ built His sacrament on an old custom just as He did in Baptism. 

The Apostles and early Christians practiced Confession. 

Acts xix, 18, says: "And many of them that believed came confessing and declaring their deeds." St. James v, 16, says: "Confess, therefore, your sins one to another: and pray for one another, that you may be saved."

When a part of the Catholic Church falls into heresy, it often happens that Confession is thrown overboard, with fasting, celibacy of the clergy, abstinence, and everything else difficult. 

We admit that it is hard, sometimes, to be a Catholic. It is anything but easy to confess one's sins when one has seriously strayed off the narrow path. But there is nothing on earth that quite equals the feeling when you come out after a sincere Confession, and feel deep in your heart that your soul is genuinely clean again! The experience has to be gone through in order to have its value realized. People outside the Church might think they are really happy at times; but there is no happiness quite like that which comes from Confession, Communion, and the other Catholic sacraments. 

History and Urging of Human Nature Uphold Catholic 

Practice of Confession 

Sometimes the charge is advanced that Confession was not found in the ancient Church, but was a late innovation. Common sense should settle this controversy, but documentary evidence can also be adduced in favor of our side. Confession is difficult, for both priests and people. Nobody with the use of reason who has sinned seriously after Baptism is exempt from it, if it is physically possible. Even the Pope and the Cardinals must go to Confession. Mere human authority would never be strong enough to force such an establishment on the people. It had to come from 

God. Consequently we declare that common sense should settle the controversy. Having had experience as a Father Confessor myself, and having learned how hard this task is from the priest's viewpoint, I seriously suspect that the reason why rebellious clergy who separate from the Church sometimes abolish Confession is as much to save themselves from labor as to please the weak people. "Up to the time of the so-called Reformation," says the Pohle-Preuss work on "The Sacraments," vol. iii, page 21, "no one ever questioned the power of the Church to forgive sins." 

The following quotation from the "Didache," a document of about 

A. D. 96, is enlightening: "In the church thou shalt confess thy transgressions, and thou shalt not betake thyself to prayer with an evil conscience." 

St. Polycarp, a personal disciple of the Apostle John; St. Irenaeus and

Tertullian, in the second century; St. Cyprian and Origen, in the third century; St. Pacian of Barcelona, Lactantius, St. Basil, in the fourth century: 

St. Leo the Great, St. Augustine, and St. Chrysostom, in the fifth century, are quoted, among other authorities, as witnesses of Confession. Attempts by non-Catholic scholars to set definite dates for the establishment of the sacrament by the Church have been answered by our theologians with proofs that settle the dispute. The reader is referred to the Pohle-Preuss work mentioned above.

PAGE  

One of the strong arguments from history in favor of the statement that, previous to the Reformation, the power of the Church to forgive sins was never questioned is found in the fact that the Oriental separated Churches practice Confession to this very day and have always had it since they left the obedience to Rome, just as before. The defection of some of these Christians occurred fifteen centuries ago. There is a natural urge in man to want to confess his misdeeds. few years ago, I was told by an Oblate of Mary Immaculate, a missionary priest, of an experience he had while coming to the United States from Alaska. A severe storm arising, the ship was in serious danger of being lost. There were only two Catholics on board, besides the priest, and of course they went to Confession, to be safe. Then every single man on that ship came to the clergyman and made some sort of informal confession revealing serious sins of his past life! There was a natural impulse in them to confess, and it came to the surface in the time of danger. 

What Is Necessary to Make Ready for Good Confession 

By examination of conscience before Confession we mean an earnest effort to bring back to mind all the sins we have committed, at least since our last worthy Confession. We can do this by recalling the Ten Commandments, the precepts of the Church, the seven capital sins, and the particular duties of our state in life. Daily examination of conscience is recommended, except in the case of scrupulous persons who make out to themselves that things are sins when they are not sinful. Before an examination of conscience we should pray for light to know our sins and to detest them.

Contrition, or sorrow for sin, a requisite of a good Confession, is a hatred of sin and a true grief of the soul for having offended God, with the firm purpose of sinning no more. We are promised time and again in the Bible that God will not despise the contrite of heart. 

We should feel a sorrow that is interior, supernatural, universal, and sovereign. By interior sorrow, we mean that it should be from the heart. 

To be supernatural, it should be prompted by the grace of God and excited by motives that spring from faith, not by merely natural motives. It is possible to excite ourselves to supernatural sorrow by prayer, by considering the infinite goodness of God, the sufferings of His Divine Son, the loss of heaven, and the punishments of hell.

By universal sorrow, we mean sorrow for all our mortal sins without exception. Sorrow for venial sins is also necessary if we wish pardon for them. When a person goes to Confession and can remember nothing sinful that he has done since the last Confession, he should tell some sin from his past life, committed after Baptism. The oftener a sin is confessed in this way, the more temporal punishment owed for it is removed. It is a good habit to confess such sins even when we have fresh sins also to tell. By sovereign sorrow for sin, we mean our grief should be caused more by having offended God than by any other evil that can befall us. 

Sin is the greatest evil that can happen to us. The greatness of an offense must be gauged not only by itself, but also by the station of him against whom it is directed. Sin is directed against God, who is Infinite 

Goodness. When it is mortal, it shuts us out of heaven and condemns

PAGE us to the eternal pains of hell. Hence it will be seen why sorrow for sin is necessary.

Two Kinds of Contrition Recognized; What Is Necessary for Confession

There are two kinds of contrition-perfect and imperfect. Perfect contrition is sorrow and hatred of sin because it offends God, who is infinitely good in Himself and worthy of all love. It remits sin immediately, not waiting until Confession, although a Catholic, in order to have it, must have at least the implicit intention of going to Confession. Biblical sanction for it is found in Christ's forgiveness of the woman guilty of many sins who now "loved much" (Luke vii), and in other Scriptural references. 

Frequent acts of perfect contrition should be made. No special words are necessary, although they help in bringing it about. From this doctrine, it can be seen, no sinner is suffering the torments of hell unless he richly deserves to be there. Particularly in the hour of death, or when in danger, we should make an act of perfect contrition. It is a holy practice to say some short prayer, like "God be merciful unto me a sinner," many times a day. Perfect love of God has the same effect as perfect contrition. 

Imperfect contrition is that by which we hate what offends God because through it we lose heaven and deserve hell, or because sin is so hateful in itself. Imperfect contrition is called attrition. It looks rather to God, the punisher, than to the God of Love.

Imperfect contrition (attrition) is sufficient for a worthy Confession, but we should endeavor to have perfect contrition. Perfect contrition brings us more graces and immediately remits sin of itself. Attrition must have absolution or another sacrament with it to remit mortal sin. 

We would not have imperfect contrition if we had a purely servile fear of God. By a purely servile fear, we mean the type that would make us avoid sin only because of God's punishment, with the feeling that, if there were no punishment, we would be willing to go ahead sinning regardless of the laws of God. 

Contrition, whether perfect or imperfect, must be based on motives that spring from faith and not on purely natural motives. 

To make a good Confession, we must have a firm purpose of sinning mortally no more, and of avoiding the near occasions of mortal sin. By near or approximate occasions of sin, we mean all persons, places, or things that might easily make us sin. It is to be feared that many a soul has been damned through being careless about keeping proper company or about remaining away from obscene shows. 

We are bound to confess all our mortal sins, but it is well also to confess our venial ones. 

A good Confession must be humble, sincere, and entire. A humble

Confession is one in which we accuse ourselves of our sins with a deep sense of shame and sorrow. A sincere Confession is one in which we tell our sins correctly, without exaggeration or excuse. It is entire when we tell the number and kind of our mortal sins, with the circumstances that involve new sins, and omit no sin. If we cannot remember the number, we should give it as nearly as we can. If we deliberately conceal a mortal sin, we tell a lie to the Holy Ghost and make a worthless Confession which, in itself, is a grievous sin. The fact that we have made a bad Confession

PAGE  

must be confessed itself in the next Confession, and all mortal sins committed since the last good Confession must be told. If without our fault we forget to confess a mortal sin in a good Confession, it is forgiven, but should be told in Confession later, if it comes to mind. 

Examine your conscience well before you enter the confessional. 

Remember that the priest's time is usually far more valuable than yours. 

The Seal of the Confessional makes it impossible, under pain of mortal sin and under extremely severe penalties from the Church, for a priest directly to divulge what he has heard in Confession. Even indirect telling of these sins is forbidden, and the Church goes so far as to advise priests to keep from discussing cases among themselves even when there is no danger that the identity of the sinner might be found out, unless it might be an intricate problem where discussion is necessary to arrive at a conclusion and the penitent himself has given permission for it. Even in these cases, the identity of the sinner is not revealed. So far does the Seal of the 

Confessional go that a Father Confessor who was named head of a religious order would not be permitted to use the knowledge gained exclusively through the confessional in governing the order. So you need not have the least hesitancy in confessing to any priest. He dare not act any differently towards you than he would if he had not learned your secrets in Confession. If a layman happens to overhear another's Confession, he does not dare, under penalty of grievous sin, to tell it or hint at it in any way whatsoever. He must be prepared, like a priest, to give up his life rather than make a sin known.

Some of the most glorious martyrs in the Church were martyrs to the Seal of Confession, and just in the last century a priest of Kiev served twenty years' slavery in Siberia rather than tell the secret of a man who committed a murder, confessed to him, and then threw suspicion on the priest. The man told of his awful crime on his death-bed, but, when the authorities went to release the priest, God had just called his heroic soul to its reward. 

What Sins Have to Be Told to Make Our 

Confessions Good

The Council of Trent defined that all the mortal sins committed after Baptism must be told in Confession. It is not absolutely necessary, therefore, for you to tell anything but mortal sins. But it is recommended that you tell venial sins also, as they cannot be forgiven without sorrow and it is easier to work up sorrow for them if you confess; the advice you receive from your Confessor may also be of great value. The practice conduces to humility and other virtues. Everything that we are sure is mortally sinful, and that has never been confessed, must be submitted to the priest, even though it has been forgotten in a previous Confession. Yet we warn the scrupulous that if they have been forbidden to go beyond past Confessions they must obey their Confessor.

It is not lawful ordinarily to make a general accusation of sinfulness, without going into the species of infamy we may have perpetrated. The priest is a judge in the confessional, and he must know the case on which he is to pass. "Whose sins you shall forgive, they are forgiven, and whose sins you shall retain, they are retained," said Christ. These words clearly indicated that the Confession was to be in the form of a judgment. 

If a penitent cannot remember the exact number of mortal sins he has committed, he should give the number approximately. If he finds out

PAGE afterwards that he has committed more than could be reasonably covered by the approximation he made, he should tell these extra sins the next time he goes to Confession.

When it comes to the confessing of doubtful mortal sins, the following rules should be followed: If the penitent is not well instructed, he should tell the sins; if he is not given to grave sins, and particularly if he is scrupulous, he is not held to confess the doubtful sins; if he has a medium conscience, neither lax nor timorous, he is not indeed obliged to confess the sins, but is encouraged to do so, for advice and for tranquillity of conscience; if he has a lax conscience, or is given to excusing himself too readily, he generally ought to declare the sins, because the presumption is against him.

If you diligently examine your conscience, and the doubt about whether the sin is mortal keeps up, you are not compelled to confess it if you do not wish to do so, unless you are lax. The Council of Trent defined that we are obliged to confess mortal sins of which we have consciousness. 

But we cannot be said to have such consciousness of a sin that we are not sure is mortal.

In order to receive absolution, you must confess some real sin, whether it be venial or mortal. Penitents who frequently go to the sacrament sometimes accuse themselves only of doubtful matter, like outbursts of temper that were perhaps involuntary. Persons such as this should always accuse themselves of some sin of their past life. If the sin has been confessed before, it is not necessary to go into detail about it again. For instance, the penitent might confess that he has been given to sinning against a certain Commandment. He would not need to tell exactly what the sin 

The details that the priest needed to know were confessed before. 

If a person has only venial sins to confess, he must have the purpose of making special efforts to avoid at least one of them in the future. God will not forgive any sin, mortal or venial, unless we have true contrition for it. But Confession, although less useful, is not invalidated if we are not truly contrite over one or more venial sins, so long as we repent some sin. 

Not Necessary to Carry Written List of Sins With You to Make Confession was.

Occasionally one comes across Catholics, particularly children, who write out their sins on a piece of paper in order that they will not forget them, and carry the paper with them to Confession-then perhaps they lose the paper afterwards, to their shame. They get into this practice through the advice of scrupulous elders. Nobody has to go to this extent to make sure of the integrity of his Confession. Even a mute person is not compelled to write out his sins, for writing is an extraordinary medium. The deaf are often able to speak and then must make their Confessions in the ordinary way. But, if they cannot speak, they make the priest understand as best they can by signs, and this suffices, just as it would suffice to make signs if you were caught in a strange land where you did not know the language, and where you could not find a priest who knew yours, and it was necessary to go to Confession, as to make your Easter duty or if you were in danger of death.* Many deaf mutes bring their Confessions written *Fear of the spiritual danger of remaining in mortal sin would be sufficient reason for making such a Confession.

PAGE 

to priests, and from pious motives the practice can be encouraged, but they are not actually compelled to confess in this way if they do not wish. 

It is better for normal children not to get into the habit, as they too often lose the papers.

Diligent examination of conscience is required before Confession, but the same amount is not exacted for all. Five to fifteen minutes will be found sufficient for the average penitent, depending, of course, on the frequency of Confession. If you are one of the unfortunates who do not go to the sacraments oftener than once a year, you ought to spend about an hour examining yourself. 

If you know that you have committed no mortal sins since your last

Confession, you are not bound under pain of sin to examine your conscience at all unless you wish, or unless you need to think of some sin to get the necessary matter for Confession (absolution cannot be given unless at least a real venial sin, either from your past life or since your last Confession, is told). It is advisable, however, to think over your venial sins, to arouse sorrow for them and to humble yourself. It is also advisable, though not compulsory, to confess them.

If you are scrupulous, and a priest has made you seem to quit examining your conscience at all before Confession, or has made you cut down the time of examination to a minimum, do not decide that he does not know his theology and is not a safe guide. He knows what he is talking about or he would not give you such an order.

If sick people are too ill to make what they deem a proper examination, they should not worry. God does not expect the impossible and He knew that there would be many sick in the world when He instituted the sacrament of Penance.

If a person has used a written list of sins in making his Confession, and we find it afterwards, we are bound not to reveal its contents. 

Our Duty in Regard to Saying the Penance 

Given in Confession

The penance given to us by the priest in Confession is expected by the Church to be vindictive, medicinal, fitting, and salutary. The priest is obliged under pain of grievous sin to inflict a serious penance if the sins told to him have been mortal (see exceptions below). The amount of satisfaction to be required is left largely to his prudence and judgment, but the Church expects him to give a penance that will bring the sinner to a realization that sin is not something trifling. If the sinner is ill, the priest can make the penance light, or even omit it altogether if necessary; but the clergy are usually obliged to give some penance, to be fulfilled when the patient is able. If the penitent would be likely not to fulfill a grave obligation in the priest's opinion, or appeared exceptionally contrite, or if it were a time of jubilee and plenary indulgence, or if a serious penance might make Confession odious, the penance could be made lighter than on other occasions. The moral theologians advise priests against giving penances that are exceptionally severe, like saying prayers daily for a year; hence you are not at all likely to get satisfaction of this kind to perform. 

Penitents are held to accept reasonable penances put upon them, and to fulfill them within the time set, if a certain period has been prescribed. 

If grave penance has been imposed for grave sins, the obligation of fulfilling

PAGE it binds under penalty of mortal sin, unless the priest indicates otherwise. 

The penitent should not delay fulfilling his penance, for if he thus imperils it to being forgotten, or if it had medicinal value, i.e., was imposed with its curative value as one of the chief purposes in the priest's mind, a delay of two or three months would make a mortal sin, provided the penance were a grave one, imposed for grave sins.

If the penitent forgets his penance, he is not held to repeat the Confession to get a new penance. Nobody is able of his own accord to change his penance into something lighter. Nevertheless, if he finds the penance given to him practically impossible of fulfillment, he should get it changed either by the priest to whom he confessed or by another confessor. It is not necessary to repeat the former Confession to have this change made, but it is necessary to tell what the old penance is. If the second priest refuses to change the penance, the penitent is held to fulfill it, for this is evidence that it is not too difficult for him or her. 

If we have been unfortunate enough to make a bad Confession, or if we have been refused absolution, we are not held to fulfill the penance given unless it is medicinal or we are penitents going back several times to the same confessor in the same Confession, and he has been giving us certain penances for different sins, as might happen occasionally with persons getting corrected spiritually after being away from the sacraments for years.

If we have been given a grave penance for grave sins and deliberately omit a notable part of it, we sin gravely. Penance fulfilled in mortal sin satisfies the obligation, according to the more probable opinion, but it is certainly not to be expected that penitents will let the fulfillment of the satisfaction imposed go until they have lapsed from the state of grace. 

In determining what is mortally sinful, always remember that grave matter, sufficient reflection, and full consent of the will are necessary. 

If a person has made a bad Confession, he is compelled to repeat it, in order to get absolution. The Council of Trent ruled that every mortal sin committed after Baptism must be submitted in Confession; hence, if a mortal sin is forgotten in one Confession, it has to be told in the next, or when it is remembered. Telling it in Confession is called submitting it "to the power of the keys," and when a bad Confession is made it is not deemed that the sins told have been submitted to this power. If it should happen that the priest even confusedly remembered the sins told in the bad. 

Confession, a penitent would be able to get off by accusing himself of them again in general terms.

Timorous penitents who fear that they have been making bad Confessions for a long period are not held to repeat these Confessions if they have been in the habit of preparing themselves well, because the presumption stands for the validity of their Confessions. But, if there is grave reason to judge that the Confessions were bad, they must make all the Confessions over again. It sometimes happens that persons continue making bad Confessions for years. This is the most risky business imaginable, for a Confession that we know is bad is a mortal sin in itself, and it adds evil to evil. 

General Confession Advisable When We Enter Upon New State in Life 

To speak of correcting bad Confessions naturally brings up the subject

PAGE 

of general Confessions. General Confessions are necessary when past 

Confessions have been bad either through keeping back mortal sins or through lack of proper contrition, or when the Father Confessor lacked jurisdiction. A man must have both Holy Orders and jurisdiction from the proper authorities to hear Confessions. Penitents as a rule need not worry about whether the Confessor has jurisdiction, for, if they go to him in good faith and he does not happen to have it, the Church supplies it. 

But if they knew that he did not have it, the Confession would have to be repeated.

General Confessions are to be recommended at long intervals, even when we are not worried about past Confessions, because they add to humility and other virtues. But they can do more harm than good in scrupulous persons and are not to be recommended for them as a rule. 

Although they are not absolutely necessary, they are to be recommended in most cases before a person is received into a religious order, is married, or takes some other important step in life. If the General Confession has not been rendered necessary because of past Confessions that were not valid, the penitent is not obliged to tell every mortal sin he has committed, but needs to make it in full only since the last Confession. Nor need he worry afterwards if he has forgotten some mortal sins that were told in previous Confessions.

If you want to make a General Confession, speak to the priest about it in the confessional beforehand, and arrange to go at a convenient time, when you will not keep many other persons waiting. 

Priest Under Strict Obligation When He 

Hears Confession 

Nearly all Catholics are well aware of how strict the Church is in demanding that, when a layman comes to Confession, he enter into the work seriously, seeing to it that his Confession be integral and that he have the right sorrow and be prepared to perform the penance that will be imposed. 

But it does not occur to some that the priest is under as strict an obligation as they are.

When a priest refuses to give you absolution, he is not satisfying some whim, but is acting in this way to save himself from a serious sin. If he saw that you were not rightly disposed, he would not dare to give you absolution, for fear of committing a sacrilege. Just as you would commit a sacrilege and gain nothing but more guilt if you purposely kept from telling a mortal sin that you had committed since your last Confession, so he would be committing one if he saw that you were not sorry for your sins and he nevertheless gave you absolution. So, if you are ever refused absolution, do not blame the priest, but yourself. Consider it as a warning coming from God, for Christ established the sacrament that the priest has the power to administer and will hold the priest to a strict accounting for the way he has administered it.

A study of moral theology reveals that every reasonable opportunity is given the sinner to square up his accounts with God. But the sinner himself must show the proper dispositions, and, if he does not do so, he will find that nobody will force him into heaven. 

How far the Church goes in helping her children is proved by the fact that not only at the moment of death, but even when one is in danger

PAGE of death, if another priest cannot be secured, an heretical, apostate, excommunicated, degraded, or irregular priest is permitted to give one absolution. 

Both Holy Orders and jurisdiction are needed to administer the sacrament, and the Church supplies the jurisdiction in these extreme cases. 

Christ, she is a strict teacher but a merciful one. Like

There is no sin that God will refuse to forgive if the sinner is penitent. 

Christ's Redemption was more than sufficient to wipe away all sin. But, under the Divine economy, we are made co-operators in our own salvation. 

Mere faith alone is not sufficient to save us, for, as the Bible says, as the body without the soul is dead, so also faith without good works is dead (James ii, 17, 20, 26). Salvation is not forced down our throats, but it is waiting for all who are willing to avail themselves of the means Christ established for us to obtain it. 

Purpose of Penances in Confession 

The priest, after hearing our sins in Confession, gives us penance to perform, that we may satisfy God for all or part of the temporal punishment due for the sins. The penance usually consists of some prayers to be said or some good deed to be done. Temporal punishment, when due, must be undergone either on earth or in purgatory. It must be paid off even in hell, where it is added to the eternal punishment. It is called temporal punishment because there is an end to it at some time. When a person persists in mortal sin he undergoes eternal, as well as temporal, punishment for it.

That temporal punishment exists is proved by the curse placed on the race in the Garden of Eden (Gen. iii, 17); by the refusal of God to permit 

Moses to lead the Hebrews into the Promised Land, because the prophet had sinned (see Deuteronomy iii); and by the clear example in II Kings xii, 13, 14: "And David said to Nathan: I have sinned against the Lord. 

And Nathan said to David: The Lord also hath taken away thy sin; thou shalt not die. Nevertheless, because thou hast given occasion to the enemies of the Lord to blaspheme, for this thing, the child that is born to thee shall surely die."

The sacrament of Penance remits the eternal punishment due for sin, but does not always remit the temporal punishment. God requires this temporal punishment .as a satisfaction for sin, to teach us the great evil of sin, and to prevent us from falling again. The chief means by which we satisfy God for the temporal punishment due for sin are prayer, fasting, almsgiving, all spiritual and corporal works of mercy, and the patient suffering of the ills of life. We get rid of or cut down this punishment through indulgences. Great intensity of our sorrow in Confession reduces or removes the temporal punishment. 

The chief spiritual works of mercy are seven in number: To admonish the sinner, to instruct the ignorant, to counsel the doubtful, to comfort the sorrowful, to bear wrongs patiently, to forgive all injuries, and to pray for the living and the dead. The seven corporal works of mercy are to feed the hungry, to give drink to the thirsty, to clothe the naked, to ransom the captive, to harbor the homeless, to visit the sick, and to bury the dead. The former are called spiritual works of mercy because they refer to the soul; the latter corporal, because they refer to the body.

PAGE 

 Indulgences 

An indulgence is the remission in whole or in part of the temporal punishment due for sin. That the Church has the right to grant indulgences is proved by the text in Matt. xvi, 19: "Whatsoever thou shalt loose upon earth, it shall be loosed also in heaven." 

St. Paul in II Cor. ii, 10, grants an indulgence in these words: "And to whom you have pardoned everything, I also. For what I have pardoned, if I have pardoned anything, for your sakes have I done it in the person of Christ." This pardon was granted to an incestuous man who had been put under penance by the Apostle. 

An indulgence is not a pardon for sin in the sense that it wipes away the sin itself, nor is it a license to commit sin, as some non-Catholics believe. 

Its end is to wipe away the temporal punishment due for sin. One who is in the state of mortal sin cannot gain an indulgence. 

There are two kinds of indulgences-plenary and partial. A plenary indulgence is the full remission of the temporal punishment due for sin. 

To gain a plenary indulgence, when it is said to be granted "under the usual conditions," it is necessary to receive the sacraments of Penance and Holy Eucharist, to fulfill the specific conditions laid down (like recitation of the Prayer Before the Crucifix) and to pray for the intentions of the Pope. 

The Sacred Penitentiary in a decision of July 5, 1930, established a minimum of one Our Father, one Hail Mary, and one Glory be to the Father to be said in these instances. Other prayers may be substituted, provided they are recited orally. Mental recitation of prayers for indulgences does not fulfill the prescription. The person attempting to gain the indulgence must have at least a general intention of praying for the Pope's intentions. 

The following intentions remain constant: The progress of the faith and the triumph of the Church, peace and union among princes and rulers, the conversion of sinners, and the uprooting of heresy. These intentions need not be recalled in particular to gain the indulgence. 

A partial indulgence is the remission of a part of the temporal punishment due for sin.

The Church by means of indulgences remits the temporal punishment due for sin by applying to us the merits of Jesus Christ and the superabundant satisfactions of the Blessed Virgin and the saints. These merits and satisfactions are called the spiritual treasury of the Church. Here is one of the features of that "Communion of Saints" which, in the Apostles' Creed, we say we accept. The superabundant satisfactions of Mary and the saints were the many good things they did above what was needed for their salvation.

It is not easy to gain a plenary indulgence, for one must detest and have the purpose of avoiding as far as possible even the least venial sin. 

But if we strive for a plenary indulgence and do not gain it in whole, we can gain at least a partial indulgence. 

Bible in Many Places Shows That God Will Not Despise the Repentant

The Bible has many texts upholding the value of penance. "Unless you do penance, you shall all likewise perish," says Luke xiii, 3. Christ declared: "I say to you that there shall be joy in heaven upon one sinner that doth penance, more than upon ninety-nine just, who need not penance"

PAGE (Luke xv, 7). "Be penitent, and be converted, that your sins may be blotted out," says Acts v, 31. "The Lord delayeth not His promise, as some imagine, but dealeth patiently for your sake, not willing that any should perish, but that all should return to penance," says II Peter iii, 9.

The love of God for penitents is shown in many places in the Scriptures. "A sacrifice to God is an afflicted spirit: a contrite and humbled heart, O God, Thou wilt not despise," says Psalms 1, 19. "If your sins be as scarlet, they shall be white as wool," says Isaias i, 18. Jesus Christ, says 

I John ii, 2, "is the propitiation for our sins; and not for ours only, but also for those of the whole world." 

St. Paul refers to the power in the Church of forgiving sins as the ministry of reconciliation. In II Cor. v, 18, he says: "All things are of God, who hath reconciled us to Himself by Christ: and hath given to us the ministry of reconciliation."

PAGE 

 XXXV CATHOLIC LAWS ON MARRIAGE AND DIVORCE 

The Catholic attitude on marriage is not debatable for anyone who is really willing to live by the doctrine of the Scriptures. Here are some of the Biblical references to marriage: "Whilst her husband liveth she shall be called an adulteress if she be with another man" (Romans vii, 3). "Whosoever shall put away his wife and marry another committeth adultery against her" (Mark x, 11). "What God hath joined together let no man put asunder" (Matt. xix, 6). "Everyone that putteth away his wife, and marrieth another, committeth adultery: and he that marrieth her that is put away from her husband committeth adultery" (Luke xvi, 18). "To them that are married, not I, but the Lord commandeth, that the wife depart not from her husband: and if she depart, that she remain unmarried, or be reconciled to her husband. And let not the husband put away his wife (I Cor. vii, 10-11). "A woman is bound by the law as long as her husband liveth; but, if her husband die, she is at liberty: let her marry to whom she will: only in the Lord" (I Cor. vii, 39).

The reason why Christian marriage is monogamous is because Jesus has made it a figure of the union of Christ with His Church. The union of the Master with the Church is extraordinarily close. We are members of His body, of His flesh, and of His bones, to use the striking words of St. 

Paul in Ephesians. The Church is the bride of Christ. Marriage is defined in the Scriptures in this fashion: "A man shall leave father and mother, and shall cleave to his wife; and they shall be two in one flesh" (Genesis ii, 24). Christ merely restored marriage to the pristine condition God intended it to have from the very beginning. Because of the hardness of men's hearts, God permitted divorce to the Jews; but there is absolutely nothing in all the Christian dispensation that gives the Church permission to grant any divorce in Christian marriage with the right to remarry. 

All the loose legislation on divorce in the dissenting denominations rests upon the assumption that it is impossible to live a happy or decent single life. Separation has to come in some marriages. If a man is always abusive, a drunkard, or a libertine; or if a woman is an adulteress or a spendthrift who cannot manage a home; or if there are other grave reasons, a divorce without the right to remarry is permissible. But it is possible for these people to live both happily and virtuously without remarrying. 

The Catholic Church knows; she has had plenty of experience with celibates, far more than any other organization. God never commands what He does not give the strength to accomplish; there is no question about the fact that He has definitely commanded the Catholic legislation on Matrimony. 

Many arguments can be brought forward to show that, from a social welfare standpoint, the Catholic legislation is ideal. But the reason why we obey it is not particularly for these reasons, but because Christ commanded it. Our chief concern is to save souls. We are not running after mere earthly goals. We are not conducting a Church simply as a sociological experiment. We are glad, indeed, that Catholicity in action turns out to be the best sociology; but that is incidental. 

We obey Christ because we deem Him to be God. We are here for a brief space on probation, and then we are to be judged. Our happiness

PAGE or sorrow throughout eternity depends on the way we now obey Him. 

Hence the argument that we hold before the man or woman struggling with the question of obedience or disobedience to the Christian law of marriage comes down simply to this: Are you willing to jeopardize eternal happiness for a few brief years of tarnished happiness in this world? That, after all, is the only question that must be answered. 

Those who oppose our marriage legislation try to make out that the Church has no right to interfere in such delicate and purely personal matters. 

Inasmuch as Christ is God, and Christ said that she has, we side with Christ. 

Modern man did not make the world; God made it; God made the laws, natural and supernatural, by which we will be judged. Christ taught that we should center our attention on the next world, not on this. He did not make Christianity a worldly religion in any sense; He often declared that it is wholly opposed to the spirit of the world. He compared the living of a Christian life with His carrying of the Cross to Calvary. We do not promise easy salvation to anybody. It is far easier to damn one's soul than to save it; but, nevertheless, a sincere attempt to save one's soul brings such peace that Christ was able to call the yoke sweet and the burden light.

Matrimony is the sacrament that unites a Christian man and woman in lawful marriage.

God instituted marriage in the Garden of Eden (Gen. ii, 24) and 

Christ raised the contract among baptized people to the dignity of a sacrament.

The primary object of marriage is the procreation and education of offspring; the second purpose is mutual assistance and the remedy for concupiscence. Large numbers of people outside the Catholic Church put the secondary purpose above the first, and this explains why marriage is falling more and more into contempt among them. 

The essential qualities of marriage are unity and indissolubility, which in Christian marriage receive their peculiar firmness by reason of the sacrament.

St. Paul in Ephesians v tells what a Christian marriage should be like. "So also," he says, "ought men to love their wives as their own bodies. 

He that loveth his wife loveth himself. For no man ever hateth his own flesh; but nourisheth and cherisheth it, as also Christ does the Church, because we are members of His body, of His flesh, and of His bones. 'For this cause shall a man leave his father and mother, and shall cleave to his wife, and they shall be two in one flesh.' This is a great sacrament; but I speak in Christ and in the Church. Nevertheless let every one of you in particular love his wife as himself: and let the wife fear her husband."

Jesus Christ Himself said, when asked (Matt. xix) whether divorce was to be permitted by Him (it had been allowed by Moses because of the hardness of the Jews' hearts), "What therefore God hath joined together, let no man put asunder." When His questioners argued with Him, He went on to say that anybody who put away his wife and married another committed adultery, and whoever married her that was put away committed adultery. In Matt. v, Jesus also warned that whoever put away his wife, except for good reasons, was to be held equally guilty of whatever adultery she might commit. "Whosoever shall put away his wife, excepting for

PAGE  

the cause of fornication, maketh her to commit adultery: and he that shall marry her that is put away committeth adultery."

The Catholic Church holds that a married couple can separate when grave spiritual or temporal good of either party demands it. Christ mentions only fornication in the instance above, because it is the chief reason for which this separation is permitted. But remarriage of either before the other's death is not allowed.

Owing to the exceptions made by Christ in regard to fornication, in Matt. xix and v, many non-Catholics hold that absolute divorce with remarriage is permitted to the innocent party in a case where adultery has occurred. But this has never been the teaching of the Catholic Church. 

Other texts in the Bible prove that Christ did not intend the permission of absolute divorce in any consummated Christian marriage. The Council of Trent has settled this matter for Catholics. Christ was referring to simple separation, not to absolute divorce, when speaking of fornication. 

If the texts are to be used in justifying remarriage of the innocent party in divorce cases growing out of fornication, remarriage of the guilty person would also have to be legalized. But this is so foreign to all other texts about divorce in the New Testament, and to the spirit of Christianity in general, that the question is not debatable. Furthermore, the saving phrase in the text "excepting for the cause of fornication," refers only to the first clause, not to the second.

Jesus, in Mark x, 11, 12, made the unqualified statement that absolute divorce is not allowable. "Whosoever shall put away his wife and marry another committeth adultery against her. And if the wife shall put away her husband, and be married to another, she committeth adultery." 

Again in Luke xvi, 18, Jesus is quoted as absolutely forbidding divorce with remarriage. "Every one that putteth away his wife, and marrieth another, committeth adultery: and he that marrieth her that is put from her husband committeth adultery."

St. Paul in I Cor. vii, 10-11, also makes it clear that divorce of Christians with remarriage is forbidden. No exception whatever is made. "But to them that are married, not I but the Lord commandeth, that the wife depart not from her husband. And if she depart, that she remain unmarried or be reconciled to her husband. And let not the husband put away his wife."

Again, in verse 39, St. Paul declares the same truth, making no exception whatever: "A woman is bound by the law as long as her husband liveth; but, if her husband die, she is at liberty: let her marry to whom she will; only in the Lord." In Romans vii, 2, 3, St. Paul again explicitly states that a woman who is fulfilling marital relations with another man while her husband is living is an adulteress, but a wife is free to marry again when her husband dies.

Marriage Laws of the Church 

Following is a summary of some of the marriage laws of the Catholic 

Church: 

Only those marriages of Catholics are valid that are contracted before the pastor or Ordinary (Ordinary means Bishop or Vicar General) of the place, or a priest delegated by either, and at least two witnesses, in con-

PAGE formity with the restrictions laid down in canon law. This rule also holds when only one of the parties is a Catholic. 

But, in danger of death, marriage can be entered into validly and licitly with only two witnesses and no priest present, when there is no opportunity to get a priest. Even apart from the danger of death, marriage may thus be entered (as before a justice of the peace) if it can be prudently foreseen that a priest cannot be secured for a month. If a priest should be in the vicinity who is not delegated to perform marriage there, he should be called and together with the two witnesses assist at the marriage, but only the two witnesses are necessary for validity. 

The Catholic marriage laws are binding upon all persons baptized in the Catholic Church and converts to the Catholic faith from heresy or schism, even though they should afterwards fall away again. 

Non-Catholics, whether baptized or unbaptized, are nowhere held to the Catholic form of marriage when they contract marriage among themselves. Exempt also are the children of non-Catholics who, though they were baptized in the Catholic Church, were reared from their infancy in the non-Catholic faith or in infidelity and without religion, when they contract marriage with non-Catholics.

The Pauline Privilege 

The Pauline privilege is the nearest thing to divorce found in the Catholic Church. It has its basis in the following words of St. Paul, I 

Cor. vii, 12, 15: "If any brother hath a wife that believeth not, and she consent to dwell with him, let him not put her away. And if any woman hath a husband that believeth not, let her not put away her husband. For the unbelieving husband is sanctified by the believing wife, and the unbelieving wife is sanctified by the believing husband; otherwise your children should be unclean, but now they are holy. But, if the unbeliever depart, let him depart. For a brother or sister is not under servitude in such cases. 

God hath called us in peace." But

Obeying this instruction from the Inspired Book, the Catholic Church has always permitted the dissolution of a marriage that has been contracted between non-baptized persons, when one of them is afterwards converted and the conditions are similar to those laid down by St. Paul. The other 

Biblical texts forbidding divorce do not nullify the Pauline privilege. They refer to Christian marriage.

Before the Pauline privilege can be used, the unbaptized person must be asked whether he or she wishes to be converted and baptized and, in case he or she does not wish Baptism, whether he or she is willing to live in marriage without offense to God, i.e., will not interfere with the religious obligations of the convert.

These interpellations must always be made, unless the Holy See decrees otherwise. The only standing exceptions now permitted are conditions that would not arise in the civilized world of today, for they refer to couples separated by kidnaping for slavery, or to converted polygamists. 

The interpellations are to be made only by a properly delegated person. 

Mere casual conversation is not sufficient. 

If the unbaptized person refuses to live peacefully, according to the above questions, with the Christian, the Christian is free to contract a new

PAGE 

marriage with a Catholic, unless after Baptism he or she gave the unbaptized spouse just cause for a separation. 

A Pauline privilege case must pass through the matrimonial court channels of the Church. The individuals cannot usurp the right to pass on the question themselves.

If, after conversion, the baptized party continued to live in marriage with the unbaptized person, the Pauline privilege can still be used, if the conditions demanded for it can be proved present. 

Other Laws 

The only engagement to marriage recognized by the Church is made in writing and signed by the two parties and either the pastor, the Bishop, or at least two witnesses. Even this engagement, however, does not bind to the extent of compelling a marriage. 

Unless the persons to be married were baptized in a pastor's parish, he must demand their Baptismal certificates. This is one of the reasons why priests so seriously object when they are asked to perform a marriage ceremony without any delay. In danger of death, a sworn statement of the parties about Baptism suffices. The Banns of marriage (announcement from the pulpit or posting on the church door of a promise of marriage) are required on three successive Sundays or holy days of obligation, at services largely attended. 

Church does this to protect innocent persons, for, if any one knows that one of the parties would do wrong by entering into the ceremony, he or she is bound in conscience to make the facts known to the priest. The banns also constantly keep before the Catholic public the fact that marriage is not a step to be considered lightly.

Publication of the banns is omitted in case of marriage between Catholics and non-Catholics. The idea of this is to keep down scandal, for such marriages are not encouraged.

The Bishop can dispense with the publication of the banns. Unless there is a good reason not to wait, the marriage should be held off until three days after the last banns are called out. If the marriage is delayed six months after the banns, they must be repeated, unless the Bishop decides otherwise. 

The canon law declares (No. 1034) that the pastor shall earnestly warn young people not to contract marriage against the reasonable objections of their parents, and, if they persist nevertheless, he shall not marry them until he refers the matter to the Bishop. This, it should be noted, refers to reasonable objections.

There are two kinds of impediments to marriage-impedient and diriment. The former contains a grave prohibition to contract marriage, but does not invalidate it when it is contracted. A diriment impediment forbids the marriage and makes it null and void if it has been attempted. 

The Church alone has the right to say what constitutes these impediments, for she alone is the custodian of the sacraments. 

If a supposed marriage has occurred, and there was a diriment impediment, and the Church through her regularly constituted matrimonial courts decides this fact, both parties are free to wed again. Henry VIII, king of England, did not ask for a "divorce” in the modern sense when he

PAGE appealed to Rome for the right to remarry, but wanted his first marriage declared null and void as a result of an alleged impediment. Had one been found, the marriage would have been declared null and void, for such cases have been before the Church in all centuries. But, if an impediment cannot be found, even a king cannot have his marriage declared null and void, for the great Catholic Church has no more power than God Himself gave her, and He did not give her the power to dissolve a consummated Christian marriage.

Summary of Impediments to Marriage 

Impedient impediments to marriage, which make the marriage unlawful or illicit, but do not make it null and void if it is contracted in spite of them, include the following:

Marriage is prohibited to those who have made the simple vows of virginity, perfect chastity, not to marry, to receive major orders, or to embrace the religious life. No simple vow invalidates marriage, unless the Pope has attached invalidity to it for some order, congregation, etc. 

In countries or states where marriage is made illicit by the civil law because of legal adoption, it is also made illicit by canon law. 

Marriage between a heretic or schismatic and a Catholic is forbidden everywhere. This is called the impediment of mixed religion, and the 

Church does not dispense from it unless there are serious reasons, the non- Catholic promises to remove all danger of perversion from the Catholic, both parties promise that their children shall be baptized and reared as Catholics, and there is a moral certainty that the promises will be kept. 

The Catholic has the obligation, under canon law, to work prudently for the conversion of the non-Catholic. It is not permitted to have one ceremony by a priest and another by a non-Catholic minister. If this is being attempted, the priest is forbidden to officiate at a Catholic marriage, except for serious reasons and after scandal has been removed, and the Bishop has been consulted.

In countries where a non-Catholic minister is a public official and it is necessary to appear before him in addition to the Catholic ceremony, the Church does not censure the couple, but tells them to remember that this act is not a marriage but merely compliance with the law. (England used to make all couples appear before an Anglican cleric.)

Bishops and other pastors are obliged by canon law to work against mixed marriages. They are also admonished to discourage the marriage of 

Catholics with fallen-away Catholics, even though the latter have not joined a non-Catholic Church.

A priest is not allowed to officiate at the marriage of a public sinner or a person under censure, unless the latter agrees to go to Confession and be reconciled with the Church; he may, however, officiate if there is a grave and urgent reason, concerning which he must consult the Bishop if possible.

Diriment Impediments to Marriage 

The diriment impediments, which make a marriage null and void as well as unlawful, include: 

A boy under 16 and a girl under 14 years of age cannot validly contract marriage. Though marriage is valid above these ages, pastors

PAGE 

are told by the canon law to dissuade couples from Matrimony at a more tender age than is customary in the country where the ceremony occurs. 

Antecedent and perpetual impotency, either on the part of the man or woman, whether known to the other or not, and whether absolute or relative impotency, annuls marriage by the very law of nature. If the impediment is doubtful, marriage is not forbidden. Sterility neither invalidates marriage nor makes it unlawful.

A person held by a previous marriage bond, even though the marriage has not been consummated, cannot validly contract another marriage. (Consummation means intercourse.) 

Although the first marriage be invalid or dissolved, Catholics cannot lawfully contract another marriage before there is legal proof of this fact. 

The proof must be given before the proper Church authorities. 

Marriage between a Catholic and a non-baptized person is null and void. Dispensations are given for grave and urgent reasons for such marriages, under the same conditions as for mixed-religion marriages. 

Clergymen in major orders, or religious with solemn vows and those with simple vows which, by special law of the Holy See, make marriage invalid, cannot marry.
