The man guilty of rape and the woman he abducts for the purpose of marriage cannot contract a valid marriage so long as she remains in his power. If she freely consents after getting away from his power, the impediment ceases.

The forcible detention of the woman either in the place where she lives or where she came of her own free will, when this detention is undertaken with the purpose of forcing marriage, makes the marriage null and void.

Crime invalidates marriage with the accomplice in the crime as follows: 

If a married person commits adultery by complete conjugal action and enters into a mutual promise of marriage with the partner in adultery, or if they attempt a civil marriage; if a married person commits such adultery and one of the adulterers kills the husband or wife of the adulterer; if a married person, without adultery, brings about the death of his or her spouse with the moral or physical co-operation of the accomplice. 

The impediment arising from blood relationship, or consanguinity, comes as follows: 1. In the direct line of consanguinity, marriage is invalid between persons of all degrees, for the legitimate descendants as well as the natural or illegitimate; 2. In the collateral or branch lines, marriage is invalid to the third degree inclusively, in such manner, however, that the impediment is multiplied only as often as the common progenitors are multiplied: 3. Marriage is never allowed if there is any doubt as to whether the parties are of blood relation in any degree of the direct line, or in the first degree of the collateral line.

The impediments arising from affinity (the relationship that exists between a husband and the blood relatives of his wife, or vice versa) are as follows: 1. Affinity invalidates marriage in any degree of the direct line, and in the collateral line to the second degree inclusively (the former impediment of affinity from sinful intercourse is dropped in the 1918 Code of Canon Law); 2. Affinity is multiplied as often as the impediment of

PAGE consanguinity from which the affinity proceeds is multiplied; by successive marriage with a blood relative of the deceased partner in marriage. 

To arrive at the degrees of relationship, set down the common ancestor and then the two lines of descendants, as follows: In the direct line, there are as many degrees as there are generations, not counting the head of the line (A, in the table above). In the branch lines, there are as many degrees as there are generations in one line (not counting the head, A), if the distance from the common ancestor is equal; if the distance is not equal, there are as many degrees as there are generations in the longer line.

The impediment of public honesty arises from invalid marriage, whether consummated or otherwise, and from notorious public concubinage. 

Marriage is invalidated in the first and second degree of the direct line between the man and blood relatives of the woman, and vice versa, etc. 

Spiritual affinity contracted in Baptism, namely between the person baptized on the one hand and the minister and the sponsors on the other, invalidates marriage.

Those who by the civil law are declared incapable of marrying on account of legal adoption are also unable to contract valid marriage in the eyes of the Church.

The Pope alone can abolish or modify the impedient or diriment impediments of marriage, and no one else can dispense from these laws except in so far as it is conceded to him either in the common law or by special indult of the Holy See. These powers are laid down in canon law. 

When you see a marriage occur in the Church and you know an impediment to it has existed, you realize that for some grave reason a dispensation has been granted. The Church grants these dispensations grudgingly, but she is a kind mother and does not willingly drop a child, while there is still hope of saving him or her.

The Holy See can dissolve a marriage that has been ratified but not consummated, if there are grave reasons for it, and can grant permission to remarry when there is strong presumption of the death of the first partner although this cannot be proved with absolute certainty; but such a thing as divorce with the right to remarry in the case of a consummated Christian marriage is not granted by the Catholic Church and never will be. 

Because the Catholic Church does not hold non-Catholics to our form of marriage, i.e., to the necessity of marrying in the presence of a priest, marriages of non-Catholics are binding, except when impediments nullify them as they would Catholic marriages under the same circumstances, or when the Pauline privilege is used.

When a Catholic or a pervert from the Church is married outside the fold, it is not a marriage in the eyes of the Church. Such a person may protect himself or herself civilly by such a wedding, but the union is simply concubinage in the eyes of the Church, which is the only organization that has the right to say what does and what does not constitute the sacrament of marriage. It is a worse sin to go through a false marriage before a clergyman of a false religion than before a civil magistrate, because the

PAGE 

wedding then is a formal recognition of the false religion. For a Catholic to live in a false marriage and to die unrepentent can have but one ending: eternal damnation. People who fool themselves into believing otherwise are destined for a terrible shock beyond the grave.

Separation of Married Couples 

Married persons are obliged to live together in conjugal relations, unless a just cause frees them from this obligation. 

If one of the two commits adultery, it is reason for the other to live apart, unless the party that wishes to leave consented to the crime, or was the cause of it, or committed the same crime himself or herself. 

Tacit condoning of the crime means living in marital relations with the guilty person, without bringing legal accusation or leaving the person within six months.

Other reasons that justify separation are: If one party joins a non- 

Catholic sect; educates the offspring as non-Catholics; leads a criminal and despicable life; creates great bodily or spiritual danger to the other party; or if through cruelties he or she makes living together too difficult; and for other such reasons, which are to the innocent party so many legal causes to leave the guilty party by authority of the Bishop, or also by private authority, if the guilt of the other party is certain beyond doubt, and there is danger in delay.

Except in case of adultery, the common life must be restored when the reason for the separation ceases. But, if the Bishop pronounced the separation for a certain time or indefinitely, the innocent party is not obliged to return during this allotted period or until the Bishop orders it. In case of adultery, the innocent party is not under compulsion ever to readmit the sinner. The innocent one, however, has the right to take back the sinner, and even compel him or her to return, unless the sinner in the meantime has, with the consent of the innocent party, embraced a state of life contrary to marriage, like going into a religious community. 

After separation, the children should go to the innocent party. If one of the parties is a non-Catholic, the Catholic should get them, that they may be reared as Catholics; unless the Bishop decides that their own welfare demands that they go to the non-Catholic, and in this case their Catholic education must be safeguarded. 

Catholics, for the protection of their property, often seek civil divorces after a separation. But in many instances complete protection can be secured simply by a civil separation. No Catholic should go into the divorce courts without first consulting the Church authorities. Nearly every case of this kind causes talk, whether the divorcee intends to remarry or not. 

Time and Place of Marriage 

Marriage can be contracted at any time of the year, but the Solemn Nuptial blessing is forbidden from the first Sunday in Advent until 

Christmas, inclusively, and from Ash Wednesday to Easter Sunday, inclusively. The Bishop may permit the Nuptial blessing in these seasons, if there is a good reason for it; but the parties must abstain from too much display.

The marriage of Catholics should be contracted in the parish church of either party, preferably that of the bride. It cannot take place elsewhere

PAGE 

without the permission of the Bishop or pastor. Marriage is not allowed to take place in a private house without some good reason. It is not allowed in the churches or chapels of seminaries or women's convents except in case of urgent necessity and by permission of the Ordinary. 

Marriage between a non-Catholic and a Catholic must be contracted outside the church building.* The Bishop has the right to allow it in. the church, if the rule cannot be insisted upon without causing greater evils, but it cannot take place with a Mass. The Church does not encourage mixed marriages. Hence she will not surround them with the same pomp as 

Catholic marriages.

Duties of Parents 

The canon law prescribes that parents are bound by a most serious obligation to provide to the best of their ability for the religious and moral as well as the physical and civil education of their children, and to care for their temporal well-being (canon 1113). 

Non-Consummated Marriages

While the valid marriage of Christians, consummated by the conjugal act, cannot be dissolved except by death, a non-consummated marriage** is dissolved by law through solemn religious profession, as well as by a dispensation of the Holy See, granted for a good reason at the request of either or both parties, or of one of them even though the other might object. The dispensation in such a case is reserved to the Sacred Congregation of the Sacraments at Rome, and no inferior judge can institute the canonical trial in the case unless the Holy See has first granted the faculty. 

About Correcting False Marriages 

If a false marriage has occurred, it should be validated as soon as possible. The couple should go to a priest, who will secure the necessary dispensation if one is needed, and marry them properly, if they are free to wed. The ceremony can be kept as quiet as possible, but it must be made known enough to kill scandal.

If a Catholic has entered a union with some one who refuses to go before a priest, but who is free to wed and is willing to continue in this union, the Church corrects the condition by what she calls a sanatio in radice (a "cure in the root"). Frequently, a Catholic in a case like this would be given permission to have the marriage rectified simply by getting a promise of satisfaction in the union from the other party and by then making a similar statement himself or herself. The non-Catholic need not even know what is happening.

Secret Marriages 

There is legislation in the Church for secret marriages. Ordinarily it is not used, as most secret weddings are kept silent for only a short period and there is no objection to having the priest and official witnesses maintain the secret for a time. If couples wish to marry away from home, a priest can be delegated for this purpose by their own pastor or Ordinary or by the Ordinary of the place where the marriage is to occur. If the bride- *Some dioceses permit it in the church, but without Mass. **A non-consummated marriage is technically known as matrimonium ratum et non consummatum.

PAGE  

groom and bride are from different places, the pastor or Ordinary of either can give the necessary permission. A pastor, even without permission, can validly officiate at a wedding for strangers in his own territory, but he needs permission to officiate licitly. 

Successive Marriage 

If a married person is freed from the bond, as by death, he or she may enter a new marriage, although the Church prefers chaste widowhood. 

Nuptial Blessing 

The Nuptial Blessing is usually given at the wedding Mass; but it can be supplied later. A woman is given it only once; hence, if she weds a second time, it is not accorded then. If a man weds again, the bride not having received it, it can be given.

PAGE 

XXXVI EXTREME UNCTION 

Extreme Unction is a sacrament in which, by anointing with oil and the prayers of the priest, a baptized person receives health of the soul and sometimes of the body. The olive oil must be specially blessed by a Bishop, this rite being performed on Holy Thursday.

The Scriptural warrant for the sacrament is found in St. James' Epistle, v, 14-15: "Is any man sick among you? Let him bring in the priests of the Church, and let them pray over him, anointing him with oil in the name of the Lord; and the prayer of faith shall save the sick man, and the Lord shall raise him up, and, if he be in sins, they shall be forgiven him." 

Mark vi, 13 says: "And they cast out many devils, and anointed with oil many that were sick, and healed them." This text, however, probably does not have reference to the sacrament of Extreme Unction, but to a rite used by the disciples at Christ's command, a forerunner of the sacrament. 

The ancientness of Extreme Unction is proved by its use to this day in sects that cut off from the universal Church so early as the fifth century. 

Both the Scriptures and Tradition are against the Protestant disregard for the sacrament.

In the Latin Church, Extreme Unction is given by only one clergyman. 

In the Eastern rites, several priests may each perform one or more of the anointings.

In the administration of the sacrament, the priest says prescribed prayers and anoints the eyes, the ears, the nostrils, the lips, the hands, and the feet, saying each time: "Through this holy unction and His own most tender mercy, may the Lord pardon thee whatever sins or faults thou hast committed by sight." The last word is replaced in accordance with the sense organ anointed. Formerly, the loins of males were anointed, but this is no longer done. For any good reason, the unction of the feet is omitted. If there is immediate danger, the sacrament is given by a single unction, usually on the forehead, with the words: "Through this holy unction may the Lord pardon thee whatever sins or faults thou hast committed" (Holy Office, April 25, 1906).

Just how the name Extreme Unction arose is disputed. Some think it refers to the fact that the anointing is done in extremis, i.e., when there is danger of death; others to the fact that this is the last of the various unctions given by the Church. It seems to bother some persons that terms such as Extreme Unction, Transubstantiation, etc., are not found in the 

Bible. But neither are the words for pulpits, printing, electricity, and many other things necessary in modern Church life mentioned in the Good Book. 

It is sufficient, in the case of important doctrines, that the truths implied by our terminology be clear in the Scriptures or Tradition. 

Extreme Unction is a sacrament of the living and hence he who receives it should be in the state of grace. He should confess his sins or, if that is not possible, at least make an act of perfect contrition. Even if he could not utter an act of contrition, but had imperfect sorrow for his sins, the sacrament would, nevertheless, be effective. If he did not have such sorrow, but had it on recovering consciousness, the sacrament would then become effective.

PAGE 

Extreme Unction increases sanctifying grace; remits venial sins and also those mortal sins which the patient can no longer confess; removes the remains of sin already forgiven; and strengthens the soul in its sufferings and temptations, especially in the last agony. It often relieves the physical pain of the sick person and sometimes restores him to health "when it is expedient for the soul's salvation," as the Council of Trent declared. 

The remains or effects of sin mentioned as being removed include the spiritual debility and depression caused by the consciousness of having sinned; the influence of evil habits caused by sin; temporal penalties remaining after sin has been forgiven; evil inclinations of the heart and weakness of the will remaining from sin; and venial, or even mortal, sins themselves.

Just how far the sacrament goes in the removal of temporal punishment due for sin is not known. If all should be removed, what about the prayers and the indulgences for the dying? The Church has left us in the dark so far about this question, but the fact that she still wants these prayers said and the final indulgences imparted is no proof that she does not regard the abolition of temporal penalty as complete in Extreme Unction, for she has these prayers said and the indulgences given even when a penitent_and believing person is baptized on his deathbed. It is not held by many, however, that Extreme Unction removes all the temporal penalty. 

There is a theological dispute about what the Council of Trent meant in saying that the sacrament sometimes restores the sick man to health “when it is expedient for the soul's salvation." Some assert that health will not be restored unless God foresees that a longer life will lead to a greater degree of glory, recovery being a sign of predestination. We have come across some pious authors who seemed to hold the uncharitable view that the person was not yet fit for heaven and was thrust back to get himself into better shape. 

Others contend that nothing more is meant than that the soul is here and now benefited by the sacrament's many spiritual riches, and salvation is thus helped along, the dispositions of the soul also bringing bodily health. 

The healing effect of the sacrament is not deemed miraculous, as a general rule. The soul is calmed and nature is given an opportunity to work. Extraordinary cures are common, however, and it would not be rash to believe that at times they surpass the laws of nature. 

Among many, there has been a tendency to give the sacrament only when death is right at the patient's elbow. The opposite extreme has caused the separated Christians of the Oriental rites to give it, at times, to people who are not ill at all. In order to be valid, according to Catholic teaching, it must be given to a person in dangerous illness. Just how dangerous this sickness must be, common sense must decide, but the better theological opinion is that the priests should be lenient rather than severe. The Council of Trent and the traditional practice of the first twelve centuries tend towards the kinder view.

The judgment of the priest, resting on the verdict of the doctor, should be the ordinary criterion about when Extreme Unction is to be given. 

As no miracle of healing is to be expected, it is rash to put off the sacrament until a time when it can have no physical effect without a certain miracle. 

The practice, even in Catholic hospitals, of putting it off until there is danger that the patient will die without it is to be deplored. When a serious

PAGE operation is performed, there should be no question of giving Extreme Unction to a person who wants it. The ignorant fear that the sacrament will scare a patient is silly. Extreme Unction never hurts. It always helps. 

Both priests and laymen should correct either physicians or nurses when the latter want the sacrament postponed if the illness is grave. We have known even an occasional hospital nun who was anything but Catholic minded on this point. The writer knows whereof he speaks. He once had to give a serious rebuke to a nun who flew into a mild tantrum because he demanded Extreme Unction after a grave operation that came close to ending his life. She had the queer idea that he was worrying himself too much about his condition. He had no thought about the matter but safety, for he was perfectly willing to die or to live, whichever God wanted.

A serious reason for not postponing the sacrament unduly is that it should be given when the patient is able to know what is happening and can aid in bringing himself to the proper dispositions. The depth of penance unquestionably affects the richness of the results obtainable. 

Extreme Unction cannot be given to a person in danger from other affairs than illness or old age. A soldier going into battle, or a man about to be hanged or electrocuted or gassed for murder, cannot be anointed. 

Priests have often given Extreme Unction by the short form, however, right after a hanging, electrocution, etc. Whenever the short form is given, the sacrament is complete, and the priest should not then supply the missing ceremonies from the long form.

Death does not come, in many cases, as soon as some think. After an accident, Penance and Extreme Unction should be given, conditionally, to a seemingly deceased person at least up to two hours and perhaps after a longer period. If a person has wasted away of disease, death probably comes more quickly. Modern science records numerous cases in which the supposedly dead have been revived by powerful drugs. The separation of the soul from the body had, of course, not yet occurred. 

In each dangerous illness, Extreme Unction can be received only once, unless the sick person somewhat recovers and again lapses into danger of death.

If there is doubt about whether the sick person has attained the use of reason, or whether he is really in danger of death, or is really alive, the sacrament is to be given conditionally. It must not be given to those who have stubbornly persevered in mortal sin without repentance, but, if there is doubt about their disposition, it can be given conditionally. Those who implicitly asked for it while conscious, or would probably ask for it, can be given the sacrament absolutely, even though they are unconscious at the time.

The sacrament is not absolutely necessary for salvation, but canon law sets forth that no one is allowed to neglect it and care should be taken that the sick receive it when they are conscious, if possible. 

Whatever has been said about sickness goes also for old age. If a person is a chronic invalid and is anointed, but makes some recovery, the sacrament can be repeated in about a month, if there is new danger. 

The Council of Trent declared anathema anybody who would say "that Extreme Unction is not truly and properly a sacrament instituted by Christ

PAGE  

Our Lord, and promulgated by the blessed Apostle James, but merely a rite received from the fathers, or a human invention." 

It must be held as of faith that Christ is at least the mediate Author of the sacrament. He undoubtedly commissioned the Apostles to use some such sign for conferring the special graces, without perhaps specifying the matter and form to be used.

PAGE XXXVII 

SACRAMENT OF HOLY ORDER; MINISTRIES OF THE CATHOLIC CHURCH 

Holy Order is a sacrament by which bishops, priests, and other ministers of the Church are ordained and given the power and grace to perform their holy offices.

In the Latin Church, the orders are bishop, priest, deacon, subdeacon, acolyte, exorcist, reader, and ostiarius or doorkeeper. The first three are as old as the Apostles, and all must be ancient, for we find them mentioned incidentally by Cornelius, Bishop of Rome, in the middle of the third century. 

Tonsure is not classed as an order; it is an introduction to the clerical state. 

The episcopacy, or rank of a bishop, is not regarded by St. Thomas and many theologians as a separate order, but only as the completion and extension of the priesthood. Hence, when a man is consecrated bishop, he is said to receive the plentitude of the priesthood. 

Probably orders lower than the diaconate are only of ecclesiastical institution, and are not, therefore, accompanied by the grace of a sacrament. * 

In the Latin rite, the orders under subdiaconate are called minor; those above and including subdiaconate, major. A clergyman takes his vow of chastity, which prohibits marriage, when he is ordained a subdeacon. In the Oriental Church, subdiaconate is regarded as a minor order; it is doubtful, therefore, whether it is a participation in the sacrament of Order. 

The way St. Paul wrote to Timothy about his orders shows that the 

Catholic teaching regarding them is the only reasonable one. "Do not neglect the grace which was given through prophecy, with laying on of the hands of the priesthood," he writes (I Tim. iv, 14). "And I put thee in mind to rekindle the grace of God, which is in thee through the laying on of my hands" (II Tim. i, 6).

The ordaining of the seven deacons, described in Acts vi, happened as follows: "These they set before the Apostles; and they, praying, imposed hands upon them." Acts xiii, 3, shows that Saul and Barnabas were ordained also by the imposition of hands, with fasting and prayer, before they were sent out to do their work. Acts xiv, 22, speaks of ordination: "And when they had ordained to them priests in every church, and had prayed with fasting, they commended them to the Lord, in whom they believed." 

When one receives priesthood, one obtains supernatural power and an indelible character on the soul that will remain throughout all eternity. 

Once a man is a priest, he is always a priest. Even though he should lead *Most popular books of reference say that the four minor orders and subdiaconate were instituted by the Church and not by Christ and that, therefore, one does not receive the sacrament of Holy Order in them. The question, however, is an open one. "The Church, not having defined anything on this point, theologians are free to debate it pro and con. In matter of fact, there is a long-standing controversy, which cannot, however, be decided on dogmatic grounds but must be fought out in the arena of history" (The Sacraments, vol. iv, Pohle-Preuss, page 106). 

The minor orders have evolved differently in the Latin Church and in the Catholic Eastern rites. The Byzantines and Chaldeans have only two minor orders, subdeacon and lector (with the Latins, subdiaconate is a major order). The 

Armenians have the same line-up as the Latins. The Abyssinians, Copts, Syrians, and Maronites have cantor, lector, and subdeacon. Except among the Armenians, the terms major and minor are not used in the Oriental rites.

PAGE 

such a life that he will be damned forever, the mark of the priesthood will be upon him. In heaven it will add to his glory. 

The office is worthy of the highest respect. St. Francis of Assisi, believed by many to be the greatest saint the later centuries have produced, held it so high that he never dared to aspire to it. Without priests, even well-intentioned Catholics often lose their faith, as is proved by the experience of many Catholics in colonial days of America and later among the Western pioneers. Each priest is said to be directly responsible for the saving of about 9,000 souls. As the worth of one immortal soul cannot be estimated, and is more valuable than all material creation put together, a reasonable man can easily see why Catholics hold worthy priests in such high respect. No other line of work can possibly compare with the priesthood in its value to mankind.

When a man is raised to the priesthood, he usually goes through six ordination ceremonies. First he receives tonsure, by which he is introduced to the clerical state. Next he receives the two minor orders, ostiarius and lector. Ostiarius, translated into English, means doorkeeper or porter, and the order recalls the days of persecution in the Church, when an ordained official stood at the entrance to keep spies out. Lector means reader. When a lector serves at High Mass, he chants the Epistle. The next ordination ceremony gives the candidate the ranks of exorcist and acolyte. Exorcists have the power of casting out demons from the possessed and were greatly needed in pagan days when there were many persons thus afflicted. Today, on the rare occasions when this power is exercised, it is done by a priest but only after he has received permission from the bishop. Acolytes were ordained in the early Church to serve the priest at Mass, and the office is continued today among men aspiring to the priesthood. 

Tonsure is not an order, but the introduction to the clerical state. 

Ostiarius, lector, exorcist, and acolyte are known as minor orders They seem to be sacramentals, rather than participation in the sacrament of Holy Order. By the term sacramentals, we here mean ceremonies introduced by the Church and not by Christ Himself, and giving grace ex opere operantis and not ex opere operato.** Though minor orders are usually given two at a time, they could be given at one ceremony if this were necessary. 

After getting minor orders, the candidate receives the major orders. 

The first major order is subdeaconship. It is at the reception of this order that the vow of chastity is imposed by the Latin Church. No actual words are said by the candidate taking the vow. The mere fact of going through the ceremony imposes it. The vow renders true marriage not only unlawful but impossible for the clergyman. Among the functions of the subdeacon are to assist at Solemn Mass and to chant the Epistle at this service. The grave obligation of saying the Divine Office daily is imposed with the subdiaconate. **The effect of an ex opere operantis work depends on the dispositions of the subject, the piety of the minister, and the suffrage of the Church. The effect of an ex opere operato work-the term is used to describe the primary effect of a sacrament indicates that grace is conferred in virtue of the sign and ceremony instituted by Christ for this purpose and is not dependent on the virtues or merits of the minister or recipient. The dispositions of the recipient may increase the fruits or block the reception of grace, but they are not the chief cause of the benefit, as in a sacramental.

PAGE The next major order is the deaconship. In the early Church, deacons were often in charge of congregations and for a time they handled practically all the business affairs of the Church. Today, only a man who intends to go through for the priesthood is given deaconship or any of the preceding orders. One reason for this is that it is not as difficult to get priests as it was at one time, when the Church was losing many of her clergymen by persecution and was at the same time growing by leaps and bounds. Deacons assist the celebrant at Solemn Mass, chant the Gospel at this service, and, when they have permission, can preach, solemnly baptize, and distribute Communion. 

The next order is priesthood. The chief powers of a priest are to consecrate bread and wine into the Body and Blood of Christ and to forgive sins. He administers sacraments, preaches, does pastoral work, etc. Preaching is chiefly the function of the episcopate, but the bishops cannot possibly reach all the people and hence the priest is asked to assist in this service. 

The highest office in the Church is that of bishop, so far as the sacrament of Order is concerned. Some authorities look upon the episcopate as an order separate from priesthood, although the same sacrament. Others consider it an amplification of the priestly power, the view taken by ourselves. The episcopate is called the "plenitude of the priesthood." The bishop, by right of his office, can ordain and confirm, and a man holding this dignity is usually head of a diocese or territorial division of the Church, although there are also titular bishops. So far as the sacrament of Order is concerned, the Bishop of Denver, or of Pittsburgh, ranks as high as the Pope. The Pope's jurisdiction is greater and he has personal infallibility under certain restrictions, but when one has become a bishop one has reached the pinnacle of the dignity the sacrament of Order gives. 

To receive Holy Orders worthily, a man should be in the state of grace, have the necessary knowledge, and a Divine call to this sacred office. 

The necessary knowledge must be obtained by stipulated studies. The ordinary education of a priest is, after he has finished high school and two years of college work, two years of philosophy and four years of theology, with kindred studies. The Catholic Church has by far the highest educational requirements of any denomination for her clergy. Her priests-to-be study for twelve years, sometimes more, after leaving the common school. When converts who wish to be priests come into the Church, and have received a good education, allowance is made for this. It is not uncommon to hear of a finely educated clergyman convert's being made a priest in a few months to a few years after his coming into Catholicism. At the Beda College in Rome, where many English-speaking converts study, the complete course is four years. The priesthood and all the orders leading to it can be received only by persons of the male sex. "Let women keep silence in the churches,' ordered St. Paul (I Cor. xiv, 34), "for it is not permitted them to speak, but to be subject, as also the law saith." There is no more injustice involved in this than is involved in the fact that few men can be bishops, despite the saying of St. Paul: "A faithful saying: if a man desire the office of a bishop, he desireth a good work" (I Tim. iii, 1).

The sacrament of Order is given, not for the benefit of an individual, but for the sake of the people, although great grace can be won by him who

PAGE 

serves faithfully as a clergyman. That a Divine call is involved in a vocation to the priesthood is evident from Christ's statement to His disciples: “You have not chosen Me, but I have chosen you, and have appointed you that you should go, and should bring forth fruit, and your fruit should remain, that whatsoever you shall ask of the Father in My name He may give to you" (John xv, 16).

How is one to know that one has this Divine call? Few are given visions or other such unmistakable signs. If a boy has the inclination to be a priest, and has the necessary physical, mental, and spiritual qualifications, he can feel safe in seeking admission to a seminary. The actual vocation will come with the call from the bishop to him to receive orders. 

The early Church recognized an office for women who were known as deaconnesses. To use our modern terminology, this institution was never deemed a sacrament, but a sacramental. Certain widows and maidens of mature age were charged with the instruction and baptism of women, with attendance on a bride at marriage, with visiting the sick and prisoners, etc. They were "ordained" with a laying on of hands by the bishop. Because of this ordination, the abuse arose in some parts of the Church of almost recognizing the deaconnesses as participating in the sacrament of Order, a mistake that was condemned by the Council of Nicaea. The office disappeared in the West by the tenth century, but held on in the Oriental rites, where its importance was more exaggerated than in the West. Some of the Oriental separatists still have a form of it. 

The office of abbot is given by the Church to heads of some monasteries and, occasionally, as a titular honor. It is conferred in a ceremony as elaborate as the consecration of a bishop. An abbot has the right to pontificate at 

Mass, to wear the pectoral cross, the prelatial ring, and pontificalia in general, including the mitre. "Once an abbot, always an abbot," the office cannot be dropped after being received at this formal rite. But the abbatial benediction is not part of the sacrament of Holy Order. It is a sacramental, not a sacrament. The Church, it seems to us, has gone as far as possible in duplicating, with abbots, the rank that Christ established for bishops. The instinct of the Catholic people is correct in evaluating this office as almost parallel with the episcopacy, differing only in that episcopal consecration is a sacrament and abbatial benediction is a sacramental. 

The Church also has a solemn service for the benediction of certain mother superiors of women's religious communities as abbesses, who have the right to use the ring, pectoral cross, and pastoral staff. Abbesses are found in the women's sections of those old orders whose male sections are governed by abbots; also among certain branches of the Franciscans. medieval times, some of the abbesses had great political and ecclesiastical power. When a bishop blesses an abbess, the rite is a sacramental, not a sacrament.

In The Divine call to the sacrament of Holy Order implies a disposition towards the priesthood and the necessary qualifications. The Epistle to the 

Hebrews v, 4, warns that nobody is to "take the honor to himself, but he that is called by God as Aaron was." To enter the priesthood with unworthy motives, such as ambition, would be a serious wrong. The Catechism of the 

Council of Trent says: "It is to those who arrogantly intrude themselves into this ministry that the Lord must be understood to refer when He says: 'I did

PAGE not send prophets, yet they ran' (Jer. xxiii, 21). Nothing can be more unhappy and wretched than such a class of men as this, and nothing more calamitous to the Church of God."

Christians should look upon the priests as God's messengers and the dispensers of His mysteries. Even though an occasional cleric might prove unworthy of his office, the vast majority are men of sterling character. The world demands a higher status of perfection from the Catholic priest than it does from any other individual, even the non-Catholic clergyman. This is proved by the universal horror when a priest falls. Unwittingly, even unbelievers give testimony on such an occasion to the fact that the priesthood is an office above anything the world itself can offer. 

The Catholic Church has both a celibate and a married priesthood. In the Latin Church, which is by far the largest branch of the Church, but only one of various "rites," no one but a celibate can be admitted to the priest hood. A married man could be ordained if he and his wife mutually agreed to live in celibacy, she, usually, going into a convent; and a widower could be ordained. But the Latin priest must be a celibate. This is a disciplinary measure, which the Church could change if she wished. In the Greek and other rites of the Church, a married man can be ordained, and remain with his wife, although bishops are chosen only from the monks or celibate clergy. 

The Catholic Church, therefore, has had experience with both a married and unmarried clergy, and unquestionably, although there are many splendid married priests, the celibate priesthood is better, as an unmarried man can devote himself more wholeheartedly to the Church. 

As St. Paul put it: "He that is without a wife is solicitous for the things that belong to the Lord, how he may please God. But he that is with a wife is solicitous for the things of the world, how he may please his wife: and he is divided" (I Cor. vii, 32, 33). "He that giveth his virgin in marriage doth well; and he that giveth her not doth better,” said 

St. Paul (I Cor. vii, 38), summing up his teachings on celibacy. Marriage is good, but celibacy is better. That is why we encourage a celibate priesthood and encourage women to assume convent life, where they are vowed to chastity. People who know the Catholic Church are well aware that the priest who is not true to his vow of chastity is a decided exception. 

The Bible is distinct in demanding ordination. When Paul and 

Barnabas were consecrated to the rank of bishops, it was after fasting and prayer, with imposition of hands (Acts xiii, 3). These same Apostles ordained priests in all the cities they visited on a missionary journey, as we are told in the Acts. St. Paul wrote to Timothy (II i, 6) to stir up the grace of God that was in him "by the imposition of hands." He also spoke of Timothy's ordination in I Tim, iv, 14.

Any man who dares to assume this office without ordination would do well to read of the terrible punishment visited on Core, Dathan, and Abiron in the Old Testament. The earth opened and swallowed them. 

It has ever been demanded by the Church that the sacrament of Order must be given only by one who has himself received it and who has the right to confer it. The candidate must intend to receive it and the ordainer must intend to give it. The fact that their early bishops had no intention of ordaining a sacrificial priesthood but only of initiating preachers invalidated the line of Order in the Anglican Church. Denominations that are

PAGE 

cut off from Rome, yet have preserved correct ordination, have their sacrament recognized by Rome and their clergy can come into our Church without reordination. This is true of the Greek Orthodox. 

Christian tradition gives not an inch of ground to those who hold that one can be rightfully ordained by other than a genuinely ordained person. Our bishops do our ordaining, although an abbot can give tonsure and minor orders to his own subjects. The Pope could give any priest the power to ordain to minor orders and also to major orders up to, and probably including, the diaconate. Not only abbots for their own subjects, but cardinals who are not bishops, vicars and prefects apostolic and prelatesnullius who are not in episcopal orders can, by the general law of the Church, ordain their own subjects to minor orders. Whether an abbot has ever received the right to ordain to the diaconate is disputed, but it now seems certain. It appears to us that the reason why men who are not consecrated as bishops can be given such great power is because every priest is a bishop in radice and this latent power can be brought forward by Papal jurisdiction, as it is when the Pope gives simple priests the power to confirm. 

Although there is this power in priests that can be brought forward by Papal permission, nevertheless there is a distinct difference between the ordinary priesthood and its plentitude as found in the episcopacy; for either a confirmation or an ordination would be invalid if done by a priest without Papal permission, not merely illicit. The Council of Trent (sess. 23, can. 7) ruled: "If any one say that bishops are not superior to priests, or that they have not the power to confirm or ordain, or that the power which they have is common to them and to priests . . . let him be anathema."

PAGE 

XXXVIII SELF-CONSTITUTED CLERGY WERE FEARFULLY PUNISHED BY HEAVEN 

Jesus Christ told His Apostles that they had not chosen Him but He had chosen them. The Catholic Church believes that her clergymen do not come to their office in the same way as another man might choose a trade or profession, but are Divinely called to it. We know from the lives of the saints that they have occasionally been summoned to the clerical or religious life as the result of visions. It is not believed by the Church that extraordinary manifestations are necessary to indicate that a man is called to the priesthood; rather, according to important authorities, actual vocation comes through being summoned by one's proper ecclestiastical superior to ordination. 

Canon 1353 of the Code of Canon Law declares that priests, especially pastors, should give attention to boys who show signs of ecclesiastical vocation and take pains to keep them from the contamination of the world, to instruct them in piety, to give them the first lessons in the study of letters, and to sow the seed of vocation in them. By signs of ecclesiastical vocation, the canon means those moral, intellectual, and physical qualities, together with a leaning towards the service of the altar, that are characteristic of boys who are destined, after the proper training, to be formally called to the priesthood. What we have said of the nature of vocation to the priesthood is true also of vocations to religious orders. 

Differing from the careful scrutiny exercised by the Catholic Church, many non-Catholics seem to build upon the idea that anybody who wishes to do so can become a clergyman. Although it is true that all the better organized denominations use some care in the selection of their clergymen, nevertheless it often seems to be possible for a man or woman simply to set up in the Church business, adopt the title, "Reverend," and be accepted by many persons as entitled to that status. All the larger sects have some sort of ordination ceremony or at least formally licensed ministers. But if we go back into the history of most of these lines of orders, we find that they were begun by individuals who were able to give no better credentials than their own desire to found a new denomination. In our Church, on the contrary, only he can ordain whose own orders go back to the Apostles. 

Just as only a human being can propagate a human being, so only a truly consecrated bishop can ordain a priest or consecrate a bishop. 

There are instances in the Old Testament that dramatically exhibit the anger of God over the insolence of daring to assume the duties of the priesthood without having been properly called and ordained. One of these occurred when the Israelites were in the wilderness on their way from

Egypt to the Promised Land. Two hundred and fifty men, having for leaders Core, Dathan, and Abiron, became jealous of the high position of Moses and Aaron, and accused them of tyranny and ambition. They argued that all the people were holy, and therefore these two should not raise themselves above the people of God. When Moses heard of this revolt, he was greatly afflicted and fell flat upon his face. 

The rebels were all men important in the religious rites of the Jews. 

Moses called them together and said: "Is it because God has chosen you to serve near the tabernacle that you wish to usurp the dignity of the priesthood

PAGE 

also? Tomorrow the Lord will make known who they are that belong to

Him. Prepare, then, and stand each with his censer on one side, and Aaron will stand on the other."

The next day, the 250 men appeared at the tabernacle with their censers, but the three leaders of the revolt remained in their tents. Moses, accompanied by his brother, the high priest Aaron, told the people to stand off from the wicked trio lest they should perish with them. Then he said: "If these men die the common death of men, the Lord did not send me; but, if the earth, opening her mouth, swallow them down, and they go down alive into hell, you shall know that they have blasphemed the Lord.”

Moses had scarcely stopped speaking when the earth opened under the feet of the three sinners and swallowed them up with the tents and all that belonged to them. Fire also came down from heaven and destroyed the 

250 men who had taken sides with Core, Dathan, and Abiron. After this, God commanded Moses to have the men of Israel hold rods, each with his name written upon them, and Moses was to lay the rods in the tabernacle. "Whomsoever of these I shall choose, his rod shall blossom." The next day, when Moses had entered the tabernacle, he found that the rod of Aaron had budded and blossomed.

Much later in Jewish history, a dire punishment of a man who usurped the work of the priesthood without authorization came to a king named Ozias, about 803 B.C. Ozias was one of the few faithful kings who ruled in Juda after the secession of the kingdom of Israel from the Jewish nation. 

For 52 years he was singularly blessed by God in what he did. But prosperity finally made him proud, and, being envious of the priesthood and not satisfied with merely being king, he went one day into the temple to burn incense upon the altar. Eighty priests, with Azarias, the high priest, at their head, opposed him. They told him that he had no right to usurp this work of the priests. They ordered him to leave the sanctuary, whereupon he grew angry and threatened to strike the priests with the censer he held in his hand. 

No sooner had he raised his hand than he was stricken with leprosy, which all the priests saw appear on his forehead and then spread quickly all over his body. The priests, horrified by this, put him out of the temple. Feeling the leprosy all over his body, he hastened away and shut himself apart from all others. He remained a leper till the day of his death. 

Still a third example from the Old Testament may be quoted, showing the contempt of God for unauthorized innovations in His worship. Exodus x, 1, 2 says: "And Nabab and Abiu, the sons of Aaron, taking their censers, put fire therein, and incense on it, offering before the Lord strange fire: which was not commanded them. And fire coming out from the Lord destroyed them, and they died before the Lord."

St. Paul, in his letter to the Hebrews, chap. 5, verse 4, said of the priesthood: "Neither doth any man take the honor to himself, but he that is called by God, as Aaron was.' 

It may be that many who have set themselves up as clergymen outside the Catholic Church will be saved from severe punishment because of their ignorance. But, if a Catholic were to dare to do this, he could expect a fearful penalty. Likewise, when priests separate themselves from obedience to the Church and establish new denominations and even dare to have themselves consecrated to the episcopacy by schismatic or heretical bishops

PAGE with valid orders, we can well imagine what punishment they must expect in eternity. God made the punishment of Core, Dathan, and Abiron and that of Ozias visible in order to teach mankind a needed lesson. But we must remember that He has all eternity in which to set things right, and that it is not necessary for any punishment to come to offending individuals on this earth; but the fact that it does not come now by no means assures them of their escape in eternity. Pride, unchecked ambition, and fancied. grievances have led many men in the course of history to try what the rebels among the Jews attempted. How can these men expect any less punishment for the same sin? What we say about them also goes for those followers who adhere to them, if this adherence is given by persons who are sane enough and well enough informed to be cognizant of what they are going.

PAGE 

 XXXIX SACRAMENTALS OF CHURCH OFFER US OPPORTUNITIES TO GROW IN GRACE 

A sacramental is a holy thing or action used by the Church to obtain for us from God spiritual or temporal favors. A sacramental excites good thoughts in us, stirring up devotion, and through these movements of the heart remits venial sin. It also applies to us the prayers of the Church; for instance, when we use holy water, the prayer of the priest said at its blessing becomes effective.

A sacramental has a certain resemblance to a sacrament, but the two differ in several ways.

By a sacrament we mean an outward sign-which, for instance, in Baptism consists of the pouring of water to the accompaniment of certain words-instituted by Christ to give grace. By a sacramental, we mean some blessing, prayer, etc., instituted, not directly by Christ, but by His Church, to stir up pious feelings within us and to have these feelings and the prayer of the Church act as petitions to God in our behalf. A sacrament gives grace of itself, and is infallible in its effects, if the necessary conditions are carried out. Even though I should be unconscious at the time I receive a sacrament, if my state of mind before I have lost sensibility has been proper, I am sure to receive the benefit of the sacrament. With a sacramental, on the other hand, it is the pious disposition that the sacramental stirs up in me that often does the chief work. 

When Christ instituted the sacraments, He did not determine their matter and form in full detail, but left this to the Church. For instance, in establishing the Mass, He did not personally surround it with all the beautiful ceremony that adorns the Eucharistic Sacrifice in the Church, but the Holy Ghost later guided the Church in the formation of this ritual, which conduces so much to the piety of the people. These various ceremonies surrounding the consecration of bread and wine into the body and blood of 

Christ are sacramentals.

When a priest solemnly baptizes, he also surrounds the actual Baptism with anointings, laying on of hands, and other rites, which are sacramentals, and have been in use from the earliest times. 

Sacramentals not only have the potency of stirring up pious dispositions in us, but also have tremendous power in offsetting the hordes of evil spirits that constantly surround men, trying to destroy their souls. Undoubtedly one of the chief reasons why cases of demoniac possession have become rare in Christendom, although they were among the commonest afflictions of mankind in Christ's day, is because of the number of sacramentals in use. 

This is further proved by the fact that some pagan lands to this day have more of these cases than Christian districts, as mission literature proves. 

In addition to the sacramentals she has instituted for use in her liturgy, the Church has many for private devotion. For instance, she blesses various medals, scapulars, etc., for the use of her people. The sacramentals, unlike the sacraments, do not have an infallible effect, but they do work marvels. 

It is by no means a rare occurrence to have a person led into the Church through the wearing of a medal of Our Lady. In a case like this, the recipient of the grace can hardly have been stirred up to pious dispositions

PAGE that have moved God to bring on His conversion, but the official prayer of the Church and the piety of the Catholic who has put the medal on the convert have brought about the result, acting as a petition to God. 

The prayer of the Church has a great deal to do with the efficacy of sacramentals. For this reason, it is valuable to surround even the unconscious dying with them.

Non-Catholics often profess to deem our use of medals, scapulars, holy water, etc., superstitious. But, if the practice is superstitious, so is all prayer. 

Prayer said with the intention of its being efficacious when the blessed article is used is certainly not any more superstitious than any other prayer. 

The Church has the power to institute unlimited numbers of sacramentals, and she has many. Her ritual contains formulas for the blessing of numerous articles, and has a general formula for the blessings of things not specifically mentioned in the ritual.

Among the sacramentals are formally authorized prayers, whether said in the liturgy or in private; the use of holy water; the unctions with consecrated oil in sacraments where these unctions are not actually part of the sacrament; the eating of blessed foods; the avowal of faults made in the 

Confiteor at Mass, in the Divine Office said daily by the priests and many religious, etc.; the giving of alms for Christ's sake; Papal, episcopal, and priestly blessings; the visiting of blessed or consecrated churches; the blessing of candles, ashes, palms, etc.

If we use a sacramental, and are at the same time sorry for our venial sins, even though the contrition be only imperfect (i.e., based on fear of punishment from God), the sin is forgiven. Therefore, if I use holy water, make the sign of the cross, hold a blessed crucifix, receive the blessing of a priest, etc., I can have my venial sins remitted. Frequent use of sacramentals may thus have the effect of cutting down my time in purgatory. 

The chief sacramental is the Sign of the Cross, which has undoubtedly come from Apostolic times, for Tertullian in the third century specifically spoke of it as a universal custom. We make this sign by putting the right hand to the forehead, then to the breast, then to the left and right shoulders, saying: "In the name of the Father, and of the Son and of the Holy Ghost. 

Amen." The sign recalls the chief mysteries of religion. The words "In the name" signify the unity of God. The mention of the Father, the Son, and the Holy Ghost recalls the Trinity. And the making of a cross reminds us that Christ, the Son of God, became man and died on a cross to save us. 

Holy water is another commonly used sacramental. The priest, acting not in a personal capacity, but as a minister of the entire Church, prays that a mixture of water and a little salt might be blessed by God, the blessing to be efficacious when the water is used. The use of blessed water has been known from the dawn of history. There was use of "astral water" in the rites of practically all the ancient pagan religions and they undoubtedly got the idea from the first patriarchs of the human race, accommodating it to their paganism, while consecrated water was constantly used in the Jewish temple, as we know from the Old Testament. Salt is often used in the sacramentals; reminding us of Christ's assertion that we are the salt of the earth.

Blessed candles recall to us the days when the Christians had to hide away in darkness and to use artificial light for their rites, in the era of

PAGE 

violent opposition. The flame of the candle is also symbolic of burning faith. Candles are used in our liturgy, not only to give light, but as a mark of joy, and of honor to God, holy persons, and holy things. Palms are blessed on Palm Sunday, emblematic of the palm branches cast in Christ's path on His triumphal entry into Jerusalem. Blessed ashes are burnt blessed palm branches and are put on the foreheads of the people on Ash Wednesday, with the warning given by God to Adam and Eve in Genesis to remember that man is dust and into dust he shall return. Scapulars are small pieces of cloth, worn suspended by strings over the shoulders, and considered as abbreviated forms of the habits or special clothing of certain religious orders, with which the person vested in a scapular is in a manner affiliated, and thus participates in their indulgences. The use of scapulars and medals is largely based on the use of flags, special insignia, and uniforms by military men. They are soldiers of the state; we are soldiers of Christ. 

One of the most efficacious of sacramentals is the saying of the Rosary, in which, while we pray, we review the chief events in the life of Jesus 

Christ and His Mother. 

The chief benefits flowing from the use of sacramentals are: 1. Actual graces; 2. The forgiveness of venial sins; 3. The remission of temporal punishment owed for sin; 4. Health of the body and material blessings; 

5. Protection from evil spirits. 

The principal kinds of sacramentals are: 1. Blessings given by priests and bishops; 2. Exorcisms against evil spirits; 3. Blessed objects of devotion.

The Church expects us to guard against superstition in the use of sacramentals.

PAGE XL SUGGESTIONS ABOUT PRAYER; WITHOUT IT, SALVATION IS IMPOSSIBLE 

One of the necessary means of obtaining grace is prayer. Prayer is the lifting of our minds and hearts to God. By it we adore Him, thank 

Him for His favors, ask His forgiveness and the remission of punishment due for sin, and beg of Him all the graces we need, whether for soul or body.

The ordinary manner of praying is to utter certain words of praise or petition to God or to His saints or angels. Vocal prayer must be used by the priest in the administration of the sacraments and when he is saying Mass or the Divine Office, or by people who wish to gain an indulgence through the saying of a certain prayer.* While we can pray by mentally following a printed or written prayer without vocal effort, in the aforementioned cases actual vocalization is required. There is one short section of the Mass when meditation is required of the priest, but distinct vocalization is demanded at the other times. 

Meditation is a great deal more difficult than vocal prayer, but, if persistently engaged in, often brings higher blessings. Owing to the insistence of the Church on vocal prayer in her liturgy, however, it would be folly to adhere only to mental prayer. Almost all the great saints have been marvels at meditation. It sometimes reaches a perfection, as in the case of St. Teresa of Avila, Spain, when there is a close intellectual union between God and the soul and God gives precious hidden information about the supernatural. Teresa was an uneducated woman, but so great was her knowledge obtained in mental prayer that her autobiography is a profound work of mystical theology. Her case was not unique among the 

Catholic saints.

In religious orders, seminaries, and in the priesthood, daily meditation is supposed to be part of the ordinary routine. It is believed by spiritual authorities that a priest or religious who is faithful to the daily meditation never fails. If daily meditation is so valuable to the religious life, it ought to be practiced more by the laity. Many books of meditation have been printed. One good way to indulge in the practice is to read a portion of the Gospels daily, and then close the book and think about what has been. read. In the case of more privileged souls, God floods the mind with thought and valuable spiritual information at such times. He grants an exaltation and close union with Himself rarely ever attained. A meditation is often a foretaste of heaven. Meditation should not be considered too intricate or difficult. Simply to raise our hearts to God and to think about sacred persons and things is to meditate. 

In vocal prayer, we can either say set forms of prayer such as the Our Father, Hail Mary, or Apostles' Creed, or we can make up our own petitions. 

It is wiser to adhere to the authorized forms. The Church greatly encourages the use of prayerbooks, for when we read them we can keep our minds better settled on what we are doing. 

Converts are at times puzzled at a common practice of ours in saying *When prayer is being said in common with others, it is not necessary to repeat that portion uttered by the leader in order to gain an indulgence.

PAGE 

set prayers for something which these prayers, in themselves, do not mention; for instance, when a girl out of a job says ten Hail Mary's daily that she might find work. The Hail Mary says nothing about finding work. 

So why use this prayer? The explanation is simple. To say the Hail Mary is an act of worship to God and praise for His mother. The act of worship is offered up and God grants the request in return. 

Prayer is essential to salvation. Without it, it is impossible for any one having the use of reason to be saved. The Church particularly encourages us to pray on Sundays and holy days of obligation, every morning and night, in all dangers, temptations, and afflictions. Even though we have to rush to work in the morning, we can always find time for at least an aspiration or a very short prayer, as "God be merciful to me, a sinner." We can pray in any position, but an acceptable way is on bended knees. The ancient 

Jews usually prayed standing up. Byzantine Catholics still have this custom. 

St. Paul tells us to "pray without ceasing," meaning that we should offer all our thoughts, words, and actions to God (I Thess. v, 17). 

Our prayers should be said with attention, with a sense of our own helplessness and dependence on God, with a great desire for what we beg of God, with trust in God's goodness, and with perseverance. These suggestions are all based on Scriptural texts. St. Monica had to pray eighteen years for the conversion of her wretchedly sinful son, Augustine, but she persevered and he became one of the greatest saints in history, while her long prayers also made her a saint.

The Lord's Prayer (Our Father), Hail Mary (Angelic Salutation), Apostles' Creed, Confiteor, and Acts of Faith, Hope, Love, and Contrition are the most recommended prayers. They should be learned by heart. The 

Rosary, composed of simple prayers combined with meditations on the lives of Jesus Christ and His mother, is highly efficacious. Novenas are a popular form of prayer. They are made by saying prayers nine days in succession, usually with Confession and Communion some time in the nine-day period. 

We can use any prayers we wish in a novena and make one to any saint we choose. Novenas are often made to the Sacred Heart, the Holy Face, the 

Child Jesus and in other popular devotions reminding us of Our Blessed Lord. 

Often it is possible to obtain printed forms for use in novenas. 

Prayers said with wilful distractions are of no avail. They are venially sinful if the distraction is deliberate. But, if our mind wanders off our prayers, while we have the intention of actually praying, the prayer is not lost. 

God looks at the intention. Prayer without distraction is more valuable.

We can pray for anything that is good, whether it concerns our spiritual or temporal welfare. But we ought not to become so engrossed in asking for petitions that affect this life as to forget our spiritual needs. If the prayer is not answered, we can rest assured that it was refused only because we lacked faith or the necessary persistency, or because its granting would be harmful to our welfare or that of others. If a prayer is not granted one way, it will be in another. Prayer is never lost. "Ask, and it shall be given you: seek, and you shall find; knock, and it shall be opened to you. For every one that asketh, receiveth; and he that seeketh, findeth; and to him that knocketh, it shall be opened" (Matt. vii, 7-8). "And all things whatsoever you shall ask in prayer, believing, you shall receive" (Matt. xxi, 22). Other similar texts could be quoted. These, statements of Jesus Himself, are sufficient to prove the efficacy of prayer.

PAGE 

XLI TEN COMMANDMENTS BASIS OF ALL RIGHT LIVING, EPITOME OF ALL DIVINE LAW 

The Ten Commandments are the basis of all right living. "If carefully examined and understood," says St. Augustine (Q. cxl. super Exod. lib. ii), "whatever else is commanded by God will be found to depend on the Ten Commandments, which were engraved on those two tables" delivered by God to Moses, "just as these Ten Commandments, in turn, are reducible to two, the love of God and of our neighbor, on which 'depend the whole law and the prophets'."

The reasons advanced by the Catechism of the Council of Trent for our obedience to the Decalogue are: First, God is its author; second, it was proclaimed with great solemnity; third, the observance of the Commandments is not difficult; fourth, the observance is necessary, for "no one who has arrived at the use of reason can be justified, unless he is resolved to keep all of God's commandments;" fifth, their observance is attended by many blessings; sixth, God's goodness invites us to keep them, for, though 

He might have forced us to serve His glory without a reward, He has deigned to identify His own glory with our advantage, thereby rendering what tends to His honor as conducive to our interests, promising us a great reward in heaven, good measure, pressed down, shaken together and running over.

The Commandments were given to Moses after that octogenarian, with miraculous help, had led the Jews out of slavery in Egypt and they were in the desert, on their way to the Promised Land. Fifty days after leaving 

Egypt, the people were gathered at the foot of Mount Sinai, while Moses. went to the summit to get the Commandments of God. Thunder pealed and lightning flashed and Moses' face so shone after his final ascent that he was forced to wear a veil or the people could not look at him. 

The Ten Commandments are (several being shortened a little to aid in memorizing them, for they ought to be known by heart): 

1. I am the Lord thy God: thou shalt not have strange gods before Me. 

2. Thou shalt not take the name of the Lord thy God in vain. 

3. Remember thou keep holy the Sabbath day. 

4. Honor thy father and thy mother. 

5. Thou shalt not kill. 

6. Thou shalt not commit adultery. 

7. Thou shalt not steal. 

8. Thou shalt not bear false witness against thy neighbor. 

9. Thou shalt not covet thy neighbor's wife. 

10. Thou shalt not covet thy neighbor's goods.

Jesus Christ promulgated the Decalogue anew in His Sermon on the Mount and at other times in His public ministry. It was He who reduced the Ten Commandments to two, the love of God and of our neighbor, when He said: "Thou shalt love the Lord thy God with thy whole heart, and with thy whole soul, and with thy whole mind. This is the greatest and first commandment. And the second is like to this: Thou shalt love thy neighbor as thyself. On these two commandments dependeth the whole law and the prophets" (Matt. xxii, 37; Mark xii, 30, 33; Luke x, 27).

PAGE 

The moral precepts in the Ten Commandments were binding on mankind even before they were given on Mount Sinai, because they are a statement of the law of nature, written by God on all human hearts (Rom. ii, 15). But it is easier to fulfill and remember what is so definitely stated as this marvelous summary of all law. The only thing in the Commandments that did not formally pass over into Christianity was the precept about the observance of the Sabbath. The New Testament, for example the Epistle to the Hebrews, firmly teaches that Christians are not held to the Jewish ceremonial law, but nevertheless the Third Commandment is a specific promulgation of that general law of nature demanding that we worship God, and, inasmuch as the Church has set another day as holy instead of the Sabbath, this commandment is still just as binding as the others, with 

Sunday substituted for Saturday. "The two commandments of the love of God and of our neighbor," says the Baltimore Catechism, "contain the whole law of God because all the other commandments are given either to help us to keep these two or to direct us how to shun what is opposed to them.” 

Not only religion, but ordinary decency, demands that the Ten Commandments be kept. Any deviation from them is a crime against society as well as against God. It is a lowering of human dignity. Different denominations vary in the way they number the commandments. 

The 

Decalogue is not numbered in the Bible.

The First Commandment 

The First Commandment is: "I am the Lord thy God, who brought thee out of the land of Egypt, out of the house of bondage. Thou shalt not have strange gods before Me. Thou shalt not make to thyself a graven thing, nor the likeness of anything that is in heaven above, or in the earth beneath, nor of those things that are in the waters under the earth. Thou shalt not adore them, or serve them" (Exodus xx, 2-5).

The commandment is affirmative in that it prescribes acts of religion by which due worship is given to God, and negative in that it forbids the vices opposed to religion. The principal acts of religion are adoration, prayer, sacrifice, vows, oaths, and the sanctification of certain days. We adore God by faith, hope, and charity, by prayer and sacrifice. Forbidden by the First Commandment are the giving to a creature of honor that belongs to God alone, false worship, and all that accords Divine perfections to a creature, such, for instance, as the power of foretelling future events that depend upon man's free will. Belief in spells and charms, the superstitious interpretation of dreams, or consultation of mediums, spiritualists, fortunetellers, etc., breaks the First Commandment, inasmuch as these practices show at least an implicit attributing to creatures of perfections that belong to God alone. Mere curiosity without belief is not sufficient to relieve us of sin in visiting places where there is danger of diabolic interference, such as spirit seances. Often at parties, bazaars, etc., "fortune-telling" is engaged in. This is not sinful if it is not taken seriously and no scandal is given. 

Sins against faith oppose the commandment. If we do not try to know what God has taught, if we refuse to believe all that He has revealed, or neglect to profess our faith in His revelation, we sin against faith. The 

Epistle to the Hebrews (xi, 6) says that "without faith it is impossible to please God." Those who today declare that it does not matter what one

PAGE believes, so long as one "lives right," are wholly misinformed. Christ taught that it would be more tolerable in the Day of Judgment for Sodom and Gomorrha, the ancient cities destroyed for sexual depravity, than for the cities of His own time that refused to accept His Apostles' doctrine (Matt. x, 15). If we refuse to accept part of what He taught, we are heretics. If we refuse to accept Christianity, we are infidels. 

Those who neglect to profess their belief in what God has taught are persons who know the Catholic Church to be true, yet fail to acknowledge her. Christ said that he who denied Him before men would be denied before His Father in heaven (Matt. x, 33).

We must make open profession of our faith as often as God's honor, our neighbor's spiritual good, or our own requires it. Christ promises to confess before His Father in heaven those who confess Him before men (Matt. x, 32). 

Sins against hope also break the First Commandment. They are despair and presumption. Despair is the loss of trust in God's mercy. There is no sin so great that it will not be forgiven if we repent, and it is an insult to God to think that He will not forgive us. Presumption is the rash expectation of salvation without making use of the means necessary to obtain it. An example would be a couple living in a false marriage, feeling that 

God would make a particular exception in their case and take them to heaven. 

We sin against the First Commandment also by lack of charity. We are commanded to love God and to love our neighbor for God's sake. Every sin, particularly mortal sin, offends one or both of these precepts.

The early Protestants interpreted the First Commandment as forbidding the use of images or the honor due to saints. If the commandment forbade images, we could not have photographs or pictures of any kind, whether for religious or secular purposes. But the Jews to whom the commandment was given had images in their temple, by order of God. There were golden cherubim over the Ark of the Covenant (Exodus xxv, 18) and brazen oxen held up the huge laver in which the priests bathed before officiating. (See 

II Kings xvi, 17, King James version.) It is true that there were decided limits on the statuary allowed the Jews, owing to the danger of idolatry in the ancient world, but the fact that even one statue was permitted gave the sanction of God to the principle. The First Commandment forbids the making of statues or pictures if they are to be adored as gods, but it is in accord with it that we pray before the crucifix and images or holy pictures, in order to have them enliven our devotion by reminding us of those whom they represent. Of course we do not pray to them, for they cannot hear us. 

It is to those whom they represent that we pray. Any honor given to 

Catholic images is purely relative, going to the one for whom the statue stands.

Far from forbidding prayer to the saints and angels, the commandment implicitly orders it. We honor God in His works, and the saints are His masterpieces. We cannot honor an artist if we despise his painting, and neither can we honor God if we despise His saints or angels. We can and often do pray directly to God. But we are sinners and the saints and angels are spotless; hence their prayers may sometimes be more acceptable to God than our own. Being face to face with God at all times, they instantly know when we pray to them. Christian tradition is saturated with

PAGE 

the doctrine of the invocation of the saints. The Scriptures have many instances in which the prayers of fellow creatures are sought. Death, it must be remembered, does not obliterate us. We live eternally. If I can pray for you now, I can pray for you after my soul is separated from the body.

Common sense demands the Catholic doctrine of the invocation and honor of the saints. If I want a favor from a governor and his mother thinks well of me, I shall likely work through her. In the same manner, I often ask the help of God's Mother, for I know that she loves me. 

In the Apostles' Creed, we say that we believe in the communion of the saints. This means that we and they are members of the mystical body of which Christ is the head and we are the members, and that those members who need help receive it from one another, just as in the human body. The blessed in heaven, the faithful on earth, and the souls in purgatory all belong to this mystical body. The faithful on earth are assisted by one another and by the prayers of the blessed in heaven, while the souls in purgatory are helped both by us on earth and by the blessed in paradise. 

The First Commandment also implicitly orders us to honor the relics of the saints. We are told in the Old Testament that when a dead man touched the bones of the prophet Eliseus (Elisha), the dead man came to life. (See II Kings xiii, 21, King James version.) In the New Testament, we are told that miracles were worked by the touching of the hem of 

Christ's garment (Matt. ix, 20; xiv, 36); that handkerchiefs and aprons touched to the body of St. Paul, and then taken to the sick or demoniacs, wrought cures (Acts xix, 12), and that even the shadow of St. Peter effected miracles (Acts v, 15). Hence there is abundant Scriptural backing for the veneration we give to the relics of the saints. 

The First Commandment forbids us to engage in false worship. It is not for man, but for God, to decide how God must be worshiped. I have no right to twist the worship of God to suit my personal convenience, and neither had Martin Luther, John Knox, or anybody else. While we recognize that non-Catholics are generally sincere in their form of worship, this does not make it right. Hence, we are not allowed to join them in it. 

We can assist as passive spectators, but never actively, if there is a good reason for our being present, as at a funeral, a wedding, a graduation, or as a representative of the civil government on special occasions.

PAGE 

XLII THE SECOND COMMANDMENT 

The Second Commandment is: "Thou shalt not take the name of the 

Lord thy God in vain: for the Lord will not hold him guiltless that shall take the name of the Lord his God in vain" (Exodus xx, 7).

We take the name of God in vain when we irreverently say it, deride religion, blaspheme, curse, take unlawful oaths, or break our vows.

We are not permitted to use the name of God, Scriptural quotations, or other things intimately connected with salvation in joking, anger, or in other careless ways.

This applies as well to the Blessed Virgin and the saints or good angels, because of their holiness.

He who scoffs at religion or the ceremonies of the Church breaks this commandment. He who uses abusive or contemptuous language against 

God or holy things is guilty of blasphemy. It is blasphemous to utter denials of truths that are guaranteed by God, such as the virgin birth of Christ. One can blaspheme in thought, if one wilfully yields to contemptuous thought in the way that, uttered, it would constitute spoken blasphemy.

Therefore, we are ordered by the Second Commandment to speak with reverence of God, of His saints and angels, and of all holy things, and to keep our lawful oaths and vows.

An oath is the calling upon God to witness the truth of what we declare. This is what properly constitutes "swearing," although popularly one is deemed "to swear" when one blasphemes, uses vulgarity, curses, etc.

It is lawful and sometimes even obligatory to take an oath when ordered by authority or it is required for the honor of God, our own or our neighbor's good. It is greviously sinful not to fulfill a lawful oath, if we are able to do so.

Sin is committed by us if we swear falsely or when in doubt; when we swear or have others take an oath without necessity; when we swear to do what is evil or to omit what is good.
