An oath to do evil or to omit good is not binding and it would be sinful to fulfill it.* False swearing or perjury, especially in courts of justice, is one of the great evils of our times.

A vow is a deliberate promise made to God to do something that is pleasing to him. It can concern something to which we are not otherwise obliged (as a vow to say two Rosaries daily) or something which it would be sinful to offend against even without the vow (as chastity in a nun). 

If a vow is taken to omit some sin, and then the sin is committed, a double sin is charged to us. But, if the vow is kept, there is likewise a double virtue, as the keeping of a vow is worthy of Divine reward in itself. 

Not to fulfill our vows is a mortal or venial sin according to the intention we had when making the vow. If I vow to say the Rosary daily, it is a mortal or venial sin if I omit it, according to my intention at the *Lutherans, at confirmation, take an oath never to leave their Church. Sometimes, after a course of Catholic instructions, they are bothered about whether this oath must be kept and they must stay out of the Catholic Church in which they now believe. The oath is not binding on them. A man must follow his conscience in order to be saved.

PAGE 

time of making the vow. In some cases, however, the Church determines the binding force, as when one enters a religious order, to become a sister, a brother, etc. In these vows, and in the obligation of chastity resting on her major clergy, the Church informs us that it is mortally sinful to break the vow by committing a mortal sin against the virtue promised by the vow -two mortal sins are then committed, one against the virtue, one against the vow.

If a venial sin against the virtue is committed, there is another venial sin against the vow. Both the Old and the New Testament unquestionably uphold the validity of vows, and Martin Luther was absolutely un-Scriptural when he broke his vow of chastity and took as his wife a nun who broke hers. "Thou shalt pay thy vows," says Job xxii, 27. The Acts of the Apostles story of the reason why St. Paul went to the Temple at 

Jerusalem when he was arrested before his appeal to the Roman emperor is sufficient guarantee that God is pleased by the fulfillment of vows. 

If one has made a vow that seems too difficult to keep, one should consult a Father Confessor, who can arrange for a dispensation if this is advisable. The Pope can dispense from any vow, even the obligation of chastity in the clergy. If there is a good reason why a religious should leave his or her community, this can be arranged through dispensation, and it is by no means an unheard of thing to have such a dispensation given. 

Obviously such a step is not to be encouraged. 

Cursing, which is forbidden by the Second Commandment, is an expression of hatred or of anger by which we wish evil to ourselves or our neighbors. It is a mortal sin if great evil is wished. If we curse animals or inanimate things, the sin is venial, unless great scandal is caused. 

Cursing often includes blasphemy and, if deliberate, is then mortally sinful, regardless of whether, simply as a curse, it is venial. 

Some sectarians refuse to take oaths because of the statement of Christ, "I say to you not to swear at all" (Matt. v, 34). Catholic exegetes contend that this text is not meant to forbid every type of swearing, but only rash and profane swearing, in common discourse, without necessity.

PAGE 

XLIII WHY SUNDAY MUST BE KEPT AS LORD'S DAY 

The Third Commandment is: "Remember that thou keep holy the 

Sabbath day. Six days shalt thou labor, and do all thy works; but on the seventh day is the Sabbath of the Lord thy God; thou shalt do no work on it, neither thou nor thy son, nor thy daughter, nor thy man servant, nor thy maid-servant, nor thy beast, nor the stranger that is within thy gates. For in six days the Lord made heaven and earth, and the sea, and all things that are in them, and rested on the seventh day; wherefore the Lord blessed the seventh day and sanctified it" (Exodus xx, 8-11).

Reason proves that God exists and that He is our Creator, without whom we cannot be happy. It shows our dependence upon Him, and the necessity of internal and external worship. The Third Commandment prescribes part of the external worship that we owe to God. The other commandments of the Decalogue are expressions of the natural law or the law of reason written on the human heart, whose wisdom nobody having a normal mind can question without a warning from conscience. They are obligatory at all times and unchangeable. The law of nature, however, no more prescribes special worship on the Sabbath than it does on any other day, and the Third Commandment is, consequently, considered a part of the old ceremonial law of the Jews, which was abrogated upon the foundation of the Christian Church. The Epistles to the Galatians, the Colossians and the Hebrews, as well as other parts of the New Testament, make evident that the ceremonial law of the Jews is abrogated and that to look upon Mosaic practices as now necessary to salvation is to oppose true Christianity. 

In Galatians, St. Paul says: "You observe days and months and times and years; I am afraid of you lest perhaps I have labored in vain amongst you" (iv, 10). The point under discussion by him is the observance of the ceremonial law of the Jews by Christian converts as a necessity to salvation, and the Jewish holy days are, consequently, held by the Apostle as not binding. 

For this reason, no scholar can hold with the Seventh Day Adventists and other little sects of our own day that consider it evil to keep Sunday instead of 

Saturday as the day of rest and worship.

The word Sabbath is Hebrew and signifies "cessation." The Sabbath of the Jews was Saturday. In the Catholic Church, which is imitated by nearly all the Christian sects in this practice, Sunday is kept holy instead of Saturday. The Apostles themselves made the change from Saturday to 

Sunday and tradition tells us that they did it because Christ rose from the dead on Sunday and the Holy Ghost descended upon the disciples on this day. It is, therefore, by the authority of the Catholic Church that the precepts of the Third Commandment are transferred from the seventh to the first day in the week. St. John mentions "the Lord's day" in the Apocalypse i, 10, and St. Paul commands that certain collections be made on the first day of the week (I Cor. xvi, 2)—the same as we would do today if we wanted to take up a church collection and make sure that the greatest number of people would be there to give. The Church's official name for 

Sunday is "Dominica," which in Latin means Lord's day.

By the same power with which she designated Sunday as holy, the Church also sets aside other days throughout the year, known as holy days of obligation, when we are bound to follow the same regime as is com-

PAGE 

manded for Sunday. The Church can change these days at will, and make alterations in the schedule of holy days for different nations. 

In the United States, the holy days of obligation are: Feast of Christ's 

Circumcision (New Year's Day); Ascension Thursday (marking Christ's ascension into heaven, the date being variable as it falls forty days after 

Easter); Feast of the Assumption (August 15), commemorating the fact that the Blessed Virgin's body was rejoined to her soul in heaven after death and she now reigns there in her complete self, a fact that we know by tradition; the Feast of All Saints (Nov. 1, honoring the memory of all the human beings in heaven and seeking their prayers for us); the 

Feast of the Immaculate Conception of the Blessed Virgin (Dec. 8, com memorating the fact that, in preview of her wonderful dignity as the Mother of His Son, God preserved her from the taint of original sin, a grace gained for her by her own Son's death on the cross); and the Feast of Christ's 

Nativity (Christmas day).

The Third Commandment as renewed by the Church orders us to worship God on Sundays and holy days of obligation, and the Church tells us that we are to do this by hearing Mass, by prayer and other good works. 

The Church also says that we should avoid all unnecessary servile work, public trading, or whatever else may interfere with the proper observance of the Lord's day.

By servile works, we mean those labors that require exertion of the body, rather than of the mind, as farming, machinery repair work, etc. 

But if the honor of God, the good of our neighbor, or necessity require them, these works are permitted. The commandment does not forbid works that are chiefly mental, as the operation of a typewriter, painting in water colors, the sewing of "fancy work" (for pleasure, not for business, in this latter case), etc. There is also nothing in it to oppose doing light housework, such as tidying up a room or preparing a meal. Heavier housework, however, should be avoided. If a girl employed throughout the week cannot keep her clothes in decent condition except by mending and pressing them on Sundays, this is not sinful. The Church expects us to use common sense in the interpretation of her laws. There is absolutely nothing in the Scriptures to uphold sectarian theology that would make it sinful to proceed at more than a walk, to travel, to read newspapers, or to take innocent pleasure on Sunday. These sectarians are followers of the 

Pharisaical idea of Sunday, not the Christian. Christ repeatedly condemned such teachings. He reminded us that the Sabbath was made for man, not man for the Sabbath.

He who makes a baptized person break the Sunday work commandment is guilty of sin himself. Employers are bound to see that their employes have the opportunity of keeping Sunday as a day of rest, unless there is necessity for the work. How about public Sunday amusements? Where they are necessary, they are all right, and they seem to be advisable in the intense life of America. The Church gives no leeway to strainers at gnats and swallowers of camels who would make Sunday Puritanical. People cannot rest when they are bored to death, and a blue Sunday is, under the cover, often one filled with vice. 

The prohibition in the Third Commandment against work of beasts of burden on the Sabbath was put there to keep man from working. If the

PAGE beast works, the man does also. But the command must not be interpreted as forbidding a pleasure drive with a horse on Sunday. 

The precept against servile work on Sunday binds under pain of mortal sin. Father Thomas Slater, S.J., in A Manual of Moral Theology, vol. 1, pp. 174-175, says: "If, however, the matter be light, the doing of a little servile work on a Sunday will be only a venial sin, and none at all if there be a good reason for it. According to the common opinion, it would be necessary to work for well over two hours at something which is forbidden in order to commit a grave sin. Still longer time would be required for a mortal sin in doing servile work of a lighter kind, which had for it some sort of excuse on the ground that it helps on the cause of religion and charity. Making rosary beads or scapulars belongs to this category. Public trading is also forbidden on Sundays, as well as judicial proceedings in the exercise of contentious jurisdiction, and the solemn and public taking of oaths (Can. 1248)."

PAGE 

 XLIV HONOR TO OUR PARENTS AND SUPERIORS IS COMMANDED BY LAW OF GOD 

The Fourth Commandment is: "Honor thy father and thy mother, that thou mayest be long lived upon the land which the Lord thy God will give thee" (Exodus xx, 12).

The direct object of this commandment is obedience, not only to our parents, but to all lawful superiors in those things in which they have power over us, and its indirect object is that parents should take proper care of their children, and other superiors of those under them. 

Authority, according to Apostolic teaching, comes from God and disobedience to properly constituted authority, whether it be to our parents, to our religious superiors, to the civil authorities, etc., is disobedience to God. "He that resisteth the power resisteth the ordinance of God," declared St. 

Paul (Rom. xiii, 2). 

If we seriously fail in love, respect, obedience, and assistance to our parents, and act with sufficient reflection and full consent of the will, the sin is mortal. If our failure in these things is trifling, the sin is venial. 

Parents or other superiors who fail, in serious matters, to take proper care of those under them are likewise guilty of mortal sin, and are guilty of venial sin in little matters.

We must show affection to our parents, we must not reveal their shortcomings or calumniate them, we must not provoke them without just reason, we must not cause them sorrow, we must not wish them evil. While an extreme case can be imagined where a child could refuse temporarily to recognize a parent-as when a daughter was being inducted into the presidency of a college and a drunken sot of a father reeled in and claimed relationship before the crowd-ordinarily this cannot be tolerated, and insults or ridicule of our parents, being ashamed of them, or even refusing to show ordinary marks of affection for them, are in nearly all cases mortally sinful.

A child is usually bound to obey the parent until of legal age, except if emancipated from the home by marriage, etc. Even after that, what a parent advises should always be considered with respect. A child, however, is never bound to obey when a parent orders him to commit sin or when a parent unreasonably opposes a religious vocation. In case a parent shows himself constantly unreasonable, the child should consult with his Father Confessor about the extent of obedience demanded. The child himself is usually a poor judge of what is reasonable or unreasonable in a parent, as most human beings are rebels during childhood and need to be curbed. 

The fact that he who spares the rod spoils the child is Scriptural advice.* 

Respect for authority must be taught in the home. Children who are permitted to haggle with their parents over every command are not being given that training in will power necessary for the rightful ordering of their later life. The truly Christian home is not a debating society; it is an absolute monarchy. Even when a child is forced by conscience to disobey *"He that spareth the rod hateth his son: but he that loveth him correcteth him betimes” (Proverbs xiii, 24). God expects us to administer punishment with mercy.

PAGE a parent, as when the latter commands him to commit a sin, he must show respect and is advised to exercise greater alacrity for a time in obedience to good commands. The modern contempt for authority, which teaches that a child must never be curbed, but that the tables must be turned and the child is to rule the parents instead of the parents ruling the child, has no place in Christianity. Parents are to be warned, however, that cruelty is a poor way to win a child's heart. 

Mercy and kindness will often win the most stubborn will.

Parents are bound in conscience to give good example to their children, and if they commit mortal sins that prove a scandal to their little ones they sin doubly, offending both against the virtue and also by scandal. The parents must look after the physical needs of their children, see that they are properly educated, and guide them in religion. Where there is opportunity of sending the child to a Catholic school, the parent is seriously bound in conscience to do this, as experience has proved that only in this way is the child given proper religious training. If there is no Catholic school available, the parent is gravely bound in conscience to see that religious instruction is given in some other way. The best method is to have regular catechism recitations in the home. 

The parent has a right to the assistance of the child when the latter can earn money and the parent is in need, but the child is not a chattel slave and it is false to hold that he has no rights at all over what he earns. 

Furthermore, it is false to hold that the property of the child is at the whim of the parent. Some parents make a terrible mistake by allowing too much freedom in money matters to girls and boys in their wildest years; others sin by regarding children as virtual slaves, with no rights over property or money.

Perhaps the most frequent mistake in modern parents is their tendency to imagine that their own children are too angelic to fall before the temptations that beset others. The careful mother and father will always make the boy and girl under age account for where they spend their time.

Christianity demands that we show obedience and proper respect for all our other superiors, whether it be the civil government, our employers, our spiritual superiors, or whatever else they may be, in the things in which they have power over us. The most acceptable theory of government is that power comes to the ruler from God but through the people, and that, if the ruler is tyrannous and the majority of the people are in favor of a change, they have a right to make it. Revolution by a disgruntled minority, however, is immoral. The exercise of the ballot is, frequently, a matter of conscience. If religion or justice is in danger, it is difficult to excuse the non-voter from sin, except when it is too difficult for him to visit the polls.

PAGE 

 XLV COMMANDMENT, “THOU SHALT NOT KILL,” BROKEN TODAY BY DESPOILERS OF UNBORN 

The Fifth Commandment is: "Thou shalt not kill" (Exodus xx, 13).

It forbids all sins that do injury to one's own life or that of others and, by analogy, likewise prohibits injury to the life of our neighbor's soul by scandal.

Among these sins are murder, striking or wounding in an unjust manner, and the embitterment or shortening of another's life by vexatious or cruel treatment. Fighting, hatred, revenge, and bad example also must be numbered among these sins.

As the Baltimore Catechism says: "We are commanded by the Fifth 

Commandment to live in peace and union with our neighbor, to respect his rights, to seek his bodily and spiritual welfare, and to take proper care of our own life and health."

Murder is one of the sins that cry to heaven for vengeance, according to the Scripture. It invades the rights of God, who has given us no such power over the lives of our fellows; it is an attack on human society, which cannot have an orderly existence with private individuals given such power over others, and it is the greatest temporal wrong that can be done to the murdered person, while it is also often a cause of his spiritual ruin, as many are plunged into eternity this way without having the proper preparation. 

Murder is murder, no matter how young the person may be who is killed. For this reason, a wilful abortion, no matter in how early a stage it occurs, is murder. Those who procure abortion, not excluding the mother, incur excommunication from the Church, if abortion has actually taken place (Canon 2350, Code of Canon Law). The number of abortions today is very great. This is the worst form of birth control, but mortally sinful also is the use of contraceptives, to prevent conception. The latter sin is not murder, but is mortally sinful because it thwarts the course of nature. 

Abortions are sometimes suggested by doctors, for the mother's sake. 

In these cases, it is never lawful to place any direct action that will murder the child. If the mother is ill and some medicine is given to her, or an operation that is performed is not directly inclined to kill the child, but the child may accidentally die, the step necessary can be taken to save the mother, provided the abortion is guarded against. If no other course can be taken except directly to kill the child, it is not lawful to use such means, even though both child and mother will otherwise die. We are not permitted to do an evil act with the hope of a good end. Catholic theology nowhere upholds the adage that "the end justifies the means." To permit a mother to die in these circumstances might seem harsh viewed only from the standpoint of time, but a mother who willingly goes to death under such conditions would, it seems, gain the crown of martyrdom. Doctors often exaggerate the danger in these cases. 

It is lawful to slay in defense of one's country in war or, when necessary, to kill or maim in just self-defense. Romans xiii, 4, shows that it is lawful for the government to execute criminals. 

Dueling is forbidden, and those who fight a duel, or who make or accept a challenge thereto, or who give any aid to it or favor it in any

PAGE way, or who purposely go to see it, or permit it, or do not stop it insofar as they can, fall under the excommunication of the Church (Canon 2351). 

Nobody has the right, on his private authority, to put a malefactor to death in the same way as society deals out capital punishment. But legitimate self-defense against an unjust aggressor permits us, if necessary, to go even to the extent of killing him or her, without sin. The person defending himself should not intend the death of the aggressor, but merely self-protection; he should have no other means of defending his life; and he should have recourse to violence only in case of actual assault. Hence if there were merely fear that another was going to attack us, we would not be justified in killing him. We are never justified in killing or doing serious bodily injury merely to vindicate our reputation.

There is no moral justification for murder or serious bodily injury to avenge attacks on matrimonial infidelity. But a man could act to prevent an attack about to be committed against his wife. Just as we are allowed to protect ourselves against an unjust aggressor, so can we protect others, under the same conditions. We are not obliged to do this unless we are charged to watch over the public safety, or the person attacked is of importance to the community, or is a near relative. Even then we would not be obliged to go to their defense if the aggressor would be likely to do serious damage to ourselves.

Catholic moralists recognize the right to take the life of or to do bodily injury to the enemy in case of just warfare. By a war we mean the condition in which two or more nations contest with death-dealing instruments or when public order is re-established by force of arms. Most of the wars in history have been unjust and it is doubtful whether there have ever been any in which at least one contender was not unjust. 

A war can be justified on the same principles as self-defense in an individual. If a nation has been invaded, its rights have been trespassed or threatened, it can morally go to war. Unlike the individual, it is not necessary for it to wait until it is actually attacked, if there is a certainty that danger exists. If a war has been unjustly started, but the nation, as a result of it, is put into danger of great harm or extinction, citizens who could not give their support at the beginning can do so now under the principle of self-defense. The usual just war is a defensive one; but an offensive one can be justified if it is necessary to obtain due compensation or this is the only way in which the balance of justice can be restored. Mere desire for aggrandizement or territory does not ordinarily justify warfare. 

A soldier has the right and even the duty to kill his foes in war, but should spare those who have no part in the struggle. Sometimes the innocent accidentally suffer as the result of bombardments, sieges, etc., but all reasonable precaution should be taken to warn and to protect them and they must not be directly attacked. If they accidentally suffer, after these steps have been taken, the attacking army is not morally culpable. 

If a war is evidently unjust, a man does not have the right to volunteer as a soldier. If there is any doubt as to whether it is just, and he has already enlisted or been drafted, he should obey his superiors. Practically, the private or minor officer can nearly always enlist of fight with a clear conscience, as only the chiefs of any nation have sufficient information to make a clear estimate of the injustice of warfare. In many unjust wars,

PAGE 

even the leaders have gone into them with clear consciences, as they have been swayed by invincible prejudice or ignorance from making a correct judgment.

Christianity should strive by every decent means to eliminate war. 

But it must never be forgotten that some evils are worse. 

Christ taught peace on earth, but to men of good will. And He never argued for peace at any cost, else He would not have permitted His own crucifixion. 

Almost every war brings a train of horrible sins. The first World war descended to ordinary murder when officers, even Americans, ordered soldiers to kill helpless prisoners, in order not to be bothered with their care.

Suicide is voluntary self-murder. It is never permitted, even to escape an attack on purity. Only God has the right to determine the length of our probation, and suicide is a serious crime against Him, against ourselves, and against society. The Church deprives a suicide of Christian burial, but if there is any doubt at all as to the mental soundness of the suicide or if there has been evidence of repentance before death, she gives him the privilege of a Christian funeral. A deliberate suicide, who dies without repentance, is sure of hell, except when he has not been mentally responsible. 

We are permitted, however, to perform an act that will probably cause death to ourselves if there is no direct intention of committing suicide and there is a grave reason for the act (for instance, if an aviator in time of war flew close to a powder magazine to destroy it, knowing that it would probably cause his own death; or if a priest, physician, or nurse underwent great danger in caring for the sick). 

Anger and quarreling are forbidden by the Fifth Commandment. By the former, we mean desire to do injury to another or the doing of serious injury, in revenge or through loss of temper. This sin becomes mortal if there is the aim to do serious injury. It is also mortally sinful deliberately to arouse another to a point where he wishes to do serious damage to ourselves or to others. Ordinary loss of temper is just an imperfection or at most a venial sin.

We are also forbidden by the commandment to mutilate a member or organ of the body, unless its removal is necessary for the preservation of the rest of the body (this is never permitted to preserve purity, escape being drafted in war, etc.); to deprive ourselves or to overwork to the extent that we lose our health or shorten our lives, unless a grave reason justifies the act (then it often becomes a great virtue); or to practice excessive mortification that ruins our health or makes the fulfillment of our duties impossible or too difficult. Mortification, however, can go to a great limit without involving sin, and in fact it is usually one of the greatest virtues. 

Experience is the best teacher as to how much we can stand. 

By scandal, which is also forbidden by the Fifth Commandment, we mean the doing of any act that will probably lead others into sin. We are not bound to keep from scandalizing Pharisees who swallow camels and strain at gnats, but St. Paul urged the Corinthians to avoid even some acts that were lawful in order not to prove a stumbling block to the weak or the poorly instructed.

In any of the sins against the Fifth Commandment, we must repair, or at least have the intention to repair, so far as possible, the injury we have done our neighbor, before the sin can be forgiven.

PAGE 

XLVI EXPERTS ISSUE DOCUMENT ON MORALITY OF WAR 

A comprehensive statement on the ethics of war and peace-representing the first concrete action in an organized Catholic movement in the United States toward international peace-was issued several years ago. 

It is the report of the Committee on International Ethics of the Catholic Association for International Peace. The document was drawn up by a group headed by Monsignor John A. Ryan, director of the Social Action 

Department of the National Catholic Welfare Conference. Other members of the group were: The Rev. Dr. Charles Bruehl, St. Charles Borromeo seminary, Overbrook, Pa.; the Rev. J. P. Donovan, C.M., Kenrick seminary, Webster Groves, Mo.; the Rev. Cyprian Emanuel, O.F.M., Franciscan monastery, Cleveland; the Rev. Leo Charles Gainor, O.P., Providence college, Providence, R. I.; the Rt. Rev. Monsignor Michael J. Grupa, SS. Cyril and 

Methodius' seminary, Orchard Lake, Mich.; the Rev. Linus Lilly, S.J., St. 

Louis university; the Rev. Moorhouse F. X. Millar, S.J., Fordham university, New York, and the Rev. Charles C. Miltner, C.S.C., Notre Dame university, Notre Dame, Ind.

This group, after preparing the report, presented it at a national meeting of the association, where it was exhaustively discussed. 

It was then revised and presented to the executive committee of the association, which ordered it published. It was issued by the Paulist Press, New York. 

States Bound by Moral Law 

Some of the outstanding assertions in the document are: 

States, like individuals, are subject to the moral precepts of both nature and revelation.

Only on five specific conditions-if observed, they "would make war practically impossible," is a state justified in entering upon war. These conditions are set forth as follows: "Actual or certainly imminent violation of rights; moral certainty that this is the situation; a degree of evil in the injury proportionate to the evils involved in war; inefficiency of peace means, and a well-grounded hope of bringing about better conditions." "Banez was right when he compared a declaration of war to a sentence of death. If a jury or a judge is morally forbidden to condemn a man to death unless they feel assured of his guilt beyond all reasonable doubt, how can it be reasonably maintained that a civil ruler is justified in virtually pronouncing the death sentence upon hundreds of thousands of men while he still has a reasonable doubt that the rights of his country are undergoing violation?"

Yet, the right to wage war under extreme conditions, and of selfdefense "by all legitimate means," belongs to a state, and it also has the right to require its members to "defend the common good against aggression." 

Undue Prolongation of War Immoral 

"To continue a war longer than is necessary for the protection or vindication of rights is quite as immoral as to begin it unnecessarily.” "If victory crowns the arms of the state whose cause was just, it confers no right to exact more than adequate reparations and indemnities,

PAGE 

while charity may require these obligations to be postponed or reduced or entirely condoned and cancelled."

Since "no victorious nation can be trusted to treat the conquered nation with either justice or charity," it is desirable that "peace treaties should be made under the supervision of some impartial tribunal.” 

Intervention by one state in the affairs of another is "sometimes required by the law of natural morality and the law of Christianity," but such evils as the following, singly or in groups, must be "definite, certain, and extreme" and hope must be wanting of any remedy from within: "Grave and long-continued oppression of one state by another, the revolt of a people or a nation against intolerable tyranny, the unsuccessful efforts of a state to put down a rebellion which injures national or international welfare, grossly immoral practices, such as cannibalism and human sacrifices under the guise of religion, and continued anarchy in a state that is for the present unable to maintain a tolerably competent government. 

Of course, a state has no right whatever to use armed force in the affairs of another so long as milder methods, even those of moral coercion, are sufficient."

Protection of the lives or property of nationals in another land, or insistence on proper treatment of official representatives in another country, may never rightly be carried to the extent of using armed force unless all peaceful means have failed, such as negotiations, severing diplomatic relations, and putting an embargo on trade.

That the natural law always requires the protection of the lives and property of nationals in foreign countries "may be doubted." But selfpreservation, which implies the right of self-defense, may also include such protection of lives and property. "All the moral presumptions" are against going to war to prevent another country from fostering seditious doctrines and movements in its territory against a state, but the right to prevent such hostile action "by just means" is recognized. 

Morality of Conquest 

Conquest and forcible annexation of territory once subject to a state are wrongful, but occupation of a sparsely developed territory without an organized government worthy of the name may be justified. This occupation, however, implies heavy obligations on the part of the occupying state, such as safeguarding all the natural rights of the natives, including property; providing for their education, physical, mental, and moral; and developing their capacity for some measure of self-government. 

The "right of self-determination," while often valid, is not universally so, and a state sometimes has a right to prevent the independence of national minorities; the possibility of doing the subordinate group more harm than good, or of working grave injury on the dominant state, must be considered. National minorities within a state are entitled to have their rights and welfare safeguarded by the dominant state. 

Unjust treaties are not always morally obligatory, but treaties which terminate wars are universally binding unless made under extreme duress and inflicting extreme injustice.

New forms of government should perform all international obligations contracted by their predecessors.

PAGE 

Obligation of Promoting Peace 

Having expressed these and other conclusions on the various phases of peace and war, the report takes up at length the sub-heading, "The Obligation of Promoting Peace." Chief among the methods it urges are the curbing of nationalism and "excessive patriotism," and the promotion of "a reasonable and moderate internationalism." "Probably the most powerful interferences with international intercourse," the report asserts in another section, "are customs tariffs, export taxes, and embargoes and restrictions upon immigration." 

Summing up the methods now available to promote peace, the report suggests: "The substitution of moral right for material force, general disarmament, compulsory arbitration of disputes among states, the codification of international law, an international tribunal of justice and an association of nations, such is a complete and coherent summary of the practical methods available and necessary for preventing war and assuring peace."

PAGE  XLVII SIXTH AND NINTH COMMANDMENTS OF GOD; PRACTICAL SUGGESTIONS FOR CHASTE LIVING 

The Sixth Commandment is: "Thou shalt not commit adultry" (Exodus xx, 14). 

The Ninth Commandment is: "Thou shalt not covet thy neighbor's wife" (See Exodus xx, 17).

These commandments order us to practice the virtue of chastity and forbid anything that is against this virtue. The sixth deals with acts of impurity; the ninth, with the desire for them. 

There is

Chastity is commanded by the natural law, although it is perhaps the most difficult test to which one is put in the present probation. no worse slavery than that to which impurity reduces its addicts. It leads to innumerable other sins and has more to do with the weakening of faith than any other evil. St. Alphonsus Liguori taught that most of those suffering in the fires of hell are damned directly because of impurity, while nobody is in agony there unless he is guilty of this sin as well as others. On this earth, nothing so speedily leads to loss of reputation or family discord as loss of chastity, but purity is a flower bed in which every other virtue readily flourishes.

It makes men like angels: whence its name, the angelic virtue. So glorious is this virtue in the eyes of God that He never permitted a suggestion of impurity to be charged against His Son, while He kept Christ's mother ever a spotless virgin, put Him under the care of the virgin St. 

Joseph, and had Him select, as His most beloved disciple, the virgin St. 

John. The Catholic Church has ever been conspicuous for her love of purity, as evidenced by her thousands of clergy and religious vowed to this form of life.

Chastity is of three kinds: Virginal, which consists in constant abstaining from unlawful pleasures of the flesh or the voluntary desire for them; conjugal, which consists in the use of marriage in accord with religion and reason; that of widowhood, which consists in leading a life of continence after the death of one's spouse.

Lust, or the love of fleshy pleasure, is mortally sinful outside marriage, if it is indulged in with sufficient reflection and full consent of the will. 

Married people are compelled by justice to render the marital rights to each other when asked for, unless under conditions that are clearly opposed to common sense, but the use of contraceptives turns this sanctified right into mortal sin.

The right given to mankind in marriage cannot, however, be urged as an excuse for false marriages. If people are the victims of split-up marriages, from which they cannot be freed, they must be content to lead single lives. Grace will not be denied them if their will is right. It is wrong to contend that one must have a religious vocation in order to lead a decent single life. 

Unchaste talk, songs, looks, touches, reading, or any other action leading to impurity is forbidden. Usually, these sins are mortal, although some people might be able to put themselves into more danger than others without feeling unlawful pleasure and might thus have less sin or even

PAGE only venial sin. We must remember, however, that there is often grave danger of scandal, i.e. of leading others into sin against this virtue. 

Impure thoughts or desires are also forbidden. These become sinful only if they are wilful. If there is doubt about whether they are consented to, recall the advice of the saints: If we can remember having invoked the name of Mary at the time, we can rest assured that we have not given consent, because of her power in upholding the virtue of purity. 

For the Catholic woman, devotion to the Blessed Virgin is by far the most powerful deterrent against sins of the flesh. There is less impurity among practical Catholics, both men and women, than among any other people, and devotion to the Blessed Virgin and the power of Holy Communion are the cause.

The chief means for preserving purity or overcoming impurity are: 

Frequency in the sacraments of Confession and Communion; devotion to the Blessed Virgin; prayer; constant vigilance; avoidance of the deliberate occasions of sin, such as risky reading, attendance at questionable shows or dances; avoidance of companions who are a temptation to us; the cultivation of temperance, both as to liquors and foods; the avoidance of too rich foods; deliberate mortification of the flesh, even to actual fasting if necessary.

Sometimes, people are so prone to impurity that they must wage a constant warfare. It is the obsessing thing in life with them. If they are keeping up the good fight, even though impurity should sometimes happen, let them remember that if it is not deliberate it is not mortally sinful, sometimes not even venially sinful. If it is engaged in with another, of course, it cannot help but be mortally sinful.

Some people pretend to be so prudish that every time the question is mentioned from the pulpit or put into print they seem to be terribly horrified, saying that it should not be talked about. It is true that St. Paul taught that the vice should not even be mentioned, but it is plain that he meant in ordinary conversation. Pharisaical prudery fools nobody except the subjects themselves; it reminds one of the woman described by Shakespeare, who boasted too much of her virtue. Ordinary instruction must be given on this point, and the fact that it is not given to growing children often leads them to obtain the information where it will do them untold harm. Boys especially are led into the net of vice by forming habits which they would not have if they had known sufficiently how to evade them. 

The young should know that sins against purity bring on the most horrible diseases and lead to quick degeneracy. This instruction ought to be given to the son by his father, the girl by her mother, and nowadays it should be given early. A frigid person who is rarely tempted should remember that his or her son or daughter may not be similarly gifted by nature. 

Nothing is to be feared from proper instruction in the home; everything is to be feared from not giving it.

Unchaste living cannot usually be stopped gradually. It has to be suddenly terminated, for the sin has the effect of constantly increasing desire. We are like dirt dams. If a trickle of water goes over the top, the dam is in serious danger. 

In dealing with this vice, common sense is necessary. The sexual appetite is perfectly natural to us. If Adam had not sinned, we would be

PAGE  

without it, but by a special gift of God. So far as our animal nature is concerned, the appetite is as much to be expected in us as in the lower beasts. For this reason, it is necessary to insist that sin does not enter unless the sexual pleasure is sought or desired. Night emissions of one's seed are not sinful unless one has placed an unlawful act or had deliberate thoughts before going to sleep from which one foresees that the emission will occur.

In the same way, the involuntary emissions that many persons have during waking hours cannot be regarded as sinful unless the conditions are placed for them and we have no right to place those conditions. We are not compelled to keep from horseback-riding, swimming, from taking positions necessary for sleep, etc., in order to avoid pollutions, if we do these things, not to cause the pollutions, but to effect the other results. 

We are never permitted, however, directly to commit any impure act with the purpose of obtaining any good or indifferent end; for impurity is in itself intrinsically evil.

PAGE 

XLVIII "THOU SHALT NOT STEAL”-NECESSITY OF MAKING RESTITUTION FOR ILL-GOTTEN GOODS 

The Seventh Commandment is: "Thou shalt not steal" (Exodus xx, 15).

It commands that we give to every man what belongs to him and that we respect his property rights. It forbids all unjust taking or keeping of what belongs to another. If we have taken what belongs to another, we are bound to restore it or its value, insofar as we can; otherwise we will not be forgiven. We are also bound to repair what damage we have unjustly caused others.

The Church stands absolutely for the right of private property, which is dictated by the law of nature. Yet she always reminds us that we are merely the stewards of wealth that belongs to God, and that salvation will not be given to any who have superfluities and refuse to aid the poor. 

She also insists that the starving man has the right to help himself suf ficiently from the goods of others to keep away starvation here and now pending, unless he would reduce the others to starvation.

The rules of restitution are necessarily complicated, and if we have done an injustice to others we should take our case up with a Father Confessor, to see what return we must make. The rules he will apply represent the thought of centuries. If we find that we are in possession of property that rightfully belongs to another, but which came by legitimate means into our own hands, the necessity of getting the proper advice about restitution binds here just as much as if the property had been deliberately stolen by us. 

We are not allowed to use what we are sure belongs to another, unless he is willing or should be willing.

Converts coming into the Church should remember that, so far as possible, they are bound to repair injustices they have committed in the past. Even though they may not be compelled to confess their sins, as in the case when they are unconditionally baptized, they are bound to repair injustice of which they have been guilty, and, if they cannot figure out themselves just to what extent they are indebted, they should seek advice from a priest.

It is not necessary to become a highwayman in order to sin against justice. If we deliberately run up charge accounts that we know we cannot pay, if we charge outrageous prices for goods we sell, if we use false weights or measures, if we destroy a competitor's reputation to injure his business, if we take unfair advantage in contracts, if we pay less than living wages to our employes, if we give people counsel or instruction that leads them to commit injustices against their fellows, if we do not return what we borrow, if we receive stolen goods, and so on, we are just as guilty of sin as if we reached our hand into somebody else's purse and took what belonged to him. It must be remembered that while human courts are prone to regard great offenders with more leniency than small ones, God does not judge in this way. He is no respecter of persons. 

If we have inherited unjust wealth, we usually cannot retain it, unless prescription gives us a right to it. By prescription, we mean a title obtained by long possession. In this case, definite rules establish the morality of our possession, and hence again the question must be passed upon by an expert.

PAGE  

If a man has unknowingly and unwillingly come into possession of another's property, and then finds that it belongs to that other, the general rule is that he must restore all that is still left and as much more as his wealth has been increased by its possession. One can deduct those expenses which even the owner would not have been able to avoid. 

Restitution must be made in all kinds of injustice to the' owner or his heirs. But, if this is not possible, it may be made to the poor or to some religious work. We are not permitted to continue in the possession of ill-gotten gains ourselves. In this, as in other features of justice, the advice of theologians should be sought, as there may be occasional exceptions. 

One of the commonest and most abhorrent methods of injustice is by usury, i.e., the taking of an excessive rate of interest. 

Sometimes Christians in business today are hard pressed by the dishonest methods of their competitors and wonder whether they are not justified in practicing some shady things themselves. Let them remember that God's laws demand that we give to every man his due, and also that a sin against injustice is not forgiven unless we have the firm intention of making restitution insofar as we are able. The sins of others never give us the right to sin. The chief purpose why each of us is on earth is to look after his own soul.

When a man has become involved in a business failure, the conditions may be such that he is not bound to restitution. This is particularly true when creditors have known that they were taking a risk and when the man has tried to act honestly. Such cases should be taken to a priest who is known to be expert in moral theology.

In cases of bankruptcy, if a bankrupt has honestly complied with the formalities required by law and obtains a legal discharge, he can feel as free in conscience as he is before the law. If afterwards he can pay the debts, he ought to do so, but he need not if this would work grave injury on himself or his family. Modern business, it should be remembered, is conducted on the principle that risks should be expected; hence prices of service are made high enough to cover these risks.

PAGE 

XLIX CATHOLIC DOCTRINE IN REGARD TO LIES; MENTAL RESERVATION 

The Eighth Commandment is: "Thou shalt not bear false witness against thy neighbor" (Exodus xx, 16). "We are commanded by the Eighth Commandment," says the Baltimore Catechism, "to speak the truth in all things, and to be careful of the honor and reputation of every one. ... The Eighth Commandment forbids all rash judgments, back-biting, slanders and lies. . . . Those who have lied about their neighbor and seriously injured his character must repair the damage done as far as they are able; otherwise they will not be forgiven."

A lie is an expression, made by either sign or word, conveying to another something contrary to what we think, for the purpose of deceiving him.

A lie in itself is intrinsically evil and is always sinful. But to lie is not always a mortal sin. Lies are divided into jocose, officious, and malicious. A jocose lie is one told in fun. An officious lie is one whose purpose is to benefit ourselves or others. A malicious lie is one that harms our neighbor.

Jocose and officious lies are venially sinful, unless they give serious scandal to others. A malicious lie is a venial sin if the damage it does to others is small; but is mortal if the damage is serious. Like all mortal sins, however, in addition to grave matter a lie requires sufficient reflection and full consent of the will or it is venial. He who does serious damage to another by lying cannot receive absolution or have the sin forgiven unless he has the purpose of correcting that damage, so far as possible. 

There are several hateful forms of lying into which people sometimes fall without realizing the seriousness of their condition. One is hypocrisy, which is practiced by those who pretend to a virtue they do not have. It is a mortal sin when it is used as a mask in order to spread false doctrines or to gratify evil passions. Another form of lying is flattery, which is obsequious or insincere fawning on another and which becomes mortally sinful if it leads to serious damage to that person or others. Another form is boasting, which is self-glorification. It is usually venial, but if it leads others into serious sin or injustice it is mortal. Another form is dissimulation, which consists in hiding one's real feelings. This is not sinful unless the purpose is to harm somebody else. By no stretch of the imagination can it be justly argued that men always have to reveal their secret opinions to others. If they did, the world would have ten times as much quarreling and hatred as it now has. If we disagree with a man, it is by no means necessary to let him know this. It is a weakness, not an evidence of strength, to be too open-minded. Frankness need not be cultivated to the extent where it makes us obnoxious to everybody around us, and one of the falsest philosophies of our age is that which pretends we are all liars because we have sense enough to practice ordinary prudence. 

This leads us to the debated mental reservation doctrine of Catholic theologians. If a butterfly type of girl were to ask an army general a question involving military secrets, would he be a liar if he said to her:

PAGE  

"I don't know anything about that?" Some moralists outside the Catholic 

Church declare that he would; but our theology is based on calm reason and God's revelation, not on false idealism that is flaunted to the public but never practiced even by those who preach it. For this reason, we say that the general in the case mentioned is altogether within his rights when he gives the answer stated. He must protect professional secrets. The girl has no right to know what she asks. If she will use her head, she will readily understand what the general means by his answer; if she is deceived, she fools herself. He is not a liar. 

By mental reservation we mean the holding back of part of what is in one's mind. It is purely mental if the meaning of the speaker cannot be ascertained by the hearer; for instance, if somebody asked me whether 

I had ever been in Greenland and I told him that I had, meaning that I had seen a number of moving pictures of Greenland and had been there in mind. It is partly mental if the real intention of the speaker can be gained by the context of his conversation or from circumstances that any prudent person would notice.

Purely mental reservation is always forbidden. The partly mental reservation is permissible when there is a good reason for using it. We do not have the intention of deceiving our neighbor; in fact, what deception there is exists on his own part. He has no right to know what he asks and often damage will result to him or others if he does know. If he has any right to know, however, we have the obligation of answering truthfully. 

This is usually the case when children are questioned by their parents, penitents by their Fathers Confessor, and so on.

The same rule that applies to mental reservation is used to solve the morality of equivocation. By equivocation, we mean the use of language that can be interpreted in two ways. If the interpretation can be made in the sense of the truth by a prudent person or because of the circumstances of the case, we can use equivocation under the same conditions as mental reservation. If it cannot, we must consider equivocation a lie. 

The grim necessity of the mental reservation doctrine is constantly proved. Consider the case, for instance, of a man who had undergone a mental collapse and had been in an asylum for two years, but had recovered and had gone to another city. If it were known that he had suffered this collapse it would be embarrassing both in a social and a business way. 

Certainly he is not bound to advertise the fact right and left; and, if he is directly questioned, he is morally justified in evading the questioner. But the case need not be this serious. Everybody is entitled to privacy. Suppose a caller comes at an unreasonable hour or is known to be a bore, with no sense of the value of your time. Suppose you have the servant announce that you are "not at home." How can this be considered a lie, when every thinking person knows well what the term "not at home" means?

Anglican writers are at times critical of the mental reservation doctrine of Catholic theologians. Even some English Catholics have refused to approve it. But it is a notorious fact that this boast of scrupulous honesty in speech is self-delusion. It is like the prohibition law used to be along the 

Atlantic seaboard a statute but not a fact. Christ Himself, when questioned by officious relatives whether He was going to Jerusalem, told them that He was not and then went. Go thou and do likewise, if it be necessary.

PAGE 

The Sin of Gossip 

One of the commonest sins against the Eighth Commandment is the revealing of evil committed by our neighbors. "If any man offend not in word," teaches St. James (iii, 2), "the same is a perfect man." It is obvious that we are not allowed to lie about others; but neither are we allowed, ordinarily, to reveal the truth about them if the truth will damage their character. Certain exceptions, of course, have to be made. 

Even the dead retain the right to their good name; and not only individuals but corporate bodies have the right to a good reputation. 

If we engage in evil talk against others, the measure of the gravity of the sin will be the amount of damage we do. If we make known the sin of another and do him serious injury by the revelation, we commit mortal sin. 

At times, even the revealing of a venial sin or of a defect that he could not help, such as illegitimate birth, may do serious damage and thus become a mortal sin. If no serious harm follows, nevertheless, even the making known of a mortal sin may not itself be mortal; it would not be a mortal sin, indeed it would often not be sinful at all, to speak about a new fall by an already notorious sinner. Yet a man might lose his reputation along one line and be jealous of it in other ways, and we must respect his rights. 

If evil done by another has become publicly known, we do not sin in telling it even to those who have not yet heard about it. Hence if a matter has become public through court proceedings, through publication in a newspaper or magazine, through the arrest of the person by the police, we are not bound to secrecy. The obligation is lifted even in distant places or after long periods. If a man has been convicted and has gone to a distant place to try to live this down, he who reveals it might sin against charity but he would not be guilty of injustice and hence could not be held to material restitution for the damage done.

Even though a sin has not been publicized through the courts or newspapers, it can be spoken of without sin if it is commonly known. When it is commonly known in one place but not in another, no sin is committed by speaking of it in the other provided the news is likely to reach there soon. 

Theologians dispute whether sin would be committed in revealing the sin in a place where otherwise news of it might not reach. Practical advice is that it is more charitable to keep silent about it; but we cannot make serious sin out of dubious matter. If only a few know of a sin, we cannot make it well known without sinning ourselves. In cases where the information is certainly going to be spread, we commit only a venial sin or no sin if we talk about it.

There are conditions that make it lawful and sometimes even obligatory to reveal the secret sins of others. If we know that a person is about to employ a dishonest man, we have a right to make known this fact to the prospec- tive employer. Whenever the defense of ourselves, of our hearers, of others, of the public, or even of the delinquent himself demands the revelation of his secret sin, we can make it known. Likewise, when a person has done what we deem a wrong to us, we have the right to discuss it with a prudent person or even several prudent persons to get advice or even consolation. 

If we do damage to another by revealing his secret sin, except in cases wherein we are justified in making the revelation, we are bound to make restitution.

PAGE  

If, by listening to a detractor or a liar, we encourage him to slander another, we share in his sin. If our listening is not the efficacious cause of his detraction, we do not sin against justice but we do offend against charity. 

Nevertheless, more harm than good sometimes comes from an attempt to silence such persons and we are not ordinarily bound to create scenes with them. Private individuals are seldom bound by grave obligation if they are merely listeners. Those in authority at times may have a grave obligation to put a stop to such conversation. "Good name in man and woman, dear my lord, Is the immediate jewel of their souls: 

Who steals my purse steals trash; 'tis something, nothing. 'Twas mine, 'tis his, and has been a slave to thousands 

But he that filches from me my good name 

Robs me of that which not enriches him, And makes me poor indeed" (Shapespeare, Othello, act iii).

If secrets have been committed to us, or we have come into possession of information that we cannot reveal without causing somebody injury or displeasure, we are bound to keep the secrets, unless their revelation should be demanded by the public good, the welfare of ourselves or of another, or even of the person who might object to the revelation of the secret. In case of professional secrets, however, namely those such as would be given to clergymen,* teachers, lawyers, doctors, etc., by individuals who seek their professional aid, the cause must be extremely severe to allow us to reveal the secrets. We could do it to prevent the serious punishment of an innocent person for crime, probably to save our own lives, to prevent extreme damage to the nation, etc.; but we would have to be prepared to suffer a considerable loss either to ourselves or to others before we would be allowed to reveal professional secrets.

Because of the moral doctrine about secrets, we are not allowed to open one another's mail, to eavesdrop, or to engage in other similar practices, unless we feel that the person does not care or there is little likelihood of our getting in on secrets of importance, or it is necessary to protect ourselves, others, or the public from serious harm. Censors in time of war, superiors in boarding schools, keepers of prisons, etc., have the right to open mail to prevent harm to individuals or to the public. This writer cannot agree with the opinion that holds the public authorities never have the right to use telephonic eavesdropping apparatus to trap criminals. If they have serious reason for suspecting the criminals, the rights of the public or of other individuals must prevail over the rights of the suspects. 

The Ninth and Tenth Commandments 

The short form of the Ninth Commandment is: "Thou shalt not covet thy neighbor's wife." The long form follows: "Thou shalt not covet thy neighbor's house: neither shalt thou desire his wife, nor his servant, nor his handmaid, nor his ox, nor his ass, nor anything that is his" (Exodus xx, 17). The Tenth Commandment, "Thou shalt not covet thy neighbor's goods," is included in this long form. The Ninth Commandment has already been discussed in the section about the Sixth Commandment. *We here speak of secrets given outside the confessional; sins told in Confession can never be revealed, under any circumstances.

PAGE 

The Tenth has been pretty well taken care of in the section about the Seventh Commandment, for the Tenth Commandment forbids the desire that leads to theft.

The Tenth Commandment is directed chiefly against avarice, the inordinate love of wealth. St. Paul had it in mind when he referred to money as the root of all evils (I Tim. vi, 10). In itself it is a venial sin unless it leads the victim to other grave sins, such as unjust action against others or to serious sin against charity. It is always dangerous. It makes a man hard-hearted, causes him to be niggardly, to be too eager in piling up wealth, and to be ready to do wrong in order to get it. If it lures him to neglect of his own spiritual welfare, it is a mortal sin. The warning of 

St. Paul cannot be taken too seriously.

PAGE 

CHIEF PRECEPTS OF CATHOLIC CHURCH; LAWS OF FAST AND ABSTINENCE 

Following are the six chief precepts of the Church: 1. To hear Mass on Sundays and holy days of obligation; 2. To fast and abstain on days appointed; 3. To go to Confession at least once a year; 4. To receive the 

Holy Eucharist within the Easter time; 5. To contribute to the support of the Church; 6. To observe the marriage legislation of the Church. 

Christ told his Church: "He that heareth you heareth Me; and he that despiseth you despiseth Me" (Luke x, 16). It is therefore evident that 

He gave the Church the power of making legislation for the guidance of the faithful. We would expect this from the Old Law. The true religion has always been marked by the exercise of legislative and executive authority and one of the most unreasonable heresies of our day is the attempt to make the Church appear tyrannous because she insists that her laws be kept. 

Anarchy is no more workable in religion than in the State. 

The Church not only has the right to make legislation, but she also has the power to change it. to change it. Her doctrine never changes, but her discipline does, being adapted to different times and peoples. The Church, however, does not have the right to change the natural law or the positive Divine law; for instance, she could not make birth control all right or grant a divorce with right to remarry in the case of Christians in a consummated wedding.

The first commandment of the Church is to hear Mass on Sundays and holy days of obligation. Unless we obey this, we commit a mortal sin, provided that we lack a serious reason for the omission. A sufficient reason would be sickness, the necessity of nursing some ill person, the impossibility of leaving little children, fear of the effects of stormy weather on our own health, danger of heavy loss when work is halted at harvest, long distance from Church (we ought to walk three miles, or motor about twenty), and so forth. People should be careful, however, about dispensing themselves. 

The habit of going to church on Sunday is easy to abandon, and, when it is lost, this absence almost invariably leads to neglect of the sacraments, loss of the faith, and the damnation of one's soul. We should remember the warning of Scripture: "And if the just man shall scarcely be saved, where shall the ungodly and the sinner appear?" (I Peter iv, 18). 

Those who have other Catholics under their charge and keep them from hearing Mass on Sundays and holy days of obligation also commit a mortal sin, if there is not sufficient reason for this act. 

The holy days of obligation should be kept the same as Sunday. In the United States they are: Feast of the Circumcision (New Year's),

Ascension Thursday, the Assumption of Our Lady, the Feast of All Saints, the Immaculate Conception of Mary, and Christmas.

A person physically present at Mass and avoiding any external action incompatible with internal attention-such as the carrying on of a prolonged conversation or the doing of profane reading-fulfills the obligation. The 

Church law would seem to be fulfilled by external decorum, even though our minds wander. This theologically probable opinion is not intended to foster the careless hearing of Mass, for we should try to have internal and

PAGE external attention, but it does serve to relieve scrupulous minds of needless anxieties (Slater, Manual of Moral Theology, vol 1, p. 263).

The obligation to attend Sunday Mass flows from a precept, and, like any other precept, obliges us to use the means that lead to its fulfillment and to guard against placing any impediment that would hinder the execution. unless there is sufficient reason to act otherwise. 

An impediment may be direct or indirect. It is direct if placed for the purpose of missing Mass. For example, there is no justification for a person's deliberately driving into the country five minutes before the last Mass is to begin.

An indirect impediment is placed when, for example, a person goes on a trip for some reason other than to avoid Mass. His going is a remote impediment if he leaves Saturday or before; it becomes proximate if he departs after that time. The more proximate to the time of fulfilling the obligation the impediment is placed, the more proportionate must be the cause of its being placed. To leave town on Sunday morning, therefore, demands a more weighty reason for justification than does a departure on Saturday evening.

No measuring device can be established as the absolute standard in this matter and circumstances must be taken into consideration in each case. 

We cannot conclude that departure at 11:55 p. m. constitutes no sin or at most a venial one and that a five-minute delay will immediately place the act in the category of grievous sin. Such a conclusion does not employ common sense; hence, is not good theology.

A business trip is more important than one undertaken merely for pleasure and weighs more as an excusing cause in this discussion. Even here we are not setting up an absolute principle, for a weekend pleasure trip can be important business to a tired mother who has spent 51/2 weeks in the kitchen.

Usually, if there is a good reason, such as the need for recreation or business necessity, it would not be wrong to leave Saturday or before and to miss Sunday Mass. If there were grave necessity, it would not be wrong to leave even on Sunday.

The second precept of the Church is to fast and to abstain on days appointed.

A fast day is one in which we are allowed but one full meal. A person fasting would be permitted, for instance, to take a cup of coffee or other liquid and a cracker or small piece of bread in the morning, a full meal at noon, and a collation or the fourth part of a meal (not to exceed eight ounces) in the evening. The full meal or other permissible eating on the fast day can be transferred at will. Liquids that are not generally considered food can be drunk at any time in the day (milk would be forbidden, but coffee or tea would be allowed).

The chief fasting times of the year are: Lent (Sundays excepted); the Ember days, or Wednesday, Friday and Saturday of weeks at the beginning of the four seasons; and the vigils or eves of certain great feasts, i. e., Christmas, Pentecost, Assumption, and All Saints.

Days of abstinence are days when we are allowed the usual amount of food, but must keep from flesh meat. The law of abstinence forbids the

PAGE 

eating of flesh meat and of broth made of meat, but does not exclude the use of eggs and milk or the products of milk, and it permits the seasoning of foods even when this is done with the fat of animals. 

Abstinence is enjoined on Fridays throughout the year (except in part of the former Spanish territory of the United States, where an ancient dispensation still prevails, having been granted in gratitude to the Spanish for their work in halting the Moors). Fast and abstinence both are prescribed for Ash Wednesday (the opening day of Lent), the Fridays and 

Wednesdays of Lent, Ember days, the vigil of Pentecost, of Assumption, of All Saints, and of Christmas (with fast and abstinence up to noon on Holy Saturday). Fasting alone is commanded for the other days of Lent (Wednesday replaces Saturday as a day of abstinence in the United States during Lent, by reason of an apostolic indult, renewable each five years. Wednesday of 

Holy Week is not a day of abstinence, but is a fast day). 

If a vigil with fasting and abstinence falls on Sunday, it is not to be observed. Abstinence and fasting do not bind on any holy day of obligation, unless the day occurs in Lent (which does not happen in the United States).

The purpose of the laws of fast and abstinence is to teach us to mortify our passions and to satisfy for our sins. The Friday abstinence is established to remind us that Christ died on Friday.

By Papal permission, renewed from time to time, United States working men and their families are not bound to abstain from flesh meat except on 

Fridays throughout the year, Ash Wednesday, the vigil of Christmas, and

Holy Saturday up until noon. This ruling is often interpreted as applying both to physical and to mental workers. The concession is given because of the strenuous demands made upon Americans by our intense industrial and business life. 

The following are always excused from fasting: Those not yet aged 21 or those aged 59 or more; those who are sick, convalescent, or in delicate health; all who must work hard either on account of the nature of the labor they perform or because of the number of hours they spend in that labor, which is generally interpreted as eight or more hours a day; all women about to become mothers, or who are nursing a child, and finally the very poor.

Any person who, on account of some reason not sufficient to excuse him from the law, finds the observance of fasting rather difficult, should submit his case to a priest, either in the confessional or outside it, and the priest can either dispense him, if he has the faculties, or can get the faculties from his superiors, provided there is sufficient reason. 

The law of abstinence binds all who have completed their seventh year. Pastors can dispense their parishioners in individual cases from fast and abstinence, or both, as well as from the law forbidding servile work on Sundays or holy days of obligation. Any good reason suffices. This power of dispensation applies to a pastor's subjects even outside his territory as well as to transients in his territory. Ordinaries, of course, have the same power. Ordinaries may also, for reason of a great concourse of people, or for the sake of public health, dispense the entire diocese or any place in it from the laws of fasting or abstinence or both. Often, through a

PAGE special faculty, Bishops dispense their dioceses on the occasion of holidays that fall on days of fast and abstinence. In clerical exempt communities, the superiors have the same right for the communities as are granted by canon law to pastors and ordinaries in regard to dispensation from fasting, abstinence, or servile work. 

Fast or abstinence days are computed from midnight to midnight. 

We may follow any ordinary means of computing time we wish (for instance, standard time, daylight-saving time, sun time, etc.). The same rule holds about the Communion fast. 

In times of war or whenever food is not easy to get, general dispensations from fast and abstinence are often issued. People living on charity who find it difficult to get nourishing food are never bound by fast or abstinence.

If our work is so heavy that we feel we need flesh meat, if it is hard to get meat substitutes when we are traveling, or even if we are invited out to dinner and the hostess serves meat, we can eat it on days of abstinence. 

But we are obliged to avoid giving scandal and are reminded that sometimes non-Catholics like to trap us. The writer knows of a group of 

Catholic women who were invited to a Protestant house for a Friday social. 

The hostess deliberately served meat sandwiches. None of the women ate them. The hostess and her entire family were converted as a result. 

In the case of any ecclesiastical law, however, like fast, abstinence, or attendance at Sunday Mass, we should remember that we are not bound if put to grave inconvenience. Ordinary inconvenience is not enough to exempt us.

PAGE 

LI 

PRECEPTS OF CHURCH ABOUT GOING TO CONFESSION AND COMMUNION 

The third precept of the Church is to confess our sins at least once a year. It obliges under pain of mortal sin.

Frequent Confession is encouraged, but we must confess at least annually, unless we are positive that we are not in the state of mortal sin. 

Confession is a sacrament and the sacraments bring us spiritual strength that we could not ordinarily obtain by private devotion; hence the advisability of frequent Confession, which is promoted by the Church through her insistence that those going to Communion daily or almost every day must confess at least once every two weeks in order to receive the indulgences that require Confession and Communion. The Church law also tells her religious to confess weekly, which is an indication of her love of devotional Confessions.

Frequent Confession is essential to a good life, in the majority of cases nowadays.

Children should go to Confession when they are old enough to know what sin is, which is usually about the age of seven years. If they have the use of reason earlier, the duty of Confession and Communion binds. 

The fourth precept of the Church is to receive Communion within the Easter season, which in the United States extends from the first Sunday of Lent to Trinity Sunday, a stretch of about a fourth of a year. An unworthy 

Communion does not fulfill the obligation. 

Communion must be received at least once a year under pain of mortal sin, but frequent reception is usually necessary for a good life. God sometimes gives extraordinary grace to those living in good conscience outside the Church, and keeps them from mortal sin without Communion, but He expects those who know the means He has established for virtuous living to use them, and experience proves that frequent Communion is the best incentive to a truly Christian life. The Church encourages even daily 

Communion. If we cannot go daily, let us go weekly; if we cannot do this, at least let us go monthly. The frequency of reception of the sacraments is largely a matter of habit. 

The fifth commandment of the Church is to contribute to the support of our pastor. To break this involves mortal sin if we can give and do not, and thereby cause grave inconvenience to the Church. 

The sixth commandment of the Church is to obey her marriage legislation. What we must chiefly remember in connection with this is that we are not allowed to marry within the third degree of blood relationship (without a dispensation) and that we must marry in the presence of a duly authorized priest and two witnesses (even though one of the contracting persons is a non-Catholic). The authorized priest is our own pastor or 

Bishop or one delegated to act. If a couple are living far removed from a priest and none can be reached within a month, the marriage can be performed with the presence of two witnesses only, but to prevent legal difficulties should be performed in the presence of a magistrate (never, however, in the presence of a clergyman of another denomination, when he acts as a clergyman, for this would be participation in an act of false worship).

PAGE We are forbidden to solemnize marriages in Lent and Advent, that is, to have them performed with pomp or a Nuptial Mass, unless this is especially permitted by the Bishop. Marriage can be entered into, however, at any time.

The Catholic marriage ought to take place at Nuptial Mass, which is a Mass appointed by the Church to invoke a special blessing on the couple. 

On some days of the year, the Nuptial Mass cannot be celebrated, but its special prayers and blessing can be incorporated into the Mass of the day. 

From the beginning of Advent until Christmas day, and from Ash 

Wednesday to Easter Sunday, inclusive, marriage cannot be "solemnized,”* i.e., the Nuptial blessing cannot be given without special episcopal permission. Marriage may, however, be contracted during these forbidden seasons and the Nuptial blessing supplied later. The Nuptial Mass and blessing may be supplied after any lapse of time, no matter for what cause these rites were missed. The Nuptial blessing is given only once in her life to a woman. It is never given at mixed marriages, but may be supplied after the conversion of the non-Catholic. *This term has a special meaning in the Church. It does not mean merely to contract marriage.

PAGE  

LII 

WHAT AFTER DEATH? SOME OF CHURCH'S BELIEFS DESCRIBED 

Many curious beliefs are held by the different denominations about life after death. The Catholic belief may be summed us as follows: Immediately after death, the soul undergoes a private judgment, in which its condition is made known to it, and it then goes to heaven, if it is free of sin and of all the temporal penalty due for sin; to purgatory, if it has died free of mortal sin but guilty of venial sin or with some temporal penalty for sin still to be worked out; to everlasting hell, if it has died in mortal sin. Only the soul goes to these places right after death, but at the end of the world there will be a general judgment of all mankind, following a general resurrection, in which the bodies will be joined to the souls, to remain united forever, in heaven or hell. Purgatory will not exist after the general judgment.

Death 

Every man must die. If Adam had not sinned, the good would be taken to heaven at the end of their probation without tasting death. While death is natural to us, we would have been preternaturally preserved from it. 

The fact that we must undergo it is a punishment for sin. 

With death, our probation ends. There is no chance of correcting mistakes after it. If we are in the state of grace at the instant of death, we are saved. If we are not, we are damned. The night when no man can work, about which Christ warned, has come (John ix, 4). The good are then confirmed in good for all eternity; the bad are confirmed in evil. 

The Particular Judgment 

Belief in the private judgment immediately following death rests largely on tradition.
