Yet Ecclus. xi, 28, asserts: "It is easy before God in the day of death to reward every one according to his ways." Other texts are also quoted in favor of the particular judgment, but the classical example is Christ's parable of the beggar, Lazarus, and the rich man, the former of whom He described as being in "Abraham's bosom" and the latter in hell. 

It is not necessary to stretch the story of Christ out of the parable class to realize that He was speaking of definite individuals or at least of typical ones. 

The fact that He represented them as already undergoing reward and punishment clearly manifests that we are to expect the same after death. The parable disposes of the belief held by some modern Protestants that the soul is completely unconscious from the time of death until the general judgment.

The common opinion of Catholic theologians is that souls are judged at the moment and in the place of death. Through some interior illumination, God manifests its condition and future lot to the soul, which, according to St. Thomas Aquinas, then finds its place in heaven, purgatory, or hell, just as bodies find their place according to gravity. Popular illustrations of this judgment are often given, showing the soul before God's tribunal, with the devils accusing it and the guardian angels coming to its defense. 

Such descriptions are not intended to be taken with utter literalness, but they are not to be scoffed at.

PAGE Purgatory

A soul has often passed into eternity with only venial sin on it. 

Perhaps a man has wished harm to his neighbor, but not serious injury, and he has not repented. According to the Apocalypse (Revelation), nothing impure can enter heaven. Sacred Scripture declares that into heaven "there shall not enter anything defiled, or that worketh abomination or maketh a lie" (Apoc. xxi, 27); nothing defiled can enter into it (Wisd. vii, 25) and the polluted shall not pass through it (Is. xxxv, 8). The Scriptures elsewhere warn that God will render to every man according to his works. Yet is a man in venial sin to be damned? God would not be just if He damned a soul eternally for a light sin. Reason demands the existence of a place where trivial sins can be wiped out. That place is purgatory. 

Purgatory is also necessary for those souls who have been guilty of serious sin and have not yet worked out the full temporal punishment that remains after the eternal guilt has been forgiven. No soul can enter heaven until it is spotless.

The necessity of helping the dead by our prayers was recognized in the ancient world and particularly by the Jews, who to this day, in their orthodox synagogues, regularly offer prayers for the dead. But the souls in heaven do not need our help and those in hell cannot benefit by it. 

Hence an implicit recognition of the existence of purgatory is traceable back to the dawn of history. The Egyptians prayed constantly for their dead and practically all the ancient pagan systems had this doctrine, which they had undoubtedly received from primitive revelation; they corrupted it to suit their various forms of paganism. The Buddhists of the Orient, even today, pray for the eternal repose of their dead (using these very words).

The doctrine of the necessity of prayers for the dead was widely taught in Christ's day and was not denounced by Him. Some passages in the New 

Testament are not explainable without this doctrine. That the first Christians believed it is shown by many inscriptions in the catacombs, made by the earliest converts, asking prayers for the dead.

The chief Scriptural authority is taken from the Second Book of the Machabees, one of the books not admitted to the Protestant canon but for whose discarding convincing reason cannot be adduced. No scholar, however, dares to deny it as history, and this admission is enough to prove on its evidence that the ancient Jews recognized the necessity of prayers for the dead.

In the twelfth chapter of the book we are told about a large group of Jewish soldiers who, in defiance of the Divine Law, took, as booty of war, votive offerings that had been made to idols. According to the practices of the times, the precious metal was rightful war booty, but according to the Jewish law the fact that this metal had been molded into idols made it unlawful for the soldiers to take it. God, as a punishment, permitted the soldiers to be slain. Their act, however, had been only a venial sin and they were not damned. Judas, their leader, therefore, made a collection and sent 12,000 drachms of silver to Jerusalem "for sacrifice to be offered for the sins of the dead." The chapter ends with: "It is therefore a holy and wholesome thought to pray for the dead, that they may be loosed from sins."

We can help the dead in purgatory by our prayers, fasts, and alms

PAGE  

deeds, by offering indulgences for them, and particularly by having the 

Mass celebrated for them.

We know little about purgatory. Whether there is fire in it, we are not sure. The weight of theological opinion in the Latin Church is that there is, and whether there is or not, we are certain that there is serious suffering.

We can have our purgatory by suffering on this earth. 

Our funeral service is not merely an empty rite for the consolation of the family. It is chiefly a supplication to God for the repose of the soul of the deceased person, and, if a child too young to have sinned is buried with a Mass, it is a Mass of the Angels, celebrated in white, and an act of worship that is a rejoicing that another pure soul has entered heaven. 

St. Ephraem the Syrian, who died in 373, bears testimony to the ancient doctrine of prayers for the dead. In his Testament, the great Doctor of the Church declares in Poetry: 

"Come, my brothers, and lay out my remains; 

The decree has gone forth that I can tarry no longer. 

Give me, as provision for my journey, Your prayers, your psalms, and your sacrifices.

When the number of thirty days is complete 

Then, O my brothers, make remembrance of me, For the dead truly derive succor

From the sacrifices offered up by the living." 

The ancientness of the custom of a "month's mind" Mass for a deceased person is shown by this quotation.

Enemies of the Church try to make out that purgatory is an invention to promote clerical business income, because offerings are received for Masses for the dead. The answer is: 1. Mass stipends are so small as to be insignificant when compared with any other form of American professional stipend (usually $1 for a Low Mass, $5 for a High one, part of the latter going to the musicians); 2. Persons asking an individual service of this kind ought to give something for it; 3. The poor are not neglected, for every Mass offered has prayers for the faithful departed; 4. The rich are not given an extra advantage, for nobody knows just what benefit God gives to a soul from a Mass and we can be sure that He will not neglect the poor. It is of faith, however, that we can aid the dead by our prayers, particularly by the Holy Sacrifice of the Mass. 

We cannot "buy" or "pay for" a Mass. A Mass stipend is merely an alms. If these stipends did not exist, some other form of clerical income would have to be devised to replace them. Masses are requested by the faithful for all sorts of intentions. Those who have them offered often see quick results.

The Resurrection of the Body 

Our bodies will be reunited to our souls on the Last Day and will spend eternity with them, in heaven or in hell. This reunion will be effected by a miracle of God. No unsolvable difficulties are offered in a scientific way by this belief. If any considerable part of the matter that has been in our body at any time during our earthly life is brought back to it, the body will certainly be ours; and, remembering that the soul is the form of the body and any matter that passes into that form actually becomes

PAGE 

our body, it is not strictly necessary to expect that any of the actual matter that now forms us will have to come back to us. 

The bodies of the saved in eternity will be perfect and of wondrous beauty. The bodies of the damned will be hideous. 

Concerning the body of the saved soul, St. Paul wrote: "It is sown in corruption, it shall rise in incorruption. It is sown in dishonor, it shall rise in glory. It is sown in weakness, it shall rise in power. It is sown a natural body, it shall rise a spiritual body" (I Cor. xv, 42-44). "The trumpet shall sound, and the dead shall rise again incorruptible; and we shall be changed. For this corruptible must put on incorruption, and this mortal must put on immortality. And when this mortal hath put on immortality, then shall come to pass the saying that is written: Death is swallowed up in victory. O death, where is thy victory? O death, where is thy sting?” (I Cor. xv, 52-55). The joining of the soul and the body will precede the General Judgment.

The General Judgment 

At the end of the world all mankind, at least all who have had the use of reason, will be gathered together for judgment. "For the Son of man. shall come in the glory of His Father with His angels: and then will He render to every man according to his works" (Matt. xvi, 27).

At the general judgment, Christ will come in the clouds of heaven, and gather all nations together before His throne, placing the good on His right hand, and the wicked on His left (Matt. xxiv and xxv). Then He will make clear the good and the evil that every man has done, even our most secret thoughts, and also the graces He has given to each one; finally, He will pronounce judgment upon all (II Cor. v, 10). "And I saw the dead, great and small, standing in the presence of the throne, and the books were opened; and the dead were judged by those things which were written in the books, according to their works" (Apoc. xx, 12). "For there is nothing covered, that shall not be revealed; nor hidden, that shall not be known" (Luke xii, 2; Mark iv, 22). "The Lord will bring to light the hidden things of darkness, and will make manifest the counsels of the hearts" (I Cor. iv, 5).

The man Christ will be the judge, but He will act with an authority and power that are not human but Divine. The Apostles will assist Him, though in just what way we do not know. The saints will also act with 

Him (I Cor. vi, 2), but probably all that is meant by the Scripture is that they will approve of His sentence. St. Thomas conjectures that the 

Apostles and "perfect" men will notify others of their sentence.

It is theologically certain that all men will be judged and it is commonly held that even unbaptized infants will be there. The more approved opinion of theologians is that angels will also then be tried. The opening of the books probably means that God will enlighten all concerning their own deeds and also those of all others. The books, it would seem, are to be our own consciences and our intellects vivified by God. Nearly all theologians hold that even the sins of the just will be made manifest, although not in such a way as to bring them shame. With the unjust, the shame will be overwhelming.

The popular belief among Christians, Jews, and Moslems that the judgment will occur in the Valley of Jehoshaphat (Josaphat), which has

PAGE 

been identified with the narrow ravine of the Kidron on the eastern side of Jerusalem, is based on a somewhat obscure text in Joel iii, 2. No Christian commentary thus set the geographical place before the ninth century. 

We do not know where the judgment will take place. St. Thomas Aquinas thought that, as Christ ascended to heaven from Mount Olivet, so He will descend upon it to judge the world. 

Some Catholic authorities have leaned to a literal, some to a metaphorical, interpretation of the Biblical description of events that will occur at the last judgment, such as Christ's coming in clouds, the appearance of His sign (undoubtedly the Cross) in the sky, the sound of the trumpet wakening the dead, the flame of fire that will accompany the Judge, etc.

Judgment will be pronounced on each person, but St. Thomas thinks no oral words will be used in the sentence. Many authorities believe that the general judgment will be pronounced orally. 

At the General Judgment the Providence of God, which on earth often permits the good to suffer because of the cleansing necessary for their sins, and often permits the wicked to prosper because they get on earth the reward for all the good they do, will appear just before all. 

The End of This World 

The Last Day will see a great catastrophe happen to this world, when, in its present stage, it will be destroyed by fire. But a new earth, as we know from the Scriptures, will arise, and it is probable that we can visit it if we wish, provided we go to heaven. "For behold I create new heavens and a new earth: and the former things shall not be in remembrance, and they shall not come upon the heart" (Isaias lxv, 17); ". the new heavens and the new earth. . I will make to stand before Me, saith the Lord" (Isaias lxvi, 22); "And I saw a new heaven and a new earth. For the first heaven and the first earth was gone, and the sea is now no more" (Apoc. xxi, 1). Hell

The Catholic Church believes firmly in an everlasting hell, with punishment greater than any we know on this earth. The reason for this belief is that Christ taught it and He proved Himself to be Divine. Hard as the doctrine is, it is well to remember that only those who die as deliberate rebels against God go into eternal torment.

Here are some of the Scriptural references to hell: "Who shall suffer eternal punishment in destruction, from the face of the Lord, and from the glory of His power" (II Thess. i, 9). "They that work iniquity shall be cast into the furnace of fire: there shall be weeping and gnashing of teeth" (Matt. xiii, 42). "The chaff He will burn with unquenchable fire" (Matt. iii, 12). "The hell of unquenchable fire" (Mark ix, 44). "Depart from Me, you cursed, into everlasting fire, which was prepared for the devil and his angels. . . . And these shall go into everlasting punishment: but the just, into life everlasting" (Matt. xxv, 41 and 46). The statement is by Jesus Christ, who is describing what will happen on the Day of Judgment.

Some sectarians strive to prove that hell is not eternal because the

PAGE 

Bible speaks occasionally of the destruction of the wicked. The argument has no validity. Such words as destruction are the only terms that can be used to describe the completeness of loss and defeat by one who has been condemned to eternal punishment (see quotation above from II Thess. i, 9).

The entire end of living has been missed. All supernatural life and the chance of ever getting it back are gone. But the condemnation, as the Bible shows, is to everlasting fire. "They shall go into everlasting punishment,' says Matt. xxv, 46. We must interpret the softer texts in the light of those that tell the difficult truth. There is no evading the Christian doctrine of eternal hell.

It is likely that there are millions of souls in limbo, a place that is really a part of hell but which lacks its punishment by fire. Unbaptized babies are there. We have frequently come across the popular opinion that there are many adults in limbo. But the modern theologian, Father Tanqueray, held that this is not so, except those who never attained the use of reason; because when an unbaptized man attains the use of reason he either turns to God and is justified of original sin, or he neglects to turn to God and commits an actual mortal sin. Therefore, only unbaptized infants and unbaptized adults who never emerged from intellectual infancy are in limbo. 

What Is of Faith About Purgatory and Hell 

In regard to purgatory, it is of faith: 1. That there exists a purgatory where are detained the souls of such as have departed this life without mortal sin or who have still to pay a certain debt of temporal punishment; 

2. That the souls in purgatory are punished by the pain of loss as well as by the pain of sense; in other words, that they are deprived for a time of the beatific vision of God and they suffer other grave pains; 3. That the pains they endure vary in length and intensity in proportion to the debt of temporal punishment due; 4. That their sufferings can be mitigated or shortened by the suffrages offered for them, particularly the Holy Sacrifice of the Mass.

It is not of faith that the souls in purgatory are tortured by a real or corporeal and not merely by a metaphorical fire. Catechisms of the Eastern 

Churches say nothing about the fire of purgatory; the Catechism of Pope 

Pius X, that of Cardinal Gasparri, etc., also omit mention of it, because the Greeks at the Council of Florence contended that the souls in purgatory do not suffer pain of sense from a real or corporeal fire but rather from the gloom in that realm of woe.

Free theological questions are such as whether the fire of purgatory is the same as that of hell, although less excruciating; how it can affect souls separated from the body; where purgatory is located; whether it is a place or a state; whether the guilt of venial sin is remitted by the fire or by an act of love of God.

The following facts about hell must be accepted as of Divine faith: 

1. That there is a hell where are punished the demons and those men who die with even only one mortal sin; 2. That there is a two-fold punishment in hell the pain of loss and the pain of sense, especially that of fire; 

3. That the pains of hell are eternal, without end or mitigation; 4. That these pains differ in accordance with the number and gravity of the sins. 

The Church has not defined the nature of the fire, but it would be intolerably rash not to deem it real or to accept it as a figure of speech.

PAGE 

Penitents who allow only a metaphorical and not real fire in hell cannot be absolved in Confession if they are obstinate in these views (Sacred Penitentiary, April 30, 1890).

Free theological questions are how it is possible for real fire to affect pure spirits and separated souls; what the nature of this fire is; where hell is located, above the earth or beneath it; whether it is a place or a state, or both, etc. Heaven

Heaven is a state and place of everlasting life, where we see God face to face, know Him as He is, are made like Him in glory and enjoy the greatest happiness. There is not the slightest admixture of pain in heaven.

As St. Paul taught, it is impossible for us even to imagine on this earth what heaven is like, because it is so incomparably above anything we have ever experienced. "Eye hath not seen, nor ear heard, neither hath it entered into the heart of man, what things God hath prepared for them that love him (Isa. lxiv, 4; I Cor. ii, 9).

Heaven consists, primarily, in seeing God face to face the beatific vision. The creature spiritually possesses God by this vision and loves Him to the utmost of its power. But there will be other joys-the sight of 

Christ's glorified humanity, the companionship of Our Lord, His Mother, the angels, and the saints, and an understanding of the marvels of creation. 

God will be seen, not through others as we see Him reflected in creatures on this earth, but without an intermediary. There will not even be any intervening medium of idea or thought. Though beheld directly, yet 

He will not be seen with the same intensity by all, for He is infinite and He will be understood and enjoyed by the saved in accordance with the degree of their reward. The blessed will recognize one another and love one another in God. They will be able to learn all they wish to know by looking at God. The will of man in heaven will be so fixed on God that it cannot turn away and hence the happiness will never end. In addition to the beatific vision, there will be complete beauty in all the surroundings and all the inhabitants of heaven.

The question is often asked: How can a mother be happy in heaven if her son is in hell? The answer is: The mother will have complete understanding in heaven, something that she does not have here.

Gold, precious stones, etc., are mentioned in the Apocalypse as existing in heaven; they are highly symbolic of the joys that "eye hath not seen."

Man in heaven will, in a sense, be deified. Though he will retain his individuality, he will be so closely united with God as to partake of the Divine nature physically and formally, although only analogically. "We are called the children of God and we really are such" (I John iii, 2).
